# Changelog

All notable changes to this project are documented here.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [1.0.0-SNAPSHOT] — Unreleased

### Added

- **Playwright leaderboard scenarios over the end-of-game summary screen, and a rewrite of four of the five acceptance criteria that described behaviour the application does not have (EOP-219, [ADR-068](docs/adr/ADR-068-playwright-e2e-testing.md)).** `e2e/tests/leaderboard.spec.ts` adds four scenarios across chromium, firefox and webkit, taking the suite from 33 to **45 passing** with zero failures, flakes or skips. It ships no production code: the leaderboard is already delivered behind `eop.features.game-over`, which needs no override in `compose.e2e.yml` because all three back-end flags ship `true`. **Four of the five filed criteria were rewritten before any test was written, the same failure mode EOP-218 met and for the same reason — they were authored from the API surface rather than from the code.** *No historical leaderboard exists*: the ticket asked for a scenario showing the current game's players **and** rows seeded from a prior game, but `GetLeaderboardUseCase.java:76` refuses any session that is not `COMPLETED` and the projection is scoped to the one session in the path, one result row being kept per session so the view always reflects that session's latest completed game — so there was nothing to seed, and the criterion became an **isolation** scenario asserting a leaderboard shows only its own players while other completed games share the database. That is non-vacuous precisely because the three browser projects run serially against one stack, so Chromium's finished game is already persisted by the time Firefox and WebKit complete theirs. *A new game returns to `IN_PROGRESS`, never to `LOBBY`*: `NewGameUseCase.java:130` clears tricks and hands, resets straight to `IN_PROGRESS` and re-deals to the same seats, and `SessionStatus.LOBBY` is not re-entered by any code path — the lobby the facilitator briefly sees at `ui/src/App.tsx:192` is a transition that `LobbyScreen` forwards through on observing `IN_PROGRESS`, the same path a mid-game reload already takes. *A tie cannot be arranged*, because a score is derived and never stored — `ScoreSheet` recomputes every total from the whole trick history on each read (ADR-030), so no SQL can seed one and no API forces one — so the scenario asserts the ranking **rules** against whatever the game produced, which holds for every possible outcome and is strictly stronger than one arranged case: totals ordered descending, `position` as competition ranking, `tied` true exactly when another player shares that total asserted in both directions against `ScoreSheet.java:177`, and every member of a shared first place agreeing on the flag so no player is ever rendered sole winner of a shared lead. And *the flag-OFF criterion was dropped, not rewritten*, on EOP-218's precedent: `VITE_GAME_SCREEN_ENABLED` is a build-time Vite variable (ADR-037) baked in at `ui/Dockerfile:40`, so covering it needs a second image and a second stack per run, while `ui/src/App.test.tsx:190` already asserts it in milliseconds and `GameOverControllerDisabledIntegrationTest` covers the back-end half. **The suite found a defect, filed as `EOP-233`**: when the facilitator starts a second game the facilitator advances and every other player stays mounted on the previous game's leaderboard indefinitely, because `LobbyScreen` and `GameScreen` both open a session subscription while `GameOverScreen` opens none — it fetches once on mount — so although `NewGameUseCase` publishes `HAND_DEALT` before the 204 returns, no subscriber exists on that page to act on it, and the facilitator moves only because its own callback navigates it locally. That ordering is also why the scenario is deterministic rather than flaky: the event precedes the response the facilitator's navigation depends on, so any subscriber would already have been notified by the time the facilitator's new hand renders. A reload recovers the stranded player via `sessionStorage` and the transitional lobby, which is why it is medium rather than a lockout; the scenario pins **current** behaviour, names the ticket in a comment, asserts the reload escape, and carries failure messages telling a future fixer to invert the two assertions that encode the defect. It is self-checking in a way a test asserting an absence usually is not — the same hand locator is asserted absent while stranded and present after the reload within one test, so a broken locator cannot yield a false pass, and an earlier run proved it fails loudly with a 30-second timeout when the behaviour differs. **Two design decisions worth carrying forward.** Rendered cells are compared against the payload the server actually sent, captured by a `page.on('response')` listener installed before the game completes (`e2e/tests/leaderboard.spec.ts:170`), which also reaches `sessionStatus` — a field the screen never renders; re-issuing the request from the test was rejected because it needs the player-token header name and would prove only that two responses agree, not that the table matches the bytes that produced it. And the four scenarios share **one deck per engine** in a `test.describe.serial` block whose `beforeAll` plays all 68 plays and reaches game-over on every seat, with the destructive new-game scenario declared last — safe because `playwright.config.ts` already runs `workers: 1` with `fullyParallel: false`, and visible in the leaderboard tests reporting single-digit millisecond durations while their shared setup carries the whole cost. Four scenarios each playing their own game would have tripled the runtime for no additional coverage. **The standing bound is unchanged and still applies:** both rate limiters run at `Integer.MAX_VALUE` in this stack because Caddy forwards one client address for every browser, so the suite is never evidence that throttling works — `SessionControllerIntegrationTest.ThrottlingCreation` and `ReadRateLimitIntegrationTest` own that — and certificate verification is off, so it is not evidence TLS is correctly configured either

- **Playwright boundary and error-path scenarios, and a rewrite of the four acceptance criteria that described behaviour the application does not have (EOP-218, [ADR-068](docs/adr/ADR-068-playwright-e2e-testing.md)).** `e2e/tests/boundary.spec.ts` adds six scenarios over the shipped TLS stack — a seventh player refused at a full table, a duplicate display name admitted, an unknown and a malformed join code answered identically, a mid-game tab close locking the player out, a lobby tab close costing a second seat, and the start control enabling only at the minimum — running green on Chromium, Firefox and WebKit (33 tests including the pre-existing happy-path and smoke suites, 2.0 minutes, zero failures and zero flakes). The substance of the story was refinement rather than authoring: four of the six criteria as filed asserted things the domain refuses. The ticket claimed a fourth player is rejected when `GameSession.MAXIMUM_PLAYERS` is six and the refusal falls on the seventh; it claimed duplicate display names are rejected when no check exists anywhere in `usecase/`, so a second "Alice" is seated beside the first; it claimed a player who closes their browser mid-game returns to their seat when `GameSession.join` requires `status.acceptsNewPlayers()` and an in-progress session does not, so that player is locked out permanently; and it exercised a one-player start that the front end makes unreachable, because `LobbyScreen.tsx:37` disables the control below three and the server's `TooFewPlayersException` therefore never reaches a browser. Each was rewritten to assert what the code does, which is why the suite passed on its first execution against unmodified production code — had the original wording survived, four tests would have failed and the natural reading would have been that the application was broken. Two scenarios are pinned as *observed* behaviour rather than endorsed, with `EOP-230` (duplicate names) and `EOP-231` (seat recovery) owning the product decision and instructed to update the scenario in the same change if either behaviour moves; `EOP-232` owns the minimum-player literal duplicated across `GameSession.java:38`, `LobbyScreen.tsx:37` and `e2e/game.ts:31`. The join-code scenario is deliberately stronger than the ticket asked: `JoinCode.parse` returns an empty optional rather than throwing precisely so that "that is not a code" and "no session has that code" are indistinguishable, and `JoinSessionUseCase.execute` honours it by throwing the same no-argument `UnknownJoinCodeException` on both paths, so the test asserts the two rendered messages are *equal* and pins the anti-enumeration property over a forty-bit keyspace instead of merely checking one 404. The flag-OFF scenario the ticket listed was dropped rather than rewritten, because `VITE_GAME_SCREEN_ENABLED` is substituted at build time (ADR-037) so covering it at this tier would mean a second UI image and a second stack per run to re-assert what `ui/src/App.test.tsx:190` already asserts in milliseconds. `e2e/game.ts` gains `MAXIMUM_PLAYERS`, a `lobbyPlayerNames` reader — a player count cannot tell two Alices from an Alice and a Bob — and `expectJoinRefused`, added as a sibling of `joinSession` rather than as a flag on it, so the happy-path suite keeps a helper that strictly insists on reaching the lobby; it returns the message rather than asserting it, which is what lets the anti-oracle scenario compare two refusals. The standing bound on this suite is unchanged and still applies: both rate limiters run at `Integer.MAX_VALUE` because Caddy presents one client address for every browser, so nothing here is evidence that throttling works (`SessionControllerIntegrationTest` and `ReadRateLimitIntegrationTest` own that), and certificate verification is off, so nothing here is evidence that TLS is correctly configured.

- **A fourth test tier: end-to-end UI tests driving real browsers against the shipped containers (EOP-216, [ADR-068](docs/adr/ADR-068-playwright-e2e-testing.md)).** The suite had unit tests, API integration tests and a k6 load canary, and none of them could tell whether the application actually worked in a browser. The gap was specific rather than theoretical: `ui/` Vitest tests stub `fetch`, so they cannot prove a real payload arrives or that the bundle even loads; a Spring `@SpringBootTest` evaluates no JavaScript, so a Content-Security-Policy that blocked the bundle outright would pass every assertion in the Java suite; and the CI smoke test proves the stack answers `/health`, which is a liveness probe and not a user. A new root-level `e2e/` package runs `@playwright/test` against a new `compose.e2e.yml`, and one smoke test asserting the home-screen heading renders passes on **Chromium, Firefox and WebKit** — `3 passed (43.2s)`, with `globalSetup` bringing the stack up and `globalTeardown` removing its containers, both volumes and its network. **The tier's value comes entirely from running the production images unmodified**, so the shipped Caddy configuration is exercised rather than approximated: its security response headers ([ADR-035](docs/adr/ADR-035-tls-and-security-response-headers.md)), its request-body cap on `/api/*` ([ADR-033](docs/adr/ADR-033-session-creation-rate-limit-and-body-size-cap.md)), its refusal of source maps (EOP-107) and its single-page-application fallback are all live under test. **The ticket's plain-HTTP premise was false and was reversed during delivery.** `EOP-216` specified a plain-HTTP stack and an acceptance criterion that no `--ignore-https-errors` flag would be needed, but `ui/Caddyfile` is copied *into* the image, declares exactly one site block with `tls internal`, and sets `auto_https disable_redirects` — so **nothing in the artefact listens on plain HTTP at all**, and the three routes to obtaining one each cost more than they saved: editing the Caddyfile changes production, bind-mounting a second one means the E2E tier tests a web server that never ships while guaranteeing drift between two files, and a separate UI image means the same thing with extra steps. Keeping TLS and setting `ignoreHTTPSErrors: true` is also not a new concession — it is the same trade the existing smoke test (`curl -fsSk`), the k6 canary (`--insecure-skip-tls-verify`) and the UI image's own healthcheck (`wget --no-check-certificate`) already make against the same self-signed CA. Plain HTTP would additionally have been *hazardous* rather than merely inconvenient, because HSTS on `localhost` is port-agnostic for the two-year `max-age` the Caddyfile sets, across every `localhost` port: Playwright's throwaway profiles dodge that, a developer's own browser does not. **Two assumptions were settled empirically rather than argued.** Caddy matches its site block on hostname alone and ignores the port, so publishing on host `8443` against a Caddyfile that names only `:8080` needs no configuration change — already load-bearing in CI, where the smoke test curls port 443 against the same file — and the feared silent failure mode, a request matching no site block and receiving an empty `200`, is exactly why the smoke test asserts a rendered heading instead of a status code. And addressing the target as `localhost` is mandatory, not stylistic: Caddy aborts the handshake server-side on a non-matching SNI, a constraint inherited from EOP-160 where `default_sni` did not rescue it and no client-side flag could. **`compose.e2e.yml` is a standalone file rather than an override**, because an override inherits the project name, network and volumes and would therefore destroy a developer's running `compose.app.yml` stack; it takes its own project name, subnet, container names and volumes, publishes only `127.0.0.1:8443`, and diverges from the production compose file in two deliberate ways — Postgres credentials are *defaulted* rather than fail-if-unset, since the database is a throwaway that `down -v` destroys and requiring a `.env` would block a fresh clone, and Caddy waits on the app's **healthcheck** rather than merely its start, because `up --wait` is the signal `globalSetup` relies on and start-ordering alone would let it return while `/api` still answered 502. That host-side health poll is not redundant with `--wait`: compose's flag only proves each container's *internal* probe, which says nothing about port publication or the TLS handshake the browsers depend on, and the poll compares the body to exactly `OK` because a Caddy matching no route answers `200` with nothing in it. **Both rate limiters are raised to `Integer.MAX_VALUE` in this stack, and the consequence is stated rather than buried:** Caddy forwards `X-Forwarded-For` and the app is told to trust it, so every browser Playwright launches resolves to the one host address and the entire suite would share a single bucket of five session creations and three hundred reads per minute — against a two-player game that peaks near a hundred reads a minute on its own. The limiters still run and still count, so their code path is not bypassed, only the ceiling moved; **this tier therefore proves nothing about throttling and must never be cited as evidence that it works**, which remains the Java integration tests' job. Three further bounds are recorded as decisions rather than left to be read as oversights: the suite is **not** part of `./mvnw verify`, because it needs a container runtime and roughly 500 MB of browser binaries; it runs **serially** on one worker, since one app instance, one database, a leaderboard that reads whole game history ([ADR-030](docs/adr/ADR-030-scoring-is-derived-not-accumulated.md)) and a single shared rate-limit key all argue against parallelism until someone measures the alternative; and `e2e/` has a strict `tsconfig.json` with a `typecheck` script but **no ESLint**, unlike `ui/`. The UI image is built and tagged `eop-ui:e2e` rather than `:local` because `ui/Dockerfile` defaults all three `VITE_*` flags to `false` at build time with no runtime override, so the game screen only renders when the image is built with `--build-arg VITE_GAME_SCREEN_ENABLED=true` — a distinct tag makes that visible in `docker images` instead of leaving two functionally different images sharing one name. Scenario coverage, CI wiring and the published stakeholder report are deliberately not in this story; it is the walking skeleton of the tier, and it proves the wiring before any scenario depends on it.

- **Scratch files outside the worktree are now refused by the shell instead of discouraged by a document, and the document's stated reason turned out to be false (EOP-000, Blueprint §7.8 and §7.9).** `AGENTS.md` and Blueprint §7.9 have required scratch work to go in `.tmp/` at the repository root for weeks, and on 2026-09-05 an agent that had read both wrote **nine** scratch files to `/tmp` in a single day — `eop156-canary.sh`, `assertion_probe.java`, `hash_safety.java`, `hc.json` and five more. The cause was not forgetfulness but the justification: both documents claimed that `external_directory` is left unconfigured, falls back to `ask`, and therefore makes "every touch of `/tmp` block on a prompt the human may not notice for minutes". **`external_directory` does not gate `bash` at all** — it gates the file tools — so a heredoc or a `>` redirect into `/tmp` from a shell command never consulted it and was silently permitted by `permission.bash`'s `"*": "allow"` wildcard. Every one of those nine writes is in the log as `action.pattern=* action.action=allow`, with no `external_directory` decision recorded anywhere. The rule was prose with nothing behind it, which is the reverse of how it read. **Enforcement is six `deny` entries at the end of the `permission.bash` map** — `*/tmp/*`, `*/tmp`, `*/tmp *`, `*/var/folders/*`, `*$TMPDIR*`, `*mktemp*` — `deny` rather than `ask` because the convention exists precisely to stop spending human attention, so an `ask` would reinstate the cost it was written to avoid. Three of the six are not redundant padding: `*/tmp` and `*/tmp *` exist because a trailing-`*` pattern cannot match a command that *ends* at `/tmp` or passes it as a bare argument, which is the exact shape of the day's real violation `javac /tmp/assertion_probe.java -d /tmp`, and `*mktemp*` rather than `mktemp *` catches command substitution such as `d=$(mktemp -d)`. `*/private/tmp/*` is deliberately absent as dead config, since `/private/tmp/x` and `/var/tmp/x` both already contain the literal `/tmp/`. **Verified firing in the log rather than asserted from the config**, which §7.8 now requires of any such claim because this is the third statement in that section falsified after being read off `.opencode/opencode.json`: a fresh `opencode run` produced `pattern="ls -la /tmp/a.txt" action.permission=bash action.pattern=*/tmp/* action.action=deny`, and `--auto` was passed deliberately and did **not** override it, bounding §7.8's second escape route to `ask` requests. The denied sub-agent then reasoned its own way to "temporary files should be created in `.tmp/` at the repository root instead" — the deny is **self-teaching at the moment of the violation**, which a paragraph in `AGENTS.md` demonstrably is not. A positive control in a second fresh session confirms `.tmp/` is untouched (`action.pattern=*` allow) and that the eight pre-existing `rm … .tmp` allows still resolve to their own patterns rather than being shadowed. `OpencodeTmpDenyRulesTest` joins the documentation-integrity gates in `src/test/java/org/maglez/eop/docs/` as the seventeenth of them, and it does more than assert six keys are present: it **mirrors OpenCode's matcher** — each glob compiled to an anchored regex, last matching rule wins, default `allow` — so a later rule appended *below* the denies that re-opened those paths fails the build too, which a presence check would not notice. Five tests: the six keys read exactly `deny`; eight outside-scratch commands including the three real violations resolve to `deny`; six `.tmp/` commands including `git worktree add .tmp/scratch HEAD` and `rm -rf .tmp` resolve to `allow`, that being the half it would hurt most to get wrong; a `MINIMUM_BASH_RULES` floor of 25 against the actual 30 stops it passing over an empty map; and the mirror is validated against the two observed log lines by asserting the winning pattern it computes. **Four limits, stated plainly.** A `deny` on a command *pattern* is a speed bump, not a boundary — it matches command text, so it cannot see inside a script it invokes and `bash -c '…'` is a single node; write to `.tmp/` because it is correct, not because you would otherwise be stopped. The map is read at **process start**, so editing it does not disarm the session that edits it: this change was made from a session that kept its `/tmp` allow throughout, and the residue cleanup was performed under that pre-enforcement grant. The test proves the rules are *declared*, never that one fired, the same split as `AgentPermissionDeclarationTest` and `ConditionalOnPropertyHavingValueTest`. And container-internal `/tmp` paths such as `tools/sonar/scan-ui.sh`'s `SONAR_USER_HOME=/tmp/.sonar` are unaffected only because invoking a script exposes just the script path to the matcher — a hand-typed `docker run …/tmp/…` would trip, and the remedy is a narrower allow placed after the deny. The change also **overrules this agent's own bash tool briefing**, which recommends `/var/folders/vv/…/T/opencode` as "pre-approved for external directory access": that path is now denied outright, and the same briefing's claim was already falsified once on 2026-08-20.

- **Container logs are aggregated, labelled and queryable for the first time: Promtail tails the Docker socket into Loki, Grafana reads it, and an `EOP Infrastructure` dashboard renders error rate, error breakdown by logger, log volume and level distribution (EOP-209, ADR-067).** EOP-117 gave the application structured JSON logging and an MDC correlation id; until now nothing collected it, so a log line existed only inside `docker logs` for as long as the container lived and answering "what errored, in which logger, on which request" meant grepping one container at a time. The pipeline ships as **`compose.observability.yml`, a separate opt-in stack rather than an addition to `docker-compose.yml`** — that file is the k6/InfluxDB/Grafana performance harness `.opencode/rules/performance-testing.md` treats as the local baseline, and folding a log pipeline into it would make the two start and stop together for no reason. Both services join the pre-existing `eop-monitoring_default` network as `external: true`, which is what lets the already-provisioned Grafana reach Loki by service name; `tools/monitoring/grafana/datasources/datasource.yml` **extends** that provisioning rather than replacing it, appending a Loki datasource beside the InfluxDB one that remains the default. Loki is a single-binary filesystem deployment with `retention_period: 720h`, and that number is a correction rather than a transcription — the ticket asked for 744h while calling it 30 days, which is 31. **Promtail parses two log formats through mutually exclusive `match` branches, and the discriminator is whether the line begins with JSON's opening brace — `|~ "^\\{"` against `!~ "^\\{"`.** `logback-spring.xml` selects a JSON encoder under the `prod` profile and a plain pattern otherwise, and the container runs with `SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prod`, so the JSON branch is the live path and a regex branch for Spring Boot's default plain pattern is the non-prod fallback. The first attempt discriminated on a line filter for the word `timestamp`, and that was **unsound in the false-positive direction rather than merely inelegant**: `timestamp` is a SQL column type, so Liquibase and Hibernate startup messages contain it in plain text — precisely the lines the second branch exists to parse — and each would have been routed to the JSON branch to fail its `json` stage and arrive unlabelled. Anchoring on the brace tests a property of the *format* instead of a word that may appear in any *message*, which is why it cannot fail that way. Two details are recorded in the file itself because both are easy to re-break: the escaping is `"^\\{"` because the backslash is escaped for LogQL rather than for YAML, and the backtick raw-string form LogQL documentation tends to show is rejected outright by Promtail's match-stage parser, which refuses to start with `invalid selector syntax for match stage`. **Three findings came out of reading real emitted lines rather than reading the source comments, and two of them contradict those comments.** logback 1.5.x `JsonEncoder` emits `loggerName`, not `logger`, and nests MDC under an `mdc` object, so the field map is `logger: loggerName` and the JMESPath `correlationId: mdc.correlationId` — `logback-spring.xml`'s own comment listing `logger` and a flat MDC is simply wrong. Its `timestamp` is **epoch milliseconds as a number**, not the ISO-8601 string the same comment implies. And `message` carries the **unformatted** SLF4J pattern with `{}` intact while the substituted values sit in a separate `arguments` array — a real line reads `"message":"Rejected caller input: {}"` with `"arguments": ["page must not be negative, was -1"]` — so a Loki line filter must never be written expecting interpolated text. **`correlationId` is delivered as structured metadata rather than as the index label the ticket contracted, and that is the sharpest decision in the change.** `level` and `logger` are bounded sets and are safe as index labels; a per-request UUID is not, and as a label it would create one Loki stream per request — a documented Loki anti-pattern. Structured metadata is the purpose-built home for a request or trace id, available because Loki 3.3.2 runs the tsdb `v13` schema where `allow_structured_metadata` defaults true. **Verified by query rather than by configuration review, which matters because the two are indistinguishable from the config file alone:** `{container="eop-app", level="WARN"} | correlationId="..."` returns the line **with no `| json` parser anywhere in the pipeline**, which is only possible if the field is genuinely structured metadata. The WARN was provoked rather than waited for, by `GET /api/v1/cards?page=-1` reaching the `InvalidInputException` handler. **The dashboard has four panels, not the six originally contracted, and the two that are missing are missing because they cannot be built — not because they were descoped.** HTTP latency p50/p95 and HTTP status distribution have no data to read: `CorrelationIdFilter` populates MDC but logs nothing, no controller logs a status code or an elapsed time, and `logback-spring.xml` declares no such field. The sharpest evidence is a request that fails validation — `POST /api/v1/sessions` with an empty `displayName` returns 400 **and** an `x-correlation-id` response header, proving the filter ran, yet produces **zero** log lines at any level. That is a standing violation of `.opencode/rules/observability.md`, which mandates a request/response summary at INFO, and it is filed as EOP-212 under Epic EOP-208 rather than left as review prose. **Limits worth stating before anyone cites this as coverage.** It is a **local developer stack**: nothing in `.github/workflows/` is touched, so no CI behaviour may be claimed of it, and no alerting or notification exists — the dashboard is read by a human who chooses to look. Loki runs `auth_enabled: false` and publishes only on loopback, which is a deployment fact and not a security control. The `external: true` network name depends on Compose's implicit `<project>_default` convention, so `docker compose -f docker-compose.yml up -d` must already be running or the stack will not start — a real coupling, recorded in ADR-067 rather than discovered later. And the two images take [ADR-064](docs/adr/ADR-064-pinned-container-audit-coverage.md)'s digest-pinned roster from three to five, neither carrying a provenance attestation, consistent with the residual already accepted in `SETUP.md`

- **A k6 `check()` may no longer assert against a response timing, so the ungated-latency-check shape EOP-157 deleted cannot be reintroduced (EOP-206, ADR-006).** `K6LatencyCheckTest` joins the documentation-integrity gates in `src/test/java/org/maglez/eop/docs/` as the sixteenth of them, and the third to gate `test/k6/` rather than prose. EOP-157 removed a predicate named `"response time < 100ms"` from `test/k6/health-check.js` because it read as a latency gate while gating nothing: a k6 `check()` never affects the process exit code, and no threshold over `checks` or `checks_failed` exists in either `options.js` or `options-ci.js`, so a response between 100 ms and the real ceiling failed the check silently and the run still passed. What EOP-157 left behind was a *prose* prohibition — a comment block in the scenario, a commit body, and an entry under `### Removed` in this file — and prose does not fail a build, so the next author to reach for a latency assertion inside a `check()` would have met no resistance. Raised by `@tester-unit-and-quality` as a MINOR, explicitly non-blocking finding during that gate round, and filed rather than left as review prose. **The read is bound to a `check()` call rather than banned outright, and that is the whole design:** reading `res.timings.duration` to log it, or to feed a custom `Trend`, is legitimate and is what the ticket asks for as the alternative, so a bare ban on `timings` would forbid the very thing it recommends. The matcher therefore extracts each `check(` argument list by paren-depth scanning and searches only inside it, over a sanitised copy of the file in which comment bodies and string-literal *contents* are blanked while length and newlines are preserved — necessary in both directions, since a comment documenting the deleted predicate must not fire the gate, and a predicate *name* containing the word must not either, while a `)` inside a name must not truncate the body. `card-catalogue.js` is the reason the extractor is not line-scoped: its `"totalPages matches the element count at size=1"` predicate wraps across two lines. The discriminator is the whole `timings` object rather than one of its seven fields, and it is matched as a bare word so that `const { duration } = r.timings` is caught as well as `r.timings.duration` and `r.timings["duration"]` — safe only because literal contents are already blanked. **Two floors, and the second matters more.** A file-count floor of two stops the test passing by matching no scenarios at all, in the style of `K6ThresholdCouplingTest.MINIMUM_THRESHOLDS`; but a per-file floor of one extracted `check()` body is the one that would actually fire first, because a broken extractor returning nothing would otherwise pass on every file in the tree while inspecting none of it. **Proved by observation rather than by reasoning**, which the ticket asked for explicitly: the deleted predicate was reinserted verbatim into `health-check.js` in a `git worktree` scratch checkout, and `./mvnw test -Dtest=K6LatencyCheckTest` there failed one of six tests and the build with it, naming the file, the reason a check cannot gate, and where latency does belong. Six synthetic-text cases pin the matcher besides, three of them negative. **Bounded on purpose, and the bounds are in the class javadoc:** predicate *quality* stays reviewer-enforced, exactly as `K6ScenarioWiringTest` already records for wiring — a scenario whose checks are all trivially true still passes; a latency assertion written without touching `timings`, `Date.now()` arithmetic being the obvious one, is not recognised at all; and the sanitiser is a scanner rather than a JavaScript parser, so a regex literal containing an odd quote, or `${...}` interpolation inside a template literal, is mis-read. Gating check *counts* or check *names* was considered by the ticket and rejected before delivery began: a count gate is the same tautology problem one step weaker, and a name is prose. Neither `K6ThresholdCouplingTest` nor `K6ScenarioWiringTest` was touched

- **Every k6 scenario is now provably wired into the CI performance canary, so one can no longer be merged while never executing (EOP-204, ADR-006).** `K6ScenarioWiringTest` joins the documentation-integrity gates in `src/test/java/org/maglez/eop/docs/` as the fifteenth of them, and the first to gate the coverage of a CI step rather than the truthfulness of prose: it holds every scenario file in `test/k6/` against an actual `run_canary` invocation in `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, and every invocation against a file that exists. Before it, a new `test/k6/whatever.js` could be added, reviewed and merged with no invocation at all — every gate green, the file reading as coverage, the scenario never running, and any regression it would have caught still shipping. `K6ThresholdCouplingTest` (EOP-158) does not reach that, since it holds the threshold count in step with `options-ci.js` and says nothing about which scenarios execute. **It matches an invocation rather than a filename, and that is the whole design:** the story specified asserting each filename "appears in" the workflow, which would have passed vacuously on the tree it was written against, because the workflow discusses both scenarios by name in a prose comment explaining why they are kept apart — so the pattern is anchored to a `run_canary` call at the start of a line, which a comment cannot satisfy, reapplying `K6ThresholdCouplingTest`'s own reasoning that a bare word match would count the rationale as configuration. A third hardcoded-count coupling is closed with it, the literal scenario total in the canary's failure message being compared against the number of invocations, kept as its own test method so a reviewer can drop it independently. **Bounded on purpose:** it proves each scenario is invoked and each invocation resolves, never that the invoking job runs or is required, and never that a scenario asserts anything worth asserting — a wired scenario whose checks were all trivially true would pass. It reads the workflow as text and parses no YAML, consistent with its siblings. Two findings came out of delivery rather than design: the gate was demonstrated red by adding a throwaway scenario before being trusted, and its own synthetic negative case caught a backtracking flaw where a greedy filename class let an invocation of an adjacent file report the real scenario as wired.

- **The CI performance canary now loads the database as well as the health endpoint, so it can catch the class of regression ADR-055 always claimed it caught (EOP-156, ADR-055 §3 and amendment 3 §6 amended).** EOP-155 shipped a canary that ran `GET /health` and nothing else. That endpoint returns a static string and touches no `DataSource`, no repository and no business code, so the ADR's own rationale for the relaxed `p(95) < 500` threshold — "still catching gross regressions (N+1 queries, missing indexes, 50× slowdown)" — was true of the threshold and false of the scenario it was applied to: no N+1 query and no missing index could ever have appeared in it. Raised by `@tester-api` during the EOP-155 gate round as a non-blocking follow-up. A new `test/k6/card-catalogue.js` loads `GET /api/v1/cards?size=1` under `SMOKE_STAGES`, selecting `THRESHOLDS_CI` by the same `__ENV.K6_ENV === "ci"` test as `health-check.js`, and that request traverses Caddy, Tomcat, the controller, the catalogue query, the JPA mapping, the Liquibase-managed schema and Postgres before serialising a `Page<T>`. It was picked because the smoke test immediately before it in the same job already asserts on its `totalElements`, so it was known reachable in CI before it was load-tested. `size=1` is deliberate — the point is to traverse the persistence path, not to move bytes — and its four checks are correctness-only with no per-request latency assertion, because the thresholds own latency and a millisecond check would report shared-runner noise as a failed check without failing the build. **Two scenarios, two exports, and the merge was rejected on measurement rather than taste.** One script issuing both requests, or one k6 run with two scenarios, would put both endpoints behind a single `http_req_duration`, so a cards regression would be diluted by health's much faster responses — measured here at roughly a 3× gap on an idle machine — and the aggregate would move *less* than the regression, in the direction of hiding it. Widening what `summary.json` measures is worse than untidy: `perf-trend` reads that one file and every point in `ci-history.jsonl` so far measures `/health` alone, so changing its scope would inject a step change caused by measurement rather than by code — the same error ADR-055's amendment 3 and `.opencode/rules/performance-testing.md` both warn against for local-versus-CI figures, applied inside the CI series itself. So `health-check.js` keeps the unprefixed `raw.json` / `summary.json`, `card-catalogue.js` writes `cards-raw.json` / `cards-summary.json` beside it, both are uploaded because the `k6-results` artifact's `path:` is the whole results directory, and **the published trend remains `/health`-only and stays comparable across its whole history**. The two invocations share one `run_canary` shell function and one `EXPECTED_THRESHOLD_COUNT=3`, which is a constraint rather than a preference: `K6ThresholdCouplingTest` (EOP-158) fails the build if that constant is assigned twice, since a second assignment would shadow the first and silently disarm the count comparison — and because both scenarios import the same `THRESHOLDS_CI`, the constant is a count *per summary export* rather than a total, so a third scenario would not move it but editing `options-ci.js` would. Both scenarios always run even when the first breaches, for the same reason the render step is `if: always()`; note that `errexit` is suppressed inside a function invoked as the left operand of `||`, so every failure path inside `run_canary` is an explicit `return 1` rather than a bare command trusting `set -e`. The run summary now renders one table per scenario, headed by the request each measured. **The gate was negative-tested rather than inspected, with every fault applied only to the cards scenario so a healthy `/health` could not be credited with the failure.** A stub `docker` first on `PATH` wrote doctored summary exports in place of running k6, across five modes: healthy (exit 0, six `PASS` lines), a threshold flipped to `true`, a missing export, a threshold row deleted so the count read 2, and a non-zero k6 exit with every threshold passing. All four faults exited 1 with an error naming `card-catalogue.js`, and each also emitted the aggregate `1 of 2 k6 canary scenarios failed` — which is the specific risk of the `|| failures=$((failures + 1))` shape discharged by test: the new scenario is load-bearing, and a green `/health` does not mask a red cards run. The render step was separately run with `cards-summary.json` absent and exited 0, logging the missing export and still emitting the `/health` table in full, which is the case that matters since it is `if: always()` and therefore runs in exactly the runs where a scenario produced nothing. **Evidence:** both step bodies were extracted from the workflow verbatim and run against the live Compose stack with the real digest-pinned k6 container and `--network container:eop-caddy`, exiting 0 with all three thresholds passing for both scenarios; on a warm stack `/health` reported `p(95)=3.79ms` over 663 of 663 checks and `/api/v1/cards?size=1` reported `p(95)=11.43ms` over 884 of 884 checks, both across 221 iterations, and a cold first cards run reached `p(95)=26.58ms` with `max=244.82ms` — still two orders of magnitude inside the 500ms threshold, and the figure to remember when judging whether that threshold has enough headroom for a shared runner. **Limits stated plainly.** Per ADR-055's own recorded lesson, "a step that only ever runs in CI is only ever proven in CI", so none of the above substitutes for this change's own CI run. `SMOKE_STAGES` only — `LOAD_STAGES` and `STRESS_STAGES` remain local-only, and the canary is still not a measurement, so nothing here is appended to `docs/performance/TRENDS.md`. The trend series is not extended to the new scenario, which is a separate decision rather than a side effect of this one; until it is, a cards regression is visible in a build's run summary and its artifact but not in the published chart. `test/k6/config/options-ci.js`, `test/k6/config/options.js` and `test/k6/run.sh` are all untouched — the last needs no change to run the new scenario locally, since it already takes the script as `$1` and derives the result filename from `$2` or the basename.

- **The two ADRs the security audit leaned on now say what it cited them for, and the GitHub Actions supply-chain position is written down for the first time (EOP-174).** The EOP-173 audit dismissed two findings by citing an ADR, and in both cases the ADR did not contain the argument. An unrecorded dismissal is not a decision — it is a judgement that the next auditor must re-derive, and will re-derive differently. **[ADR-045](docs/adr/ADR-045-frontend-response-validation.md) was cited for the wrong thing.** The 256-bit identity token in `sessionStorage` was waved through on its authority, but ADR-045 governs the shape of *response bodies* parsed in `ui/src/api.ts` and states in its own text that it is not a security control even inside that scope, so it could not possibly authorise a security dismissal of credential storage — a question it never addresses. The owning decision is [ADR-015](docs/adr/ADR-015-player-identity.md), whose 2026-08-20 amendment re-examined `sessionStorage` against precisely this XSS exposure and upheld it, conceding that an `HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Strict` cookie would remove the exposure outright (EOP-107). The dismissal was right on the merits and wrong in its citation, and ADR-045 now says so and redirects the reader, changing neither its Decision nor its Consequences. **[ADR-034](docs/adr/ADR-034-sse-subscriber-cap-and-emitter-timeout.md) was cited for an argument nobody had written down.** The SSE endpoint's exclusion from read rate limiting was dismissed as compensated by the subscriber caps, an argument the auditor constructed during the audit and which is only *partly* sound — and recording only the sound half would have reproduced the original overstatement, so the amendment records all three parts together: the exclusion is deliberate, because a long-lived stream holds one slot for its life and a per-minute read budget would throttle correct behaviour; the caps of twelve per session and five hundred in total genuinely do bound *concurrent* subscribers; but a concurrency cap is **not** a rate cap, and a client cycling connect, disconnect and reconnect stays inside both caps at every instant while consuming an async dispatch per attempt. That gap is a low-severity resource exhaustion with no disclosure component, it is held in ADR-051 rather than half-solved at the interceptor, and the codebase already knew — `WebInterceptorConfiguration.java:32-36` says so in Javadoc, which is exactly where an auditor reading the ADR would never look. **The third surface had no stated position at all.** `SETUP.md`'s accepted-risks section already covered the seven OpenCode npm plugins and the pinned containers, and openly conceded the whole Maven plugin layer, but said nothing about `.github/workflows/`, where 22 `uses:` references across 9 distinct actions are pinned by mutable major tag and none by commit SHA. It now records the count and why references outnumber actions (`actions/checkout@v4` alone appears eight times), corrects the belief that the k6, Trivy and SonarQube steps are SHA-pinned counter-examples — they are digest-pinned *container images*, not actions, so no `uses:` line is SHA-pinned — states why the residual risk is low (first-party `actions/*` and `docker/*` publishers only, and a default token of `permissions: contents: read` with the single `contents: write` scoped to the `perf-trend` job), states exactly what is being accepted (a retag of `v4` would execute attacker code in CI with no signal), and names the one condition that invalidates the reasoning outright: a third-party action, which must be SHA-pinned from its first commit. EOP-202 is the filed remediation, and until it lands that section *is* the control — a documented acceptance rather than an oversight. Both ADR rows in `docs/adr/README.md` carry the new amendment date, with the latest date placed immediately after the word "amended" because `AdrIndexConsistencyTest` reads the first such capture in the row.

- **`@tech-lead` is told to own a story's Jira workflow position, and its Jira permissions are declared rather than inherited in silence (EOP-000).** The orchestrator could always transition an issue — `.opencode/opencode.json` allows `atlassian_jira_*` and denies only `atlassian_jira_move_*`, and `tech-lead.md` declared no Jira rules at all, so the allow reached it by inheritance. **Nothing in its prompt said so, and the silence was misread as a denial**: during EOP-173 the orchestrator reported that it "holds no transition permission on this project" and asked a human to move the story, a claim inferred from the ten review agents — which *do* deny `transition_issue` explicitly — and never checked against its own file. The verdict comment has been corrected on the issue. Two changes close the gap in the two places it existed. The frontmatter now states the grant explicitly, for exactly the reason the file already gives for stating `edit: allow` — "an undeclared key inherits the global allow and silence would read as an oversight" — and narrows it while doing so: `delete_issue`, `remove_*`, `move_*` and the three version tools are denied, mirroring the `bash` blocklist's principle that an irreversible or publishing act stays a human one, with `move_*` repeated at agent level because a per-agent ruleset **replaces** the top-level one rather than merging with it, and the broad glob placed first because opencode evaluates the last matching rule. A new `# Jira Workflow Protocol` section then supplies the half that actually changes behaviour: move to In Progress on starting and to Done only on a genuine Definition of Done, read the ids from `atlassian_jira_get_transitions` rather than trusting the instance-specific `11`/`21`/`31`, comment the evidence — gates, verdicts, real command output, commits — *before* transitioning, and record a finding as a typed linked issue confirmed against `atlassian_jira_get_link_types`, remembering that project `EOP` has no `Bug` type so a defect is a `Task` or a parented `Subtask`. The load-bearing rule is the prohibition: **never transition to Done while any gate has returned REJECT.** EOP-173 is cited in the file as the precedent for it, and precisely because that story's 2026-08-24 round got it right — two REJECTs, so it was deliberately left In Progress with a remediation trail — while its closure on 2026-09-05 got it wrong, with `EOP-174`, `EOP-176` and `EOP-177` from that trail still open. No effective permission was granted, so no ADR amendment was raised; [ADR-066](docs/adr/ADR-066-jira-restored-as-the-tracker.md) already records the Jira restoration this follows from, and the two Jira authoring hazards the section repeats — backtick every `ADR-NNN` and quoted key, because Jira autolinks any `KEY-NNN` token, and never use a markdown table, because one provably corrupts in storage — were both learned the same day at the cost of two corrective edits.

- **Jira is the tracker again, the two forked keyspaces are reconciled, and `@product-owner` may file issues for the first time (EOP-000, [ADR-066](docs/adr/ADR-066-jira-restored-as-the-tracker.md)).** EOP-166 exported 163 issues to GitHub Issues on 2026-08-26 because the Jira Free account for `maglez.atlassian.net` was expected to lapse, and [ADR-065](docs/adr/ADR-065-agent-role-boundaries-at-the-permission-layer.md)'s 2026-09-04 amendment recorded the consequence in the strongest terms — "There is no Jira" — denying `atlassian_jira_*` outright and reducing the Product Owner to drafting pasteable issue bodies. **The premise was false: the site never went away.** What had actually broken was authentication, and the mechanism is worth recording because it fails silently in the worst possible direction — a *granular* (scoped) Atlassian token is an OAuth bearer credential honoured only at `https://api.atlassian.com/ex/jira/{cloudId}/…`, while `mcp-atlassian` sends Basic auth to the site URL, where such a token is **ignored rather than rejected**. Every call therefore executed as an anonymous user, and `/rest/api/3/project/search` returned `200` with `total: 0`: an empty backlog, indistinguishable from a dead one. The same token against the gateway returned `total: 1`. Only a **classic, unscoped** API token works with this MCP server, and that is now stated in the ADR rather than rediscovered. **Restoring access exposed the real problem, which was not configuration but data.** Both trackers had been written to for ten days: Jira had reached `EOP-182` with seventeen issues and nothing above `EOP-165` closed, GitHub had reached `EOP-193` with most of its issues merged and closed, and `main` already carried `[EOP-167]`, `[EOP-168]` and `[EOP-182]` commits denoting the *GitHub* work while Jira tickets of the same keys described entirely different, undelivered stories. Nine Jira tickets described work that had shipped weeks earlier. Flipping the permission alone would have made the Product Owner allocate from 183 while commits and CI sat at 194, so the `[EOP-NNN]` prefix mandated by `.opencode/rules/git-commits.md` would have stopped resolving to one thing — strictly worse than an agent that cannot file at all. The reconciliation was therefore done first and in four parts: seven shipped tickets closed against named commits (`EOP-166`, `169`, `175`, `179`, `180`, `181`, plus `178` as superseded by `179`); the fifteen GitHub-native issues mirrored into `EOP-183`–`EOP-197` in ascending issue-number order, which is also the only mechanism that advances Jira's sequence past GitHub's high-water mark, thirteen closed and two left open mirroring `#329` and `#332`; the three keys that `main` had already spent re-filed as `EOP-167`→`EOP-198`, `EOP-168`→`EOP-199` and `EOP-182`→`EOP-200` (the last retaining parent Epic `EOP-180`) with `Duplicate` links and a comment on each original; and the other seven carried-forward tickets deliberately **left at their own keys**, because a `git log --grep` over `[EOP-166]`…`[EOP-182]` proved those seven appear nowhere in `main` and renumbering them would have destroyed ticket identity — including `EOP-173`'s In Progress state and `EOP-172`'s epic parentage — to fix an ambiguity that does not exist. Verified end to end: `key >= EOP-166` returns 35 issues, 23 Done, 1 In Progress, 11 To Do, next key `EOP-201`. `@product-owner`'s `atlassian_jira_*: deny` becomes an allow with the delivery-side writes denied individually — `transition_issue`, `assign_issue`, `add_worklog`, the sprint and version tools, `delete_issue`, `remove_*` — so it files and refines requirements but cannot move a ticket through the workflow, and `move_*` is repeated at agent level because a per-agent permission block replaces the top-level one rather than merging with it. **Three honest limits.** The key offset is permanent and unavoidable: Jira `EOP-184` mirrors GitHub's `EOP-183`, because GitHub spent `EOP-187`, `EOP-188` and `EOP-193` twice each and left one issue unkeyed, so fifteen issues over eleven keys cannot correspond one-to-one — a bare key in the 182–193 band is ambiguous and the mirrored description, which names its origin issue and delivering commit, is the resolving evidence. `main` is not rewritten, so those three renumbered keys mean the GitHub work in the commit log forever. And **nothing in the build enforces any of this**, deliberately: the tracker is a live external system, so no gate is proposed and the safeguard is that every mirrored and superseded issue states its own provenance in its own body. Four migration-era GitHub issues (`#290`, `#285`, `#281`, `#280`) remain open against closed Jira counterparts and were left alone, because GitHub is now history and editing it would imply it is still maintained.

- **A build gate for the `EXPECTED_THRESHOLD_COUNT` coupling between the workflow and the k6 CI options (EOP-158).** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` asserts that k6 reported exactly three thresholds, and that number is the whole reason the canary's threshold-flattening check fails loudly rather than passing silently when a threshold is added or removed — yet it was tied to `test/k6/config/options-ci.js` by a comment and nothing else. `K6ThresholdCouplingTest` now parses the constant out of the workflow with a line-anchored pattern (so a mention inside a comment or an `echo` cannot be mistaken for the assignment), counts the `{ threshold:` object-literal entries in the options module (so the prose in its header is not counted), and fails when the two disagree with a message naming both files, both numbers and the remedy. It also asserts the workflow still passes `-e K6_ENV=ci`, which is the non-obvious half: dropping that flag makes `health-check.js` fall back to `options.js`'s local SLO thresholds, which declare the **same number** of entries, so the count check would keep passing while the canary silently ran 200 ms/1000 ms production-like figures on a shared two-vCPU runner — a safe failure direction, but an invisible one. A `MINIMUM_THRESHOLDS` floor stops zero-versus-zero passing vacuously, a `shouldDeclareTheCountOnce` case catches a second assignment that would shadow the first in shell, and a synthetic self-test proves each matcher fires; the gate was also confirmed live by flipping the constant to `4` and watching it fail, then restoring with an empty diff. The honest bound is stated in the test's Javadoc: it proves the two numbers agree and the flag is present, never that the thresholds are the right thresholds, that `health-check.js` really consumes `THRESHOLDS_CI` (that is JavaScript control flow, not text), or that the step is wired into a job that runs.

- **A version literal in `.opencode/rules/` must now cite the ADR that owns it, and the build fails if it does not.** `RuleVersionLiteralTest` is the tenth documentation-integrity gate in `src/test/java/org/maglez/eop/docs/` and the first to read `.opencode/rules/` at all: every gate before it walks `docs/**`, `src/**`, `docs/api/openapi.yml` or `ui/src/api.ts`, so the least supervised text in the repository was also the most read, injected into every agent session on every dispatch. The asymmetry mattered because of what a rule file lacks rather than what it contains — it carries no `**Status:**`, no `**Date:**` and no supersession mechanism, so a version claim written into one can never be retired, which is why EOP-35 deleted a `liquibase-core` pin from `database.md` instead of correcting it. That fixed an instance and established no invariant, and the defect recurred in `build-quality.md` within weeks. The pass condition is therefore a same-line `ADR-NNN` citation rather than a ban: a citation restores the dated, supersedable owner the file itself cannot provide, so the rule is never name a version without saying which dated decision owns it — `build-quality.md:6` carries six Maven plugin versions and already satisfies it. **The recognised shapes are a closed enumeration that was measured rather than assumed**: three dot-separated numeric components, plus a `image: name:tag` form. Two-component pins are deliberately *not* caught, and that bound is the interesting finding — a broad `\d+\.\d+` sweep of the fifteen rule files returned `127.0.0.1`, two `95.1` coverage percentages and three k6 threshold rates (`0.001`, `0.1`, `0.1`) against exactly **one** genuine pin, so a two-component matcher would have carried five false positives for every true one and been switched off within a week, which is ADR-060's lesson about a control nobody can live with. The one real instance was fixed the way [PR #369](https://github.com/maglez/eop-threat-modeling/pull/369) fixed three parent-managed Maven literals — `performance-testing.md` now names `docker-compose.yml` as where InfluxDB's `image:` tag lives instead of quoting the tag. `v`-prefixed tags, four-or-more-component literals and image digests are each excluded with a stated reason, and the enumeration must be revisited when a new shape appears. **Two exceptions are declared, justified and self-retiring**, both in `versioning.md`: `Semantic Versioning 2.0.0` names a specification whose current and only release that is, so no dependency bump can falsify it, and `` `1.2.0-SNAPSHOT` → tag `v1.2.0` `` is a deliberately fictional worked example of the version-to-tag mapping (the real version is `1.0.0-SNAPSHOT`). An entry whose text no longer appears fails the build, so closing an exception means deleting it rather than letting it decay — `SeparationInvariantTest`'s pattern. The gate was not introduced with a blanket exemption. It carries an anti-vacuity assertion in the style of `DeckArithmeticClaimsTest`, a fifteen-file floor so it cannot pass by reading nothing, and a synthetic self-test proving both matchers fire and that the four excluded shapes do not; it was also confirmed live (`Tests run: 7, Failures: 1` on a probe line reading `pinned at 9.9.9 with no citation`). **What it does not do**: it proves a number has a dated owner, never that the number is right — the same split as `ConditionalOnPropertyHavingValueTest` against ADR-013's reviewer-enforced bean clause. It is deliberately not extended to `docs/**`, where a version literal is correct precisely because an ADR is dated and supersedable. Recorded as a dated 2026-09-04 amendment to [ADR-006](docs/adr/ADR-006-build-quality-gates.md) (EOP-125)
- **The CVE gate's own logic is now tested, and both supply-chain harnesses run in CI (EOP-147).** The Python block embedded in `tools/supply-chain/scan-dependencies.sh` decides whether a HIGH or CRITICAL Trivy finding blocks a merge, and it enforces six distinct failure modes over `tools/supply-chain/accepted-cves.json`: an unlisted finding, an entry missing any of the eleven required fields, a `module` mismatch, a severity that has been re-scored, an `expires` value that is not a `YYYY-MM-DD` date, and an expiry that has passed — plus the anti-rot direction, an entry the scan no longer reports, which fails and tells you to **delete** it rather than leave it. Every one of those was demonstrated by hand once during EOP-89 and then never re-run, and a control that has fired correctly once is not a control that has been tested. New `tools/supply-chain/test-scan-dependencies.sh` runs eleven hermetic cases — the six failure modes, the anti-rot case, a correctly-suppressed finding, a MEDIUM-only control (MEDIUM is deliberately non-gating), a clean report, and the distinct FATAL guard against a scan that analysed no manifests at all — each asserting the exit code **and** the operative phrase of the message, so a gate failing for the wrong reason is caught rather than counted as a pass. **The harness extracts the gate rather than copying it**: `awk` lifts the first `<<'PY'` heredoc out of the shipped script into a scratch file and runs that, which is what makes a deliberate break turn the tests red — changing `GATING` to `{"CRITICAL"}` drops the run from 11/11 to 5/11. That choice is load-bearing rather than tidy-minded: the pre-existing `test-audit-plugins.sh` carries its own **copy** of `audit-plugins.sh`'s block, so it would have stayed green through the same break. Fixture allowlists are used throughout, never the real file, with `2099-12-31` and `2020-01-01` expiries so no case ages, and the extraction is guarded four ways (a line-count floor and three content assertions, one of them negative) so a silent `awk` miss cannot yield a vacuous pass. Two findings inverted the ticket's premise: `audit-plugins.sh` was **not** the untested one — it already had a harness — while `scan-dependencies.sh` had none; and that existing harness **was never wired into any workflow**, so it was a control nobody ran. Both are now steps in the job that runs the script each guards, each placed before it, so a broken policy fails before Trivy is even installed. Exit 2 stays out of scope by argument: it is raised by a shell-level guard, so reaching it means stubbing `trivy` on `PATH` — shell plumbing rather than security policy. Deliberately not addressed here and worth their own tickets: `audit-containers.sh` (513 lines, ADR-064) has no harness at all, and `test-audit-plugins.sh` both duplicates rather than extracts and asserts exit codes only, never messages.
- **`AdrIndexConsistencyTest` now reads the whole index row rather than the status cell alone, which is the point of the story (EOP-140).** Every date guard in that test read `STATUS_COLUMN` only — and a status of `Accepted` cannot go stale, so the test was watching the one cell in the table with nothing to say while every narrative claim lived in the `Implemented?` column. `ADR-014-realtime-transport.md` was the concrete double failure: it carries a bare undated `Accepted`, so `shouldCarryAmendmentDates` reached its `continue` and never asserted anything for that row, while commit `43f0cbc` folded three further events into its last cell and widened a two-event observation to five with nothing objecting. Two new invariants close the structural half of that. `shouldCarryTheLatestAmendmentDate` requires the **latest** dated amendment in an ADR body to appear somewhere in its index row; `shouldDateAnyClaimedAmendmentInTheIndex` requires any row claiming an amendment to carry an ISO date, which is the undated `(amended)` that was ADR-012's original EOP-32 defect and which the old guard misses whenever the status line carries no date. Both scan the entire row, because ADR-028 reflects its amendment in the *status* cell and ADR-024 in the *implemented* cell, so pinning either one would rebuild the same blindness one column across. **Three design choices were measured rather than assumed.** The marker pattern is the loose `[Aa]mended\s+(\d{4}-\d{2}-\d{2})` because two idioms are in use — the bolded blockquote and the italic parenthetical of ADR-027 and ADR-030, with ADR-028 using both — and the strict bolded form found no marker at all in two of the six files that turned out to be drifting. The `\bamended\b` word boundary is load-bearing: a substring search for `mended` matches "recommended" in ADR-027, ADR-052 and ADR-054. And **latest** rather than every amendment date is deliberate, because requiring every one fails ten ADRs, ADR-019 alone carrying five, which would bloat the rows against the standing complaint that they are already too long to review. **Six rows were genuinely stale and are now current** — ADR-021, ADR-024, ADR-027, ADR-028, ADR-030 and ADR-033 — each gaining a sentence carrying what its latest amendment actually says rather than the bare date. ADR-024 is the instructive one: a long, well-maintained row citing two *later* dates, which still omitted the 2026-08-13 retraction of its own second Consequences bullet. A well-maintained row is not the same as a complete one. **What is still unguarded is stated in the test's own Javadoc rather than left implied**, because the `43f0cbc` defect was semantic and no matcher can catch it, and because three further candidates were tried and rejected on evidence: the reverse check (every date in a row must appear in the ADR) produces seven false positives that are all legitimate, since a row may truthfully cite another ADR's amendment date or an observation date belonging to an event outside the file; every-date-not-latest for the reason above; and commit-hash existence, which cannot be verified in CI at all because `actions/checkout` defaults to `fetch-depth: 1`. Two synthetic self-tests prove each matcher fires, and both were also confirmed live: temporarily undating ADR-021's row marker produced two failures naming that ADR
- **Every agent's `permission` block is now walked by the build, closing the one limitation [ADR-065](docs/adr/ADR-065-agent-role-boundaries-at-the-permission-layer.md) recorded against itself (EOP-000).** That ADR scoped all seventeen agent definitions to their roles and then said plainly that nothing in `./mvnw verify` walks `.opencode/agents/`, so an eighteenth agent file with no `permission` block would hold unrestricted shell, unrestricted writes and the full scheduler set — the same posture that let `@product-owner` implement a story twice. `AgentPermissionDeclarationTest`, the twelfth gate in `src/test/java/org/maglez/eop/docs/`, parses each frontmatter block with the SnakeYAML already on the test classpath (`FeatureFlagRegistryTest`'s precedent, zero new dependencies) and fails on four conditions: no `permission` block at all, `edit` or `bash` unstated without a `"*": deny` catch-all covering it, any of the six mutating scheduler tools left undenied without a declared exception, and an exception whose justification is under 80 characters. **Closing it took eight agent files as well as a test, and that is the more interesting half.** `architecture-guardian`, `db-designer`, `devops-engineer`, `performance-engineer`, `tester-api`, `tester-unit-and-quality`, `ui-builder` and `tech-lead` declared no `edit` key at all and held it through the implicit `{"permission":"*","action":"allow","pattern":"*"}` baseline — the exact shape of the breach, since omitting a key affirmatively grants it. Each now states `edit: allow` outright. Nothing those agents may do changed; what changed is that the grant is a decision on the record rather than an omission indistinguishable from an oversight, which is the only distinction a gate can enforce, so the invariant holds with **zero** exceptions on `edit` and `bash`. The two scheduler exceptions are declared as records carrying their reasons — `@performance-engineer` owns the scheduled k6 job so it keeps `schedule_job`/`run_job`/`update_job`/`delete_job` while still denying `cleanup_global` and `install_skill`, and `@tech-lead` already holds `task: allow` so denying it the scheduler would draw the boundary in the wrong place — and an entry naming an agent that no longer exists fails, so an exception retires itself instead of decaying into silence, on `SeparationInvariantTest`'s pattern. A `MINIMUM_AGENTS = 17` floor stops the walk passing over an empty directory, and a synthetic self-test proves the missing-key detector and the catch-all reader both fire independently of the real configuration. **The bound is stated rather than implied: it checks declaration, not sense.** `bash: {"*": allow}` declares `bash` and grants everything, and a hostile author has no more trouble writing that than writing nothing — what closes is the *silent* case, which is the one that has actually happened here twice. Whether a declared value is the right one stays reviewer-enforced, the same split as `ConditionalOnPropertyHavingValueTest` mechanising `havingValue = "true"` while leaving ADR-013's bean-repetition clause to judgement. The `.opencode` sentinel file is not `.md` and so is never read. ADR-065, its `docs/adr/README.md` row and Blueprint §7.8 were all amended in the same commit, because each carried the now-false claim that no such gate exists
- **`audit-plugins.sh` now exits 2 (could not run) when the npm advisory endpoint is unreachable, instead of silently passing (EOP-193).** When `npm audit --json` cannot reach the registry it writes an error document — a JSON object with a top-level `"error"` key and no `"metadata.vulnerabilities"` — rather than an audit report. The script previously treated this as a clean tree: `report.get("vulnerabilities", {})` returned an empty dict, so no findings were parsed and the high/critical gate passed. The staleness comparison was equally wrong in the other direction: `set(accepted) - set(found)` was the full allowlist, so every entry appeared "no longer reported" and the script recommended deleting live accepted-risk documentation. The fix is a document-validity guard at the top of the Python heredoc: if `"error"` is present in the top-level document, or if `"metadata.vulnerabilities"` is absent, the script prints a transport-failure message to stderr and exits 2. The staleness comparison is skipped entirely in this case. The shell exit-code handler at the bottom now distinguishes `exit 2` (could not run, with its own message) from `exit 1` (gate rejected). `tools/supply-chain/test-audit-plugins.sh` covers four cases: a 503 error document exits 2, a document with metadata but no `metadata.vulnerabilities` exits 2, a valid audit with all three allowlisted GHSAs present exits 0, and a valid audit with an unaccepted high advisory exits 1. SETUP.md records the three exit codes and the unreachable-endpoint behaviour.
- **Every digest-pinned container in the repository is now audited against a committed baseline, not just the k6 image the finding was raised about (EOP-159, ADR-064).** `tools/supply-chain/` covered the seven OpenCode npm plugins and nothing else, so the container layer had no coverage at all. `tools/supply-chain/audit-containers.sh` now checks all three pinned images — `grafana/k6`, `sonarqube` and `sonarsource/sonar-scanner-cli` — against `expected-containers.json`, as a step in the existing non-required `supply-chain` job. Be precise about what it is: a digest pin *is* its content, so unlike the npm audit this is not compromise detection and it reads no image contents, no CVE feed and no signatures. It enforces coverage and procedure — bidirectional roster drift (which also catches *unpinning*, since replacing a digest with a tag orphans the baseline entry), agreement between the three files that each carry the `sonar-scanner-cli` digest, pin form, registry shape, and ADR-055's `linux/amd64` trap encoded for every future pin rather than recorded for one past one. That mirror check closes a live hole: `sonar-ratchet-ui`'s `sourceHash` covers `ui/package.json`, `ui/tsconfig.json`, `ui/vite.config.ts` and `ui/src/**`, so bumping the scanner digest in `tools/sonar/scan-ui.sh` without rescanning left two committed files asserting a scanner that was never used, with nothing catching it. `mediaType` is compared to the baseline rather than required to be an index, because `sonarsource/sonar-scanner-cli` legitimately is a single-platform amd64 manifest with no attestations — a universal "must be an index" rule would have been wrong on a third of the roster. Sixteen negative tests all fail with an accurate first finding, the sharpest being k6's genuine arm64 child digest paired with a fully self-consistent baseline describing it correctly, caught on the amd64 check alone. A registry failure and a zero-reference scan both exit 2, "could not run", rather than passing. The discovery regex carries a nine-case self-test that runs before any file is opened, and this is the one part of the script where hand-verification was insufficient: every other check compares two artefacts and reports a difference, so it fails loudly, whereas a reference the parser never matches is a pin the audit never mentions and the run still goes green. It was added because the review round caught the parser misreading a registry-qualified reference with a port (`registry.example.com:5000/img@sha256:…` parsed as the image `5000/img`, since a path component may not contain a colon — `ghcr.io/…` was never affected, a dot already being legal), and it was then proven to fire by reverting the regex and watching it exit 1 before any image was listed. Still uncovered and now named as such rather than merely unnoticed: the GitHub Actions layer (nine actions over fifteen unpinned sites, pre-existing posture) and the Maven plugin layer (~31 versions owned by the Spring Boot parent, a different mechanism entirely)
- **`eslint-plugin-react` is now enabled in `ui/eslint.config.js`, so JSX rule findings fail `npm run verify` locally instead of surfacing only in the post-push `sonar-ratchet-ui` job (EOP-192, ADR-009).** The config enabled `@eslint/js` recommended, `typescript-eslint` `recommendedTypeChecked` and `eslint-plugin-react-hooks` — but not `eslint-plugin-react`, of whose rules SonarQube's own TypeScript analyser runs thirty. Every `typescript:S67xx` finding backed by one of them was therefore invisible to the local toolchain, and EOP-191 demonstrated it rather than theorising it: three `typescript:S6772` (`react/jsx-child-element-spacing`) findings were fixed with `npm run verify` green both before and after, so the only detector was a CI job that runs after the push, and fixing what it reported meant booting a container and re-running an amd64-only scanner under emulation. **The rule set is selected to match what SonarQube runs, not to match `react.configs.flat.recommended`, and that is the load-bearing decision.** The preset would not have closed the demonstrated miss at all — `react/jsx-child-element-spacing` is off by default and absent from it — while switching on `react/prop-types` and `react/react-in-jsx-scope`, both meaningless here, whose noise is what invites the blanket disable that would have hidden the next finding too. Matching Sonar's set closes the gap by construction and excludes `react-in-jsx-scope` as a consequence of the principle rather than by exception, since it backs no Sonar key. Twenty-nine `react/*` rules are listed explicitly at `"error"`, each annotated in-file with the Sonar key it serves. **Two deliberate deviations, both argued rather than assumed.** `react/prop-types` (S6774) is the single rule of Sonar's thirty omitted: it demands runtime PropTypes that are redundant when every prop shape is already a checked TypeScript interface, and it would fire on every component in the tree. Its companion `react/default-props-match-prop-types` (S6775) is kept, because with no PropTypes declared it is inert rather than noisy — the two are not a package. And `react/jsx-no-bind` (S6480) carries `allowArrowFunctions: true`, decided by measurement: the default reports 33 errors across seven components, the option reports 0, and the front-end ratchet reports zero S6480 on the same tree — so the option gives measured parity with the gate while still catching `.bind()` and `function`-expression props, which nothing local caught before. Tightening it to the default is a separate ticket, not foreclosed; bundling it here would have been exactly the large unrelated cleanup that should be gated on its own. **Five findings surfaced and all five were fixed, none silenced** — no `eslint-disable` was added anywhere. Two were gated by the ratchet: `react/no-array-index-key` (S6479) in `ErrorSummary.tsx`, where the list now de-duplicates its messages and keys by text so uniqueness is guaranteed rather than assumed, with a new test asserting a repeated message renders once; and `react/no-unused-prop-types` (S6767) in `GameScreen.tsx`, where `OtherPlayerSeatProps.position` was genuinely dead because positioning is applied by the wrapper `div` at the call site. Two more `no-array-index-key` sites sat in `ErrorSummary` stubs inside `GameOverScreen.test.tsx` and `GameScreen.test.tsx` — TEST scope, so never gated, but real lint errors. The fifth is the interesting one: `react/no-unescaped-entities` (S6766) at `JoinSessionForm.tsx:118` is **absent from the Sonar baseline**, a case where local ESLint is stricter than the ratchet because Sonar configures the rule's forbidden-entity set differently. Read "the two tools now agree" as "the same rules are enabled", never as "the same findings are produced". **The ratchet tightened as a result**: `MAINTAINABILITY` fell 23 → 21 and `--tighten` lowered the baseline, with `sonar-ui-baseline.json` and `sonar-ui-report.json` committed together; `RELIABILITY` stayed at 6 because both gated findings were classified `MAINTAINABILITY`-only. No ceiling was raised, so no ceiling argument is owed. The acceptance criterion was proven locally rather than asserted — a throwaway file reintroducing the EOP-191 pattern made `npm run verify` fail at the lint step with two `react/jsx-child-element-spacing` errors, and was then removed. **What remains open, stated rather than left to be discovered:** four other plugins back gated findings and are still absent, so `sonarjs` (S3358, S3735, S3776), `unicorn` (S7781, S7741), `jsx-a11y` (S6819) and `import` (S3863) are each still CI-only. This story closed the react half; enabling the rest is a separate decision per plugin, for the same reason the rule set here was chosen deliberately.
- **The front end is now a second SonarQube project with its own issue ratchet, and it is the first static analysis `ui/src` has ever had beyond ESLint and `tsc` (EOP-187, ADR-063).** ADR-060's ratchet was Java-only by construction rather than by choice: it invokes the scanner as a Maven plugin, so its scope is the Maven project, and `source-hash.sh`'s freshness token covered `pom.xml` and `.java` files only — its own comment named `ui/` as out of scope. Roughly 2,800 lines of shipped React and TypeScript were therefore ungated. A new project `eop-threat-modeling-ui` closes that, with its own baseline (`tools/sonar/sonar-ui-baseline.json`, seeded at Reliability 9 / Maintainability 26 / Security 0 from a real scan), its own evidence (`sonar-ui-report.json`), its own entry points (`tools/sonar/scan-ui.sh`, `ratchet-ui.sh`) and its own CI job (`sonar-ratchet-ui`). **Two projects rather than one polyglot project is the decision, and the reason is that a ceiling has to mean something:** one set of three integers spanning both languages would let a new front-end finding be paid for out of Java headroom, and the number would keep moving truthfully while saying nothing about either population. The two jobs carry no `needs:` and fail independently, so a red front-end ratchet cannot hide a red Java one. **The two freshness tokens are deliberately disjoint** — Java over `pom.xml` and `src/**/*.java`, front end over `ui/package.json`, `ui/tsconfig.json`, `ui/vite.config.ts` and every `.ts`/`.tsx` under `ui/src` — so neither language's authors are asked to boot a container for the other's change; `ui/package-lock.json` is excluded on purpose, because the TypeScript sensor reads `tsconfig.json` and source rather than the lockfile, so including it would force a rescan on every `npm audit fix` for a change that provably cannot move a count. **The three shared Python scripts were parameterised into `java` and `ui` flavours rather than copied**, on the reasoning `source-hash.sh` already carried in prose: the delicate logic is the refusal to compare a stale report and the refusal to infer a missing count as zero, and two copies of a refusal is one copy that can rot unnoticed while the other is maintained.
