Every new parameter defaults to the Java value, so `ratchet.sh`, the existing job and ADR-060's recorded commands work unchanged — and the Java branch was checked byte-identical against `git show HEAD:` output rather than merely re-tested, which is what licenses the claim that the Java path was not touched. `--report`/`--baseline` default *per flavour* rather than to the Java paths, so `--flavour ui` alone cannot write front-end prose into the Java ceiling. **The scanner runs as a digest-pinned `sonarsource/sonar-scanner-cli` container**, not a `brew install`, because `ui/` has no `pom.xml` to resolve a plugin GAV from and a floating CLI would silently drift the `scannerImage` the report records; docker was already a hard prerequisite for the server, so this adds no new one. Every scanner path is `ui/`-relative behind `sonar.projectBaseDir=ui`, because Vitest's LCOV `SF:` lines are — pointing `sonar.sources` at `ui/src` from the repository root analyses exactly the same files and then matches not one coverage record, reporting 0% while looking entirely healthy. Coverage itself is new: `@vitest/coverage-v8` plus a `coverage` script, with `verify` running `coverage` in place of `test` so 259 tests execute once rather than twice, and `all: true` because without it coverage is computed only over files a test imported, so adding an unreferenced module would *raise* the percentage. **Front-end coverage is recorded and deliberately not gated** at 90.8%: there is no `thresholds` block, because JaCoCo already owns Java coverage and two numbers for one invariant is how two numbers drift apart — so unlike the Java side, a change halving front-end coverage passes provided it introduces no new issue, and that asymmetry is intentional. `src/main.tsx` is left in scope reporting 0%; excluding the mount entry point would have been the more flattering choice and the less honest one. **A latent defect in `write-report.py` was found and fixed in passing:** its first consistency check compared a facet counting one issue once per software quality against a list counting each issue once, which coincide only when no finding carries two qualities — true of the Java project by luck rather than by construction, and false the moment the front end's nine two-quality findings arrived, so the first UI scan emitted a warning naming a pagination failure that had not occurred. It is now two independent checks with distinct meanings, both writing to stderr only, so no committed report changed bytes. Four limits stated plainly. The scanner image sits in the same uncovered supply-chain gap as the whole Maven plugin layer — `tools/supply-chain/` audits only the seven OpenCode npm plugins — so the digest pin plus ADR-063 is the whole control, and moving that digest is both a supply-chain change and a baseline-invalidating one because a scanner upgrade ships retuned rules. The image is amd64-only and runs under emulation on Apple silicon, so the local scan is slow. Front-end test files are analysed and recorded under `scope.TEST` but never gated, for ADR-060's reason. And the freshness hash closes the accident of forgetting to rescan, not the intent to hand-edit a committed report — that remains a matter for review, exactly as it does on the Java side and for the accepted-advisory allowlist.
- **The CIDR mask arithmetic that decides who may spoof a client IP is now tested for non-byte-aligned prefixes (EOP-182).** `TrustedProxies$Range.contains` compares whole bytes in a loop, then masks the final partial byte — and that mask was reached by nothing in the suite. Every existing CIDR test used `/24`, `/0`, `/32` or `/128`, all of which give `remainingBits == 0` and return early at `TrustedProxies.java:166`, so the two lines that actually do the partial-byte comparison (`TrustedProxies.java:169-170`) sat at 0 of 8 and 0 of 16 instructions with both branches unexercised. Ten tests in `TrustedProxiesTest.CidrEntries` now drive `/25` from both halves (`10.0.0.0/25` and `10.0.0.128/25`, both valid because `isNetworkAddress` only requires bits 25-31 clear), `/12` where the whole-byte loop runs *before* the mask decides, `/30` at mask `0xFC`, and an IPv6 `/49` at mask `0x80` on byte 6 — each paired with a boundary assertion on the address one step outside the block. They reach the arithmetic entirely through the public seam `TrustedProxies.of(...).includes(...)` with no reflection, so they test the trust decision rather than the record that implements it. **Why this mattered more than its coverage delta:** a mask that is one bit too wide silently enlarges the set of callers permitted to nominate their own `X-Forwarded-For`, which is the rate-limiter-bucket rotation defect EOP-26 closed, and ADR-019 treats that throttle as a primary security control rather than defence in depth — so the failure mode is a quiet widening of a trust boundary, not a visible error. `contains` moves from 39/24 to 63/0 instructions and 7/3 to 10/0 branches. **Limits stated plainly.** A `/20` case was considered and declined: it yields the same `0xF0` mask as the `/12` with one extra loop iteration, so it would add a test without adding a distinct behaviour. `Range.network()` remains at 0 of 5 instructions and is deliberately still untested — it is a defensive `clone()` override on a `private record` with no production caller, reachable only by reflection, so a test for it would pin the reflection rather than the invariant. And the suite total rising from 1396 to 1406 is not evidence the project's coverage was low: JaCoCo already measured 97.0% instruction and 88.9% branch against gates of 80% and 70%, and the 60.5% figure that prompted the story was a SonarQube analysis submitted without a complete `target/site/jacoco/jacoco.xml` — the same measurement artefact ADR-060 records as its historical 68%, not a regression
- **Sections 2.3, 2.4 and 3 of `docs/architecture/building-blocks.md` are now build-enforced against `pom.xml` in both directions (EOP-000).** That inventory exists because a `spring.*` property whose owning module is not a declared dependency is inert — nothing reads it, nothing binds it, nothing warns and nothing fails — and the project had been bitten by that twice (EOP-27, EOP-33). But the inventory was itself hand-maintained prose, so it decayed exactly as `AdrIndexConsistencyTest`, `EnumMirrorParityTest` and `DbSchemaDiagramTest` were each written to stop their own documents decaying: section 3's footer claimed it had been "re-verified against `pom.xml`" on 2026-09-02 while the table omitted three of the six non-Boot dependencies the pom declares — `postgresql`, `testcontainers-postgresql` and `testcontainers-junit-jupiter`. `ModuleInventoryTest`, the eleventh gate in `src/test/java/org/maglez/eop/docs/`, now compares both inventories against `/project/dependencies` on two axes each: a declared artifact with no row fails, and a row naming nothing the pom declares fails. The inverse direction is the one that matters more, because a reader consulting the table to decide whether a `spring.*` property is bound gets a confident wrong answer rather than no answer. Two structural rules come with it — no module may be listed as both present (2.3) and deliberately absent (2.4), and no module documented as absent may actually be declared, which is what catches a dependency quietly added while the table still explains why it is missing. **Section 3 had to be restructured to be checkable at all**: its first column held prose labels — `H2 (runtime)`, `Liquibase Core`, `Springdoc OpenAPI` — which name no artifact and so compare clean against every pom there has ever been. It now carries the artifactId exactly as the pom spells it, the convention 2.3 and 2.4 already used, so one extractor and one malformation rule cover all three tables and reintroducing a prose label fails the build. The pom is read with the JDK DOM parser scoped to direct children of `/project/dependencies` rather than with a regex over the file text, because this pom holds four artifactIds a text scan would admit: the `<parent>` coordinates, the project's own artifactId, `commons-text` inside `<dependencyManagement>`, and a second `h2` inside an `<exclude>` in the Boot plugin's repackage configuration. The parser is hardened with secure processing and DTD loading disabled, and left namespace-unaware deliberately so the pom's default `xmlns` does not hide every element. Five mutations against the real files prove it is not vacuous — deleting the `postgresql` row, adding an undeclared `spring-boot-starter-webflux` row, reverting one cell to its old prose label, listing `spring-boot-h2console` as both present and absent, and declaring an undocumented `commons-lang3` — each failing loudly on the intended test and each reverted. Three limits, stated plainly. It reads the first cell only, so every Role and Notes cell, and 2.3's claims about which autoconfiguration each module activates — the part a reader actually consults — remain reviewer-enforced; `commons-text` is version-managed rather than declared and so deliberately has no row, recorded in a blockquote the gate cannot mistake for a table row. It checks artifactIds alone: no groupId, no version, no scope and nothing transitive, so it is not a dependency audit and must not be cited as one — that is `tools/supply-chain/`. And it recognises one table shape, so a rewrite into some other form would leave it matching nothing, which is why three `MINIMUM_*` floors assert the tables were found at all rather than letting an empty comparison read as agreement. `./mvnw clean verify` passes at 1396 unit and 13 integration tests, and the SonarQube ratchet holds at `RELIABILITY 1 / MAINTAINABILITY 31 / SECURITY 0` with no ceiling raised.
- **`docs/architecture/building-blocks.md` — an arc42 §5 static module view, whose point is the Spring Boot 4 autoconfiguration trap rather than the layer diagram.** Boot 4 moved autoconfiguration for optional features out of `spring-boot-autoconfigure` into standalone `spring-boot-*` modules, so a `spring.*` property whose owning module is not a declared dependency is **inert** — nothing reads it, nothing binds it, nothing warns and nothing fails. This repository has been bitten twice: `spring-boot-liquibase` (migrations silently skipped) and `spring-boot-h2console` (an `h2.console` property present in both profile files but never active, which a security audit read as a live unauthenticated SQL endpoint and reported as a false CRITICAL). Section 2.3 therefore inventories the eight `spring-boot-*` modules actually declared in `pom.xml`, section 2.4 records `spring-boot-h2console` as a deliberate absence with the dead-letter property named, and a rule requires confirming a property's owning module appears in 2.3 before adding it to `application.yml`. The same one-line warning was added to `.opencode/rules/configuration.md`, which is loaded into every agent context. Section 2.2 carries the regeneration procedure, and the inventory is a hand-maintained snapshot re-verified against `pom.xml` on 2026-09-02 — nothing in the build compares the two, so it can drift silently between verifications (EOP-33)
- **The guard order in `GameSession.start()` is now documented as load-bearing, with the reasoning that makes it so.** Identity checks must precede state checks: were state checked first, a caller outside the table could distinguish a lobby (409, session not in lobby) from an in-progress game (403, not recognised) by probing the start endpoint, and so learn whether a session is joinable without holding an identity token. Identity first means every unrecognised caller receives 403 whatever state the session is in. The comment enumerates all four guards against their mapped statuses — `PlayerNotRecognisedException` 403, `NotFacilitatorException` 403, `SessionNotJoinableException` 409, `TooFewPlayersException` 409 — each verified against `GlobalExceptionHandler`, and asks for a security review before the order is changed. This is a comment only: no behaviour changed, and no test pins the ordering, so the protection remains reviewer-enforced (EOP-23)

- **`MODEL_A` moves from the `eu.` inference profile to `global.`, because a client-side payload change made the EU profile uninvocable (EOP-000, [ADR-062](docs/adr/ADR-062-model-a-on-the-global-inference-profile.md)).** OpenCode `1.18.26` sends an adaptive-thinking `block_binding.prefix_mismatch_behavior` member inside `additionalModelRequestFields` for every model in a hard-coded list that includes `claude-opus-5`, and `amazon-bedrock/eu.anthropic.claude-opus-5` rejects it with `Extra inputs are not permitted`, so every request on the primary tier failed outright rather than degrading. The identifier moves to `amazon-bedrock/global.anthropic.claude-opus-5` at all four sites that carry it — `.env`, the active assignment in `.env.example`, the live Bedrock identifier table in Blueprint §3.4.1 and the commented `.env.example` mirror beside it — which also reconciles three values that had silently diverged: a `/models` TUI override on `global.`, a hand-edited `.env` on a bare `anthropic.` prefix, and tracked configuration still on `eu.`. `MODEL_B` and `MODEL_D` stay on `eu.` because `claude-sonnet-4-6` and `claude-haiku-4-5` are absent from that list, and the commented Zen block stays as it is because Zen model names carry no region prefix at all. The operator confirmed that EU-only inference is not a requirement here — the prefix reflected what the AWS account could invoke from London — which is what made this option available rather than blocked, so [ADR-046](docs/adr/ADR-046-gate-model-capability-floor.md)'s rejection of a `global.` profile for two audit gates stands as written and is not overturned by it: that was a gate for which a bare identifier was equally available, this is the only option that keeps the primary tier working without pinning the whole toolchain below `1.18.26`. `SeparationInvariantTest` passes unchanged, and this was run rather than assumed: the new value still matches the parser, stays distinct from the other two Anthropic tiers and stays in the same family, so no family boundary moves and `ALLOWED_OVERLAPS` stays at exactly one entry. Four limits stated plainly. This is not a model upgrade — it is the same `claude-opus-5` reached by a different inference profile, so no recorded probe verdict or capability measurement is invalidated. It does not make the project EU-resident, which it was not before either, four of the seven tiers already being bare identifiers whose routing ADR-046 records as unverified in both directions. It does not change `AWS_REGION`, which selects the endpoint a request is sent to rather than where inference is served, so editing it would achieve nothing. And it licenses nothing beyond this one tier: a second tier moving to `global.` needs its own reason written down. One gap is left open on purpose — `.env` is gitignored, so nothing checks that a developer's local value agrees with `.env.example`, and a stale `eu.` there presents as a dead primary tier with no signal pointing at the ADR.
- **The Definition of Done grows from five gates to seven: `@sonarqube-expert` adjudicates the SonarQube issue ratchet and `@dependency-vulnerability` adjudicates the Trivy CVE scan (EOP-000, [ADR-061](docs/adr/ADR-061-two-new-dod-gates-sonar-ratchet-and-cve.md)).** Both are `mode: subagent`, `temperature: 0.1`, `edit: deny`, `task: deny`, carry the full Sign-off Contract and the read-only-while-reviewing rule, and are pinned to `{env:MODEL_F}` — which now holds four agents, so it may no longer be described as the two-gate tier. `@sonarqube-expert` runs `tools/sonar/ratchet.sh` (never `--tighten`) against ADR-060's two committed artefacts, checks the `sourceHash` freshness first and treats a stale report as a rejection rather than a caveat, judges only the three gated integers, and cites a finding as the quality, rule key and path the tool prints; it computes no metrics of its own, and both the rival `.opencode/sonar-baseline.json` of self-estimated complexity and coverage figures and the "INITIALIZED" write path present in the first draft were removed, because ADR-060's whole value is a measured number two identical scans reproduce. `@dependency-vulnerability` runs `tools/supply-chain/scan-dependencies.sh` and **holds** ADR-050's HIGH-and-CRITICAL-only policy rather than tightening it — the first draft rejected on any severity including Low, which ADR-050 had already rejected as the way a scanner earns a reputation for noise and then gets switched off — and the `cargo audit`/`pip-audit`/`govulncheck` invocations for ecosystems this repository does not have, along with the `dependency-check-maven` call that needs an NVD API key a secretless repository cannot supply, were removed in favour of the pinned Trivy the CI job actually runs. Both tier pins rest on fresh two-stage probes recorded in Blueprint §3.4.1, and the round is recorded honestly because both incumbent gate tiers failed the Sonar role first: `zai.glm-5` reported "Exit code: 0" against a command exiting `1` on two separate fixtures, and `minimax.minimax-m2.5` invented rule titles for `java:S3516` and `java:S6218` and dismissed both genuine findings as scanner false positives, passing only after the brief was re-scoped to stop demanding a line number the `quality|rule|path|hash` fingerprint does not carry — its hash digests the offending line's text, not its position. The `eu.anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-6` control passed both roles but is structurally ineligible for the Sonar gate, because the two tester agents on that tier author the test code it reviews and 211 of the 243 measured findings sit under `src/test/java`. Two limits stated plainly: **neither agent is the enforcement** — the `sonar-ratchet` and `dependency-cve` jobs already fail mechanically with no LLM in the path, and neither agent may edit a baseline, an allowlist, `pom.xml` or `ui/package-lock.json` to turn a red gate green, so what they add is the judgement a script cannot make, over whether a ceiling raise was argued and whether an allowlist entry carries a real reachability trace; and **neither CI job is a required status check**, so a red one still blocks by review rather than by branch protection. `SeparationInvariantTest` was extended to seven gates (`MINIMUM_AGENTS` 15 → 17, `MINIMUM_COMPARISONS` 18 → 27 as the true pair count rose 21 → 30) and `ALLOWED_OVERLAPS` deliberately still holds exactly one entry, because neither new agent authors anything and neither reviews the architecture documentation that the one surviving exception covers. The `/goal` budgets were raised from 40 turns / 60 minutes / 500k tokens to 48 / 75 minutes / 650k, since figures sized for five sign-offs would have exhausted the turn budget before the seventh gate reported and paused the story — which reads as a tooling failure rather than as missing review.
- **Static-analysis issue counts are now ratcheted: they may fall or hold, never rise (EOP-181, [ADR-060](docs/adr/ADR-060-sonarqube-issue-ratchet.md)).** The project had plenty of static analysis and no *trend* — Checkstyle, SpotBugs, JaCoCo and the enforcer each gate an absolute threshold, which catches the individual violation and never notices the total climbing one tolerated finding per pull request. SonarQube now supplies the missing memory. It runs on the developer's machine from `compose.sonar.yml` — one digest-pinned service on `127.0.0.1:9000`, embedded H2, no PostgreSQL, and no `sudo`, because Colima's VM already clears the Elasticsearch `vm.max_map_count` floor — and `tools/sonar/scan.sh` runs `./mvnw verify`, invokes the scanner fully qualified so Sonar stays structurally out of `./mvnw verify`, waits for the Compute Engine rather than reading stale counts, and commits `tools/sonar/sonar-report.json`. The new `sonar-ratchet` CI job then compares that report against `tools/sonar/sonar-baseline.json` and **contacts no SonarQube server at all** — no service container, no scanner, no token, no JDK, about a second of runtime. An offline scanner was not an option (SonarQube removed dry-run mode in 5.2) and an ephemeral server per pull request would have cost 5–6 minutes. The hole that design opens is staleness, closed by a `sourceHash` over `pom.xml` and every `*.java` under `src/main/java` and `src/test/java`, defined once in `tools/sonar/source-hash.sh` so the local and CI sides cannot drift into disagreeing about it: edit Java without rescanning and the gate blocks. Baselines store issue *fingerprints* as a multiset, not just totals, so a regression names the rule and the file — and a multiset rather than a set because a genuinely new finding must not be able to hide behind an identical existing one. The measured baseline is 11 Reliability / 232 Maintainability / 0 Security at 95.1% coverage; the scan is deterministic, verified by two independent scans agreeing on every count, hash and all 243 fingerprints. Improvements auto-tighten the ceiling locally, so the gate strengthens as the code improves. Five honest limits: it proves three integers did not increase, not that the code is good; a hand-edited report would pass, because the hash closes the accident of forgetting to rescan and not the intent to lie; a SonarQube upgrade retunes rules, hence the digest pin and the rule that moving it means re-measuring in the same commit; `ui/` TypeScript is out of scope; and the six scripts under `tools/sonar/` have no automated tests of their own, since `./mvnw verify` sees only `src/main/java` — their behaviour was verified by hand across the at-ceiling pass, a real added code smell, a stale report, a failing run leaving the baseline byte-identical, an end-to-end tighten and three determinism samples, which is real evidence that no machine repeats, and the asymmetry is what makes it worth recording: a gate that wrongly fails is loud, whereas one that wrongly passes is indistinguishable from a clean tree. That is acceptable while the job only advises and becomes a prerequisite the moment it is promoted to a required check. The job is deliberately **not** a required status check yet, so the stale-report friction can be measured before it blocks a merge.

- **The code-review gate no longer shares a model identifier with the agents whose tests it reviews (EOP-179, [ADR-059](docs/adr/ADR-059-code-review-gate-on-its-own-model-tier.md)).** `@code-reviewer`, `@tester-api` and `@tester-unit-and-quality` all resolved to `MODEL_B` = `amazon-bedrock/eu.anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-6`, so a tester-authored test was reviewed at identical weights — neither family- nor model-independent in the two senses Blueprint §3.1 defines. The cause was arithmetic rather than allocation: six tier variables held only five distinct models (`MODEL_E` duplicates `MODEL_C` deliberately), so some gate had to share a tier with an agent authoring what it reviewed, which is why EOP-178's attempt to fix it by moving `@code-reviewer` onto the existing `MODEL_F` was withdrawn before merge — `MODEL_F` already held the ADR-authoring `@architecture-guardian`, relocating the overlap onto architecture documentation instead of eliminating it. A seventh variable `MODEL_G` = `amazon-bedrock/zai.glm-5` (Z.AI, a sixth family, 202,752 context / 101,376 max output against ADR-046's 128,000 / 40,000 floors) now holds `@code-reviewer` alone, asserted by `.envrc` and switchable through both blocks of `.env.example` with the Zen route `opencode/glm-5` marked unprobed. Because ADR-046 clause 4 makes a probe verdict role-specific, the 2026-08-21 architecture/security verdict for `zai.glm-5` was **not** treated as transferable: a fresh code-review-specific two-stage probe was run against `SecureRandomDeckShuffler.java` with a `MODEL_B` control, both passing on all four axes (real tool call, verbatim quote of line 55, correct line number, terminal `VERDICT:` line) and both correctly declining to invent the modulo-bias defect the file's javadoc pre-empts; the verdict is recorded as a dated sub-block in Blueprint §3.4.1, including the candidate's one inaccuracy in supporting prose. The invariant also stopped being prose: `SeparationInvariantTest` joins the nine repository-integrity gates in `src/test/java/org/maglez/eop/docs/`, resolving every agent's pin from `.opencode/opencode.json` against the active block of `.env.example` and failing the build — loudly, via `IllegalStateException`, never by skipping — if a Definition-of-Done gate shares a model identifier with an agent that authored an artefact class that gate reviews. Reverting the pin was demonstrated to turn it red with two failures naming both tester overlaps. **The limits, stated plainly:** this closes the invariant for production code, infrastructure and test code and for nothing else. It is not unconditional. `@security-auditor` still shares `MODEL_F` with `@architecture-guardian`, which authors ADRs and C4 models, so architecture documentation remains reviewed by a tier-mate at one model identifier — carried as the single declared, justified, self-retiring allow-list entry in the new test, and any restatement that drops the three-artefact-class qualifier is a defect a reviewer must reject. The gate→artefact-class and author→artefact-class mappings are semantic facts declared in the test, so they can still go stale without the build noticing, and family independence is a property of the allocation rather than a claim that the new model reasons better — the control's analysis was in fact the more precise of the two.

- **The k6 canary's numbers are now visible per build and accumulate into a published trend (EOP-169, ADR-055 §5 amended).** EOP-155 made k6 run on every build, but its results were reachable only by downloading and unzipping the `k6-results` artifact for one run, and ADR-055 §5 said flatly that the artifact "is for debugging regressions, not for long-term trend analysis" — so nothing showed whether latency was drifting across commits, and the fact that performance testing runs at all was invisible to anyone not reading workflow logs. Three additions, none of which changes what the canary measures or when it fails. First, a `Render k6 metrics to the run summary` step writes p50/p95/p99/max, requests per second, error rate, iterations and checks as a Markdown table into `$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY` on **every** run, pull requests included. It is a *separate* step carrying `if: always()` rather than a few lines appended to the canary, because the canary `exit 1`s the moment a threshold is breached and trailing code in that step would never run — the numbers would vanish in precisely the case they are wanted. Second, a new `perf-trend` job appends one flat JSON line per build to `ci-history.jsonl` on an orphan `perf-history` branch. Third, `tools/perf/trend-page.html` is republished beside that file as `index.html` and served by GitHub Pages, rendering p50/p95/p99 as a line chart plus a table of the thirty most recent builds each linking to its commit. **Why a separate job rather than another step in `image`:** GitHub Actions scopes `permissions` per job with no per-step granularity, so appending from inside `image` would have widened its `contents: read` to `contents: write` for the GHCR-publishing steps as well; `perf-trend` holds `contents: write` and nothing else and never sees `packages` scope. `needs: image` means a red canary records no point, and the gate tests `github.event_name == 'push'` as well as the ref — not redundantly, because `workflow_dispatch` and the weekly `schedule` also report `refs/heads/main` and neither should append. Publishing by pushing to the branch keeps the permission set that small: `actions/configure-pages` and `deploy-pages` would have required `pages: write` and `id-token: write`, and pointing Pages at the branch is instead a one-off settings change — the same class of manual action ADR-055 §4 already records for promoting the canary to a required status check, which this story deliberately does not do. Zero third-party Actions were added, keeping the property recorded in ADR-055 §2 (only `actions/checkout@v4` and `actions/download-artifact@v4` are used, both first-party), and no repository secret: the push uses the built-in `GITHUB_TOKEN`. **The two populations are now documented as incomparable rather than merely separated.** `docs/performance/TRENDS.md` is a hand-curated record of local runs on a developer Mac through Colima against a t3.small-bound topology, measured against p95 < 200 ms; the CI series is a 2-vCPU shared runner measured against the relaxed `THRESHOLDS_CI` of p95 < 500 ms. Mixing them into one line would show step changes caused by hardware rather than by code, so CI still never touches `TRENDS.md` — what ADR-055 §5 got wrong was not the separation but the conclusion that CI results therefore have no trend worth keeping. `.opencode/rules/performance-testing.md` gains a section stating both populations and the three routes CI results take to a human. **The page is deliberately not Chart.js.** The chart is roughly sixty lines of hand-rolled SVG with no build step and no CDN, because a `<script src>` would put third-party view-time code on a public page in a repository whose CI uses zero third-party Actions and whose npm roster is digest-audited, and Subresource Integrity would pin the bytes while still leaving a network dependency and a hash to maintain. It also renders from a `file://` checkout, which is what made it testable without Pages. **Four defects were found by testing rather than by review, and are worth recording because three of them fail silently.** A first attempt at the trend job read `test/k6/results/summary.json` from a fresh checkout — a path `.gitignore` ignores, so the file was never there, the `-f` test was always false and the job exited **0** having appended nothing: the exact green-while-broken class ADR-016 records for k6's own InfluxDB writes. The artifact is now downloaded and a missing or empty summary is a hard `::error::` with an `ls -R`, never a skip. `actions/checkout` with `ref: perf-history` hard-fails on a branch that does not exist yet, making a later "create if missing" step unreachable on the very first run; the branch is now probed with `git ls-remote --exit-code --heads` and bootstrapped with `git init`. The row-validation guard was first written as a comma-separated jq stream, `(.p50, .p95, … | type == "number")`, which is broken: `jq -e` takes its exit status from the **last** value only, so a null `p95` passed — confirmed empirically, rewritten as `[…] | all(…)`, and covered by a fourteen-case negative battery. And `error_rate` reads `.metrics.http_req_failed.value`, never `.passes`/`.fails`, which report `0` and `221` on a fully healthy run and would have recorded every green build as a 100% failure. **Evidence.** The publishing logic was rehearsed end to end against a local `file://` origin over five scenarios (23 assertions): bootstrap onto an absent branch, producing a true orphan with exactly one commit and a three-file tree; append onto an existing branch; a genuine `--depth 1` shallow clone (`file://` rather than a bare path, because git silently ignores `--depth` for local-path clones and the scenario would have proved nothing); a genuinely rejected concurrent push whose replay preserved the rival's data point and appended ours after it; and an exhausted five-attempt retry that exits non-zero rather than passing silently. The page was rendered in jsdom over seven scenarios (30 assertions): healthy series, single point (no `NaN` from dividing by zero, and genuinely centred), empty file, all-lines-invalid, partially malformed, both HTTP and network failure, and an XSS battery confirming that a series carrying `<img src=x onerror=…>` in `sha` and `<script>` in `checks` injects no markup — all text is written via `textContent`, `sha` is linked only if it matches `/^[0-9a-f]{7,40}$/` and `checks` printed only if it matches `/^[0-9]+\/[0-9]+$/`. **Three limits stated rather than glossed.** GitHub Pages must be enabled by hand once (Settings → Pages → branch `perf-history`, folder `/`) and until then the series accumulates with nothing serving it. The canary remains a smoke test of `GET /health` under `SMOKE_STAGES`, so the trend tracks that one endpoint and not the application's behaviour under load — `LOAD_STAGES` and `STRESS_STAGES` still run only locally. And the trend records only builds whose `image` job went green, which is the intended filter but does mean a performance cliff severe enough to breach a threshold leaves a *gap* in the series rather than a visible spike; the run summary is where that build's numbers are found.
- **The active player now sees a proactive follow-suit hint instead of learning the rule from a rejected play (EOP-168, ADR-037).** Follow-suit was previously enforced server-side only: `Trick.play` throws `MustFollowSuitException`, `GlobalExceptionHandler` maps it to a 422 (`GlobalExceptionHandler.java:463`, `UNPROCESSABLE_ENTITY` — not the 409 issue #299 and an earlier draft of this entry both assumed), and the front end surfaced that as an error banner — so a player discovered the constraint by violating it. A new `FollowSuitHint` component renders an inline `govuk-hint` line (`role="status"`) naming the suit that must be followed, in the hand section directly above the keyboard-fallback play button. It is gated by a build-time Vite flag, `VITE_FOLLOW_SUIT_HINT_ENABLED`, not by anything under `eop.features.*`: front-end flags are textual substitutions into the bundle, and issue #299's Definition of Done asked for a Spring `@ConditionalOnProperty` gate and a `feature-flag-registry.yml` entry that are not implementable for a presentational feature — the third time that confusion has occurred, now recorded in ADR-037's 2026-08-24 amendment. The flag is read once at component scope in `GameScreen.tsx` and threaded into `FollowSuitHint` as a prop, and the component itself returns `null` when the flag is off, so the gate sits at the single point the feature is entered and no future caller can bypass it. The hint is suppressed in three further cases: when it is not the player's turn, when no suit has been led yet (the player is leading, and the server omits `ledSuit` until a trick's first play), and — beyond the ticket's acceptance criteria — when the player's hand holds no card of the led suit, because the domain rule is follow-suit-*if-able* and a hint naming a suit the player cannot play would state a falsehood. The suit name is rendered lowercase with underscores replaced by spaces ("a denial of service card"), matching the convention already used at four of five in-repo call sites rather than the title case the ticket's example showed. Coverage is 7 new tests in `FollowSuitHint.test.tsx` for the prop logic and 8 new cases in `GameScreen.test.tsx` for the wiring plus all three flag positions (`'true'`, `'false'`, unset — the last proving the `=== 'true'` comparison fails closed); the front-end suite is 256 tests across 11 files, all passing. Shipped enabled from CI and `scripts/update-local.sh` in the same story that introduces the flag, as EOP-145 did, with the `ARG` default in `ui/Dockerfile` left at `false` so a plain `docker build` is still dark. Also distinguishes the two flag mechanisms in `.opencode/rules/feature-flags.md`, which previously named the front-end one only as "a separate mechanism" in a closing parenthesis: a new section gives a decision procedure keyed on whether the flag decides that a Spring bean exists, lists the four wiring sites beyond the declaration, and states that neither mechanism substitutes for the other. That file is loaded into every agent by one glob, so the distinction now travels with the rules rather than living only in ADR-037.
- **The database schema now has a complete, column-level ER diagram (EOP-167).** `docs/architecture/db-schema.md` renders all ten tables — `card`, `game_session`, `player`, `hand`, `hand_card`, `trick`, `trick_play`, `trick_play_component`, `game_result`, `game_result_player` — as a Mermaid `erDiagram` with every column typed and marked PK, UK or FK, alongside a "Tables at a glance" summary and a "Key design decisions" section. The gap it closes is narrower than "there was no diagram": one ER view already existed, in `docs/architecture/C4-Diagrams.md`, but it draws relationship edges only with no column detail and is scoped to the trick-play tables EOP-14 Slice B added, so it omits `game_result` and `game_result_player` entirely and says nothing about the columns of the tables it does show. The new file is derived from the eleven Liquibase changelogs under `src/main/resources/db/changelog/changes/` rather than from a live database, so it records intent as committed rather than whatever a developer's H2 file happens to hold. All fourteen relationship edges carry their `ON DELETE` behaviour, which is where the schema's lifecycle and privilege properties actually live: the composite foreign keys `fk_hand_player_seat` and `fk_trick_play_player_seat` reference `player (id, seat_order)` and so bind a hand and a play to a seat structurally rather than by convention (ADR-023); the two card foreign keys are `NO ACTION` because the catalogue is seeded reference data that no runtime path deletes; `fk_trick_winner_play` is `ON DELETE SET NULL` — the one cycle in the graph, and set-null by measurement rather than by taste, because `NO ACTION` made a resolved trick undeletable on H2; and `game_result_player.player_id` is deliberately *not* a foreign key at all, so a finished game's record survives the session sweep that removes the players it names. Identifiers are UUIDv7 minted in the application, never by a column default (ADR-018). **Limits stated plainly.** The table set is build-enforced rather than trusted: `DbSchemaDiagramTest` derives it from every `createTable` in the changelogs and fails `./mvnw verify` when the diagram omits a table, declares an entity no changelog creates, leaves an entity block unclosed, or relates an entity it never declared — a mutation check confirmed three of its six cases fire when a single entity is renamed. The limits are narrower than that sounds and are stated in the file too: it says nothing about **columns**, so types, nullability and the PK/UK/FK markers remain reviewer-enforced and a changeset adding a column to an existing table can still leave the file quietly wrong; and it is not a Mermaid syntax check, because `MermaidSequenceTextTest` is scoped to `sequenceDiagram` fences by construction, so an `erDiagram` carrying a malformed attribute line would render broken and still pass the build. Table extraction reads `createTable` only, which is sound today because every `dropTable` in the tree sits inside a `<rollback>` as the inverse of a create and no table has ever genuinely been dropped — the first real drop will need that hole closed. This entry is itself outside every prose gate's reach — those tests read `docs/**/*.md` and `CHANGELOG.md` sits at the repository root — so the claims above rest on review alone. The file's location is also provisional: `C4-Diagrams.md`'s own scope note says the ER view moves into `building-blocks.md` when EOP-47 lands, which would make `docs/architecture/db-schema.md` either that section's source or a second place to keep in step.
- **k6 now runs on every CI build as a regression canary — deliberately not as an SLO measurement (EOP-155, ADR-055).** The check is a step inside the existing `image` job rather than a job of its own, because that job already stands the whole stack up for its smoke test and already reaches it at `https://localhost`, which is exactly `test/k6/run.sh`'s default `BASE_URL` — so the canary needed no new infrastructure. It runs `SMOKE_STAGES` only (40s, 5→10 VU); `LOAD_STAGES` and `STRESS_STAGES` are never run in CI. **It is a canary and must never be read as a measurement**, because Golden Rule 1 of `.opencode/rules/performance-testing.md` requires a production-like environment and a shared 2-vCPU GitHub runner is neither the developer Mac the baseline was measured on nor the t3.small the application is deployed to. That is why the results are uploaded as the `k6-results` workflow artifact and stop there: appending them to `docs/performance/TRENDS.md` would repeat precisely the error that forced the 2026-08-05 baseline reset, when figures taken against the application's own published port were compared against figures taken through the Caddy hop. **Thresholds are split by metric class rather than uniformly relaxed**, which is the substance of the decision. `http_req_failed` keeps the real SLO of `rate < 0.001` and aborts, because a non-2xx response is never runner jitter — that is the check with teeth, catching a broken endpoint, a failed migration or a Caddy misroute. Latency is relaxed to `p(95) < 500` (aborting) and `max < 2000` (not aborting), 2.5× the 200ms SLO and roughly 50× the 9.856ms baseline, which is immune to 2-vCPU jitter while still catching a gross regression such as an N+1 or a missing index. Those three live in a new `test/k6/config/options-ci.js` selected by `K6_ENV=ci`, because k6 offers no clean CLI threshold override and `test/k6/config/options.js` encodes the real SLO values that must not be weakened to serve CI. k6 is installed as a **digest-pinned `grafana/k6` container** rather than via `grafana/setup-k6-action` or Grafana's apt repository: `tools/supply-chain/` audits only the seven OpenCode npm plugins, so an Action would have been the first unaudited external Action in the pipeline, and the apt path pins nothing. Two pinning traps are recorded in the workflow comments because both were hit while verifying it — the tag is `2.2.0` and **not** `v2.2.0`, and the digest must be the OCI image *index* digest, since pinning a per-platform sub-manifest fails on the x86_64 runner with `no matching manifest for linux/amd64` and `docker inspect` on an arm64 developer machine reports a third digest again. The container runs `--network host` to reach Caddy on loopback, and `--user "$(id -u):$(id -g)"` so it writes as the runner: the image runs as uid 12345 by default and could not otherwise write into the results mount, and switching the user is a smaller concession than making that directory world-writable. `/scripts` is mounted read-only and only the results directory is writable. **The step asserts on the exported JSON and not on the exit code alone**, which matters here more than it sounds: the InfluxDB write failure recorded in ADR-016 logs one error per flush interval and still exits 0, so an exit-code-only check is exactly the shape of gate this repository has already been bitten by. k6's exit code is captured rather than left to `set -e`, so the JSON assertion always runs, and a non-zero exit that the JSON does not explain fails separately — two genuinely independent signals. The assertion **flattens every threshold in the document rather than naming each one**, because hardcoding `.thresholds."p(95) < 500"` would silently disarm the gate the moment that string changed: `jq -r` emits `null` for a missing key, `null` is not `"true"`, and the breach test would then pass forever. A declared expected-threshold count, tied by comment to `options-ci.js`, fails the build in both directions if a threshold is added or removed. A missing or empty export, a body that does not parse as an object carrying `.metrics`, and any non-boolean threshold result all fail explicitly. Note two traps in the export format that the assertion is written around: a threshold value of `false` means **not** breached, and `http_req_failed` reports `"passes": 0, "fails": 221` on a completely healthy run because it is an inverted Rate metric whose real error rate lives in `"value"` — so the threshold booleans are the only sound signal. No `--out influxdb=` is used and no InfluxDB or Grafana container is started, so the canary cannot fail from the unrepaired write path that EOP-154 still owns. **The `image` job is not a required status check on `main`, so a breach fails loudly and visibly without blocking merges** — which is the intended end state, not an oversight. Promoting it to required is a GitHub branch-protection setting rather than anything this repository can effect, and remains outstanding.

- **Roughly 163 line citations pointed from `docs/` into the source tree and the build checked not one of them, so a citation could rot into a lie and nothing would say so (EOP-130, ADR-006 as amended 2026-08-23).** `SourceCitationAnchorTest` is the eighth documentation-integrity gate under `src/test/java/org/maglez/eop/docs/`, and it exists because EOP-49 was filed for nothing but drifted anchors — seven citations that had slid onto javadoc `@throws` tags some forty lines above the code they named — and because EOP-49's own ticket body then carried replacement numbers themselves stale by 24 lines, having been derived arithmetically rather than re-read. The ticket's estimate of "roughly thirty" citations was itself an undercount by a factor of five. Two checks are universal: every cited path must resolve against a tracked file, and the cited start and end lines must exist. The third is opt-in — a citation that carries an explicit `` (anchor: `token`) `` must contain that token inside the range it names, and the failure message reports where the token actually is, so it says how to fix the drift rather than merely that there is some. **Inference was built, measured and rejected, and the numbers are the reason opting in is the design rather than a shortcut.** A prototype that guessed the anchor from nearby backticked identifiers flagged 36 of the 67 citations it could reach — unusable, and not for want of tuning: about 58 citations deliberately target javadoc, drift *onto* javadoc was EOP-49's entire failure mode, and no rule can separate "cites the comment above `getId` on purpose" from "slid onto the comment above `getId`". Generic tokens made it worse, one citation being flagged only because `true` appears four times in `application.yml`. Opting in also buys something inference could not: a citation quoted inside an immutable dated amendment block stays truthful as history instead of being rewritten to satisfy a checker, and the 2026-08-18 amendment's own reference to `GlobalExceptionHandlerTest.java:553`'s `new NoTamperingCardDealtException(78)` — a defect since corrected to `(68)`, now at line 674 — is left exactly as written, as ADR-006's own rule about amendment blocks requires. `ADR-027:77-78`, which EOP-131 explicitly forbids editing in place, passes for the same reason. **Seventeen live citations were repaired on arrival, and one of them had been carrying a false claim rather than merely a wrong number.** `Trick.winningPlay()` was cited at `Trick.java:440`, a blank line, one line above its declaration; the same paragraph said `Trick.reconstitute` was its only caller, but `reconstitute` sits at line 147, takes a `winner` parameter and never calls it — the sole caller is `resolved()` at line 438, so the prose named the wrong method as well as the wrong line. `PlayCardUseCase.java:229` was cited three times as where `CARD_PLAYED` publishes when the publish is at 253, which ADR-025 already said correctly, so one fact was carried under two contradicting numbers in four documents. `SessionController.java:60` (a lone ` */`) and `UseCaseConfiguration.java:101` drifted by three and nineteen lines respectively across four documents each, and three `application.yml` flag citations pointed at a rate-limit key, a comment tail and a blank line. **The gate then fired on prose written by its own author, minutes after landing**, rejecting the `File.java:123` placeholders used to illustrate the syntax in the ADR amendment, which now uses real verified examples that double as live anchored citations. A ninth test, `shouldParseEveryAnchorMarkerItFinds`, exists because the first pass wrote sixteen anchors of which only fourteen were enforced: two used a placement the pattern of the day rejected, so they read as enforced while the gate saw nothing, and an anchor that appears enforced but is not is worse than no anchor. Both placements are now accepted and any unbound `anchor:` marker fails the build. Two floors — 150 citations and 12 anchors — stop it passing by matching nothing, and both have been observed failing rather than merely written. **Limits stated plainly.** Only citations that name a file are checked; the roughly 122 bare `` `:157` `` continuation references are out of scope because they are indistinguishable from ports such as `` `:8080` `` and `` `:5173` `` and their antecedent file is not mechanically recoverable, a limitation stated in the gate's own Javadoc. An anchor proves a token is present somewhere in the range, not that it is on the exact line, and never that the surrounding claim is true. Unanchored citations are checked only for resolvability and range, so the coverage this buys grows as authors opt in rather than arriving complete. Two third-party `ChangeSet.java` references are allow-listed by name. The concern recorded under EOP-132, that a line-oriented check is blinded by a 1,271-word table row, does not apply: every match on every line is parsed, and `docs/adr/README.md:23` contributes two anchored citations from one such row.
- **Card magnifier — a 2× CSS lens over hand cards and trick-zone cards (EOP-145).** Hovering a card renders a circular lens that follows the cursor and magnifies the pixels beneath it 2×; on a touch screen a tap pins the lens and a second tap, or a tap anywhere outside, dismisses it. Both game surfaces carry it — `CardFace` in your own hand (80×120) and the cards in the central trick zone (64×96, extracted to a `TrickCardFace` component because a hook cannot be called inside the `.map` that renders the plays) — and a **disabled** card still magnifies, since a card you cannot yet play is precisely the one you most need to read. The card body JSX was extracted to a `CardBody` component so that the magnified replica renders the *same* JSX as the card beneath it rather than a second copy free to drift. Three deliberate limitations, all of them worth knowing before anyone files a follow-up. It magnifies **pixels, not meaning**: `threatPrompt` reaches the DOM only through `CardFace`'s `aria-label` and is never rendered visibly, so the lens enlarges card art and rank glyphs without surfacing scenario text to a sighted player — if reading the threat text is the actual requirement then that is a different story with a different mechanism (a re-rendered text overlay, considered during discovery and deliberately left out of scope). It is `aria-hidden="true"` with `pointer-events: none` and contains nothing focusable, so it adds neither a second announcement of every card nor a keyboard trap — it is decoration layered over content that is already fully described to a screen reader, which is why no accessible name was invented for it. And it is suppressed for the duration of a drag, because `setPointerCapture` routes every subsequent `pointermove` to the captured card and would otherwise drag the lens across the table alongside the drag ghost: a mouse `pointerdown` therefore hides it, whereas a touch `pointerdown` pins it *and* still selects the card, because click-to-select plus the "Play selected card" button is the WCAG 2.2 AA SC 2.5.7 single-pointer alternative to dragging and a magnifier is not permitted to consume it. Gated by `VITE_CARD_MAGNIFIER_ENABLED`, the second live front-end flag alongside `VITE_GAME_SCREEN_ENABLED` — and **not** by the `eop.features.card-magnifier` property the ticket asked for, which could not have worked: a pure CSS lens has no Spring bean that could be absent from the application context, and a Spring property is invisible to a bundle Caddy serves from a separate path (ADR-037, amended by this change). Contrary to the merge-dark default in `.opencode/rules/feature-flags.md`, the flag is passed `=true` by `.github/workflows/ci.yml` and `scripts/update-local.sh` so the deployed artefact actually carries the feature that was asked for; the `ARG` default in `ui/Dockerfile` remains `false`, so a plain `docker build` still ships dark. That deviation is deliberate and disclosed rather than silent, justified by the change being wholly presentational with no server surface and by all five Definition-of-Done gates running inside this same story. Four limitations were reviewed and accepted rather than fixed, and each is a trap for the next person in this file rather than a defect today. **A flag-off build still ships the lens code** — building `ui/` with the flag `true`, `false` and unset emitted an identical 198,662-byte bundle with `eop-card-lens` present in all three, so the flag controls whether the lens renders, never whether it is compiled, and nothing confidential may ever be placed behind a front-end flag. **The drag ghost in `GameScreen.tsx` duplicates `CardBody`'s rendering and hardcodes `'80px'`/`'120px'`/`'6px'` rather than the `HAND_CARD_*` constants**, so a change to card dimensions or card body structure must be made in both places — the exact drift `CardBody` was extracted to prevent, left in place only because the ghost cannot itself be magnified. **The `@media (max-width: 768px)` rule shrinking `.eop-card` to 60×90 in `GameScreen.css` is dead**, because `CardFace` sets the same properties inline and inline wins — it predates this story, but the lens geometry is now pinned to 80×120, so any future responsive-sizing work must move the CSS rule and the lens constants together. **The 100 px lens overflows the 80 px card by 10 px on each side** and the card container has no `overflow: hidden`, so a lens visually overlaps its neighbours in a dense hand — the current design accepts that in exchange for showing context around the magnified point, and a future story should decide deliberately whether to clip it.

- **The OpenAPI contract is now a build gate — the generated springdoc document is compared against the hand-authored file (EOP-95, ADR-054).** EOP-72 changed observable dealing behaviour without amending `docs/api/openapi.yml`, which went on documenting the full deal correctly while the implementation drifted. It went undetected for a whole story with a green build, found by manual audit (EOP-83 divergence 1); EOP-92 resolved it in favour of the spec. Three documents were simultaneously stale: the OpenAPI spec, the ADR index and `C4-Diagrams.md`. The new `OpenApiContractDriftTest` compares the generated `/v3/api-docs` against the hand-authored file on two axes: the path+method operation set, and the declared response status codes per operation. Four tests, both axes checked in **both directions**, and **no allow-list** — the whole surface Spring serves is compared, including `GET /health`, which had to be authored into the contract as part of this story because it was served and undocumented. **Making the annotated surface truthful was the bulk of the work, and it exposed a standing violation of `.opencode/rules/api-design.md`:** `SessionController` carried `@Operation` on all five methods and `@ApiResponse` on none, so springdoc reported a bare `200` for every one of them — including `POST /api/v1/sessions`, which actually returns `201` — and every GET route across `CardController`, `TrickController`, `ScoreController` and `GameOverController` was missing the `429` that ADR-051's read limiter can produce. Each added status was traced to the code that emits it rather than added to make the gate green: the `429` on `/events` is the per-session subscriber cap (ADR-034), not the read limiter, which excludes that path; the `429` on `POST /{joinCode}/players` is the join-attempt limiter inside the use case; `503` on session creation is join-code exhaustion. **The one status the application cannot produce is declared as a required exception rather than excluded:** authored `413` on `POST /api/v1/sessions` is enforced by Caddy's `request_body { max_size 16KB }`, so no truthful `@ApiResponse` can exist for it. That list is not an escape hatch and fails in both directions, in the manner of `tools/supply-chain/accepted-advisories.json` — a fourth test fails if an entry stops being necessary, and a deliberately dishonest entry was measured to produce **two** failures rather than none, because subtracting a status the code does declare then makes the status comparison itself fail. **The gate does NOT catch prose drift, so it would not have caught EOP-72, the very defect that prompted the story** — no path, no method and no status moved when the dealing behaviour changed. It also does not compare schemas or properties (springdoc and the authored file legitimately differ on `$ref` naming, nullability, examples and descriptions, and a noisy gate gets deleted rather than obeyed), never checks that a documented status is *reachable*, and sees nothing the proxy layer contributes beyond the one declared `413`. Options (a) CI requiring a commit that touches a controller to also touch `openapi.yml` and (c) a PR-template checklist were both rejected — the first trains whitespace edits to the contract, the second is the "resolved by a promise" outcome the ticket explicitly forbade, and three documents were already missed by human attention on this very defect.

- **Javadoc is now a build gate on every public member, enforced by two tools because neither can do it alone (EOP-120).** `src/main/java` already carried Javadoc in 157 files, but nothing checked it: `pom.xml` had no `maven-javadoc-plugin` at all, and `checkstyle.xml`'s Javadoc modules were deliberately lax — `MissingJavadocMethod` used `minLineCount=10`, so any public method under ten lines needed no comment whatsoever, while `JavadocMethod` set both `allowMissingParamTags` and `allowMissingReturnTag` to `true`, so a documented method could omit every `@param` and `@return` and still pass. Enforcement is now split along the line each tool can actually police. **Checkstyle owns *missing*:** `minLineCount=-1`, `allowMissingPropertyJavadoc=false`, both `allowMissing*Tags=false`, and a new `MissingJavadocType` module, every one pinned to `scope=public`. **The new `maven-javadoc-plugin` 3.11.2 owns *malformed*:** bound to `verify` as `javadoc-no-fork` (the plain `javadoc` goal forks a `generate-sources` lifecycle, which inside a `verify` binding re-runs earlier phases) with `show=public`, `failOnError=true`, `failOnWarnings=true` and **`doclint=all,-missing`** — it catches broken `{@link}` targets, malformed tags and bad HTML, which Checkstyle cannot see. **The `-missing` is the load-bearing part and the `pom.xml` comment says so at length,** because the obvious "fix" of writing `doclint=all` reinstates a gate that cannot be satisfied: doclint ignores javadoc's `-public` flag when hunting absent comments and demands one on every *private* field, which here means ~50 private fields on the domain exception classes that are not public API. Nor can it be scoped — `doclint=all/protected` fails outright with `error: Access qualifiers not permitted for -Xdoclint arguments`, because javadoc, unlike javac, forbids the access qualifier on `-Xdoclint` when visibility is already set by its own flag. **What the tightening actually surfaced is the useful measurement here: one violation.** A single undocumented constructor (`TooManySubscribersException`) plus four genuine public-API gaps (`ScoredPlay.components()` uncommented; `LeaderboardResult`'s record components needing their `@param` tags on the *class* Javadoc, not the compact constructor; `TrickRepository.openTrick`'s `@param occurredAt`) and two hard doclint errors (an entity linking outwards to `SessionRepository`, now named in `{@code}` prose because an entity may not import the use-case layer; a `{@value}` on `EMITTER_TIMEOUT_MILLIS`, which is computed by `TimeUnit.MINUTES.toMillis(10)` and so is not a compile-time constant). Five reflectively-instantiated classes — `Main`, `SweepProperties`, `UseCaseConfiguration`, `GlobalExceptionHandler`, `HibernateUuidV7IdentifierGenerator` — gained explicit non-private no-arg constructors written out solely so they could be documented. **`src/test/java` is deliberately out of scope** for both tools, and `package-info.java` files remain absent in all eight packages: packages are not public members, and adding `MissingJavadocPackage` is left as a separate proposal rather than smuggled in here. Evidence: `./mvnw clean verify` exits 0 with 1256 tests passing, `javadoc:3.11.2:javadoc-no-fork (verify-javadoc)` executing in the `verify` phase, and `0 Checkstyle violations`. **Two limits of that evidence are recorded in the `pom.xml` comment rather than left to be rediscovered.** On a warm `target/` the goal can log `Skipping javadoc generation, everything is up to date.`, and its presence in a log is **not** proof it re-analysed your edit: the plugin's staleness record holds 117 paths of which 92 are `.jar` and **zero** are `.java`, because its `StaleHelper.walk` uses a non-recursive `Files.newDirectoryStream`, so a comment-only change need not invalidate it. What makes the gate sound is not local behaviour but CI: `.github/workflows/ci.yml` runs on a fresh runner with no `target/`, so every merge is gated on a full analysis. Locally, run `./mvnw clean verify` if you only touched comments. A related failure is worth knowing about, though it is now recorded as unexplained rather than solved: a warm `./mvnw verify` was once observed failing with `Unable to compute stale date: target/reports/apidocs/options` on an entirely correct tree. An earlier revision of this entry claimed `debug=true` fixed it and that removing it would reinstate the failure; a controlled experiment during this story's own gate round falsified that, exiting 0 five times with `debug` removed, so the causal claim is retracted and `clean` is the workaround if you meet it. `debug=true` is kept on the narrower true ground that retaining `javadoc.sh`, `options` and `packages` makes the flags that actually reached the tool auditable. The staleness check *both states and reads* `@`-prefixed argument files, and never `stat`s them — the record *both* names `@options` and `@packages` as bare argument lines *and* carries their expanded contents, and `options` is rewritten at the start of every invocation. **`maven-enforcer-plugin` is also now pinned** (to `3.6.3`, the version already resolving, so no behaviour change): it was the only declared plugin with no version of ours. A later round established it was never floating: `spring-boot-dependencies:4.1.0` managed it at 3.6.3 already, so the pin buys deliberate control rather than closing a hole, and the flip side is that a Boot bump to enforcer no longer reaches this build. `spring-boot-maven-plugin` still carries no version here and takes 4.1.0 from the parent, so this file does not pin all six. And the plugin sits outside `tools/supply-chain/`, which audits the seven OpenCode npm plugins only — there is no **enforced** verification at the Maven layer (Maven compares a `.sha1` and warns, never fails), so this plugin executes in the build JVM on the same terms as the five other declared plugins (`spring-boot`, `checkstyle`, `spotbugs`, `jacoco`, `enforcer`) — liquibase is not among them, it runs during tests as an application dependency, and is parent-managed at 5.0.3 without a lifecycle binding. Measured from the effective pom, the layer is larger than those six: fourteen bound plugin entries and thirty-one managed ones. That is accepted knowingly, which is why the version is pinned exactly and moving the pin counts as a supply-chain change.

- **`@tarquinen/opencode-smart-title@0.1.7` — automatic session titles, and an advisory allowlist to admit it (EOP-000, Blueprint §12.9).** The plugin regenerates the OpenCode session title from the conversation whenever the session goes idle. Pinned as the seventh entry in the `plugin` array of `.opencode/opencode.json`, configured by the new committed `.opencode/smart-title.jsonc` (`updateThreshold: 3`, and an explicit `model` literal because the plugin's own fallback tables omit `amazon-bedrock` entirely — without it the selector throws, the error is swallowed, and the plugin is inert with no symptom). Two supply-chain consequences were recorded rather than papered over. First, the plugin pulls `ai@^5.0.98 → @ai-sdk/gateway@2.0.137 → @ai-sdk/provider-utils@3.0.32 → undici ^5.29.0`, which reports three **high** advisories (`GHSA-vrm6-8vpv-qv8q`, `GHSA-v9p9-hfj2-hcw8`, `GHSA-vxpw-j846-p89q`) with no in-range fix available, all three being defects in undici's WebSocket *client* — code the only consumer never reaches, because it destructures `{ Agent, fetch }` and because the `require("undici")` sits behind an `isNodeRuntime()` guard that is false under Bun. Rather than accept a permanently red `supply-chain` job, `tools/supply-chain/audit-plugins.sh` gained a second baseline, `tools/supply-chain/accepted-advisories.json`: an allowlist of advisory IDs each carrying its traced reachability argument, which fails in **both** directions — an unlisted high/critical fails the job, and so does a listed advisory that is no longer reported, so a stale entry must be deleted rather than left to rot. Both negative paths were exercised against the shipped gate code. Second, `@tarquinen/opencode-auth-provider` can `bun add --force --exact @ai-sdk/amazon-bedrock@latest` at runtime on the first Bedrock title call, which is the one hole in this repository's exact-pinning guarantee and one the audit script cannot see; accepted and documented in Blueprint §12.9, `SETUP.md` and the baseline note. The same npm account (`tarquinen`) now publishes two of the seven pins, so one account handoff would reach two roster entries while the per-package maintainer check reported them as independent findings. The Blueprint plugin preamble, which had claimed "eight plugins" while the roster held seven, was replaced by a breakdown that can be counted from the two files it names.

- **`scripts/update-local.sh` — one-command local stack refresh (EOP-76).** Rebuilds both `eop-threat-medeling:local` and `eop-ui:local` from the current working tree, then restarts `eop-app` and `eop-caddy` with `--force-recreate` while leaving `eop-postgres` untouched (data is preserved). Eliminates the class of bug where the running stack predates recent merges — the root cause of the Aug 17 incident where a stale app image disagreed with the 68-card database and silently blocked the opening drag. The script is self-documenting (comment block explains the gap and the AWS obsolescence condition), passes `shellcheck`, and has the executable bit set. It is a temporary convenience: once the CD pipeline deploys to AWS on every merge, the local stack is no longer the primary test target and this script can be deleted.


- **Real card images in the Game Screen (EOP-66, flag removed by EOP-74).** 68 playable PNG card images (© 2009 Microsoft Corporation, CC-BY-3.0-US) are bundled under `ui/src/assets/cards/{Suit}/` via Vite's `import.meta.glob`. `CardFace`, the trick zone, and the drag ghost all render the real card artwork instead of plain coloured rectangles. Face-down cards (other players' hands) remain plain rectangles. The `cardImagePath(suit, rank)` helper maps suit/rank enum values to bundled PNG URLs and returns `null` for non-existent combinations (e.g. TWO of Tampering, any ACE). All `<img>` elements carry descriptive `alt` attributes (`"{rankSymbol} of {suit}"`) for WCAG 2.2 AA compliance. The 68 card PNGs (~6.7 MB) are always bundled into `dist/` — Vite processes `import.meta.glob` at parse time and cannot dead-code-eliminate it (ADR-037).

- **`VITE_CARD_IMAGES_ENABLED` feature flag removed — card images now always on (EOP-74).** The `VITE_CARD_IMAGES_ENABLED` build-time flag introduced in EOP-66 has been removed per `.opencode/rules/feature-flags.md` (flags are removed once the feature is stable, one release after full rollout). `cardImagePath()` no longer checks the flag and always returns the image URL for valid suit/rank combinations. The `cardImagesEnabled` constant has been removed from `CardFace` and `GameScreen`. `VITE_CARD_IMAGES_ENABLED` has been removed from `ui/src/vite-env.d.ts` and `ui/.env.example`. ADR-037 Consequences has been amended to document the `import.meta.glob` bundle-size behaviour.

- **Equal hands: deck truncated to discard remainder, lowest Tampering card guaranteed dealt (EOP-72, ADR-023 amended).** `Hands.deal()` now truncates the deck to `floor(D / n) × n` cards before dealing, so every seat receives exactly `floor(D / n)` cards and no seat holds more than another. The remainder is discarded. To preserve the opening-lead rule, the algorithm swaps the lowest-ranked Tampering card into the kept portion if it would otherwise be discarded. With the 74-card deck: 3 players → 24 each (72 dealt, 2 discarded); 4 → 18 each (72 dealt, 2 discarded); 5 → 14 each (70 dealt, 4 discarded); 6 → 12 each (72 dealt, 2 discarded). ADR-023 Decision §1 is superseded by an amendment dated 2026-08-17.

- **`eop.features.trick-play` permanently enabled; deal-cards wired in UI (EOP-70, ADR-040).** All three predecessor gates are now discharged — EOP-48 (commit `34d30d7`), EOP-15 (ADR-032), and ADR-026 (option 4 chosen: audit logging at the HTTP boundary in `TrickController`). `src/main/resources/application.yml` now sets `trick-play: true` as the permanent default. The three game-affecting write endpoints (`POST /{sessionId}/deal`, `POST /{sessionId}/plays`, `POST /{sessionId}/tricks/current/resolve`) each emit one `INFO` audit line naming the session UUID and the first 8 characters of the actor's token; card identities are never logged (CWE-117 and hidden-information constraints). On the UI side: `LobbyScreen` now calls `POST /deal` immediately after `POST /start` succeeds; `canStartGame` threshold raised from 2 to 3 to match `GameSession.MINIMUM_PLAYERS_TO_START`; `GameScreen.refreshGameState` now fetches `GET /sessions/{id}` first and treats a 404/403 there as session-end, while a 409 from `/hand` or `/tricks/current` (i.e. `HandNotDealtException`) shows a "Waiting for cards to be dealt…" state rather than navigating home. `ui/.env.example` documents `VITE_GAME_SCREEN_ENABLED` (note: `VITE_LOBBY_UI_ENABLED` was also documented here at the time of EOP-70 but was subsequently removed by EOP-77). ADR-040 records the flag-on decision; ADR-026 is amended to Accepted; ADR-013 and ADR-027 are amended to reflect the new flag position.

- **Game-over leaderboard and new-game reset (EOP-65, ADR-039).** When the last trick resolves, `ResolveTrickUseCase` calls `PersistGameResultUseCase` (best-effort, behind `Optional`) to write a `game_result` row and per-player `game_result_player` rows. `GET /api/v1/sessions/{sessionId}/leaderboard` returns the final standings (competition-ranked, STRIDE breakdown per player derived from trick history per ADR-030) for any seated player once the session is COMPLETED. `POST /api/v1/sessions/{sessionId}/new-game` (facilitator only) clears all tricks and hands, resets the session to IN_PROGRESS, and re-deals the 74-card deck to the same seats. Both routes are gated on `eop.features.game-over` (default `false`). The `GameOverScreen` React component (GOV.UK table, WCAG 2.2 AA) is wired to the `game-completed` SSE event in `GameScreen`. ADR-039 documents the non-atomic reset sequence and the accepted trade-off. ADR-032 is amended to reference ADR-039.

- **`GameScreen` component: virtual table layout, pointer-event drag-and-drop card play, SSE doorbell refresh, and trick-winner announcement (EOP-60, ADR-038, ADR-037).** The game screen renders the current player's hand face-up at the bottom of a virtual table, other players' hands face-down around the perimeter, and a central trick zone where cards are played. Cards are played by drag-and-drop (Pointer Events — mouse, touch and pen) or by click-to-select + "Play selected card" button (keyboard and screen-reader path, WCAG 2.2 AA compliant: SC 2.4.7 Focus Visible, SC 1.4.11 Non-text Contrast, SC 2.5.7 Dragging Movements). Out-of-turn cards are `aria-disabled`; an `aria-live` region announces whose turn it is. When a trick is complete, a `role="alert"` winner banner names the winner and their card; the winner sees a "Start next trick" button; the banner auto-dismisses after 5 seconds. State is re-fetched on every SSE doorbell event (ADR-014 doorbell pattern — no payload, re-read authoritative state). The feature is behind the `VITE_GAME_SCREEN_ENABLED` build-time flag (ADR-037), off by default. ADR-038 records the pointer-event DnD decision and the keyboard-path contract. Runtime-view sequence 10 documents the browser play-a-card round trip.

- **Feature flag `eop.features.session-lifecycle` permanently enabled (EOP-25, ADR-013).** All six gate stories (EOP-17, EOP-18, EOP-19, EOP-20, EOP-21, EOP-22) and the additional gate EOP-26 are Done. `src/main/resources/application.yml` now sets `session-lifecycle: true` as the permanent default. This is a reviewed source change, not an environment override — the audit trail is the commit history. The flag and its `@ConditionalOnProperty` guards remain in place until the feature is confirmed stable; removal is a separate story. As a result, the five session-lifecycle routes (`POST /api/v1/sessions`, `POST /{joinCode}/players`, `GET /{sessionId}`, `POST /{sessionId}/start`, `GET /{sessionId}/events`) and the `ExpiredSessionSweepScheduler` are now active in the default configuration. `eop.features.trick-play` remains `false`; the lobby-only surface is what shipped. No `EOP_FEATURES_SESSION_LIFECYCLE` entry is needed in `compose.app.yml`.

- **Session expiry and abandoned-session sweep (EOP-22, ADR-036).** Every session now carries an `expires_at` timestamp set to 24 hours after the lobby opens (`GameSession.SESSION_TTL`). `ResolvePlayerUseCase` rejects any `resolve-player` call against an expired session with `403 Forbidden` (`SessionExpiredException`), regardless of the feature flag position — the guard is a read-only check and is safe to run unconditionally. A scheduled sweep (`SweepExpiredSessionsUseCase` + `ExpiredSessionSweepScheduler`) runs hourly (configurable via `eop.sweep.interval-ms`) and deletes expired sessions along with their players, hands, tricks and plays (via `ON DELETE CASCADE`); the sweep is gated on `eop.features.session-lifecycle` and was absent in the default configuration until EOP-25 (2026-08-16) flipped the flag on permanently. Liquibase changeset `006` adds the `expires_at` column and an index on it. `Clock` is now injected into `ResolvePlayerUseCase` (previously called `Instant.now()` directly). The sweep policy lives in `SweepExpiredSessionsUseCase` (use-case layer); `ExpiredSessionSweepScheduler` is a thin adapter in `adapter/scheduling/`. `SweepProperties` (`@ConfigurationProperties(prefix = "eop.sweep")`) binds and validates the sweep interval and initial delay. This story reverses ADR-019's "no session expiry" consequence; the rationale is in ADR-036.

- **TLS and security response headers at Caddy (EOP-21, ADR-035).** `tls internal` in the Caddyfile issues a self-signed certificate from Caddy's local CA, so the bearer token no longer travels in plaintext. The site address is `localhost:8080` (hostname required for Caddy to issue a leaf certificate); `auto_https disable_redirects` replaces the previous `auto_https off` so that certificate management is not suppressed. `default_sni localhost` is added to the global block so that connections arriving with **no SNI at all** (RFC 6066 forbids IP literals in SNI, so a browser connecting to an IP URL sends no SNI) fall back to the `localhost` certificate rather than aborting the handshake; note that the HTTP routing is gated on `host: localhost`, so such connections receive an empty 200 with no security headers — the demo is localhost-only with `tls internal` and a public deployment requires a real certificate. The host port mapping changes from `80:8080` to `443:8080`; the application is now reached at `https://localhost/`. Five security response headers are applied globally to all responses — including Caddy-generated error responses via a `handle_errors` block — and the `Server` header is removed, so that `Server: Caddy` is absent on 502, 404 and 413 responses as well as on success paths: `Content-Security-Policy` (`default-src 'self'; object-src 'none'; base-uri 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none'; form-action 'none'`), `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`, `Strict-Transport-Security` (`max-age=63072000; includeSubDomains`), and `Permissions-Policy` disabling camera, microphone, geolocation and payment. HSTS is safe to set because TLS is now confirmed live; note that HSTS on `localhost` is port-agnostic and pins all `http://localhost:*` to HTTPS for two years in the browser — see ADR-035 Consequences. The container `HEALTHCHECK` is updated to probe over HTTPS (`https://localhost:8080/index.html --no-check-certificate`). A named Docker volume (`eop_caddy_data`) persists Caddy's local CA across container recreates, so that trusting the root once is sufficient for the lifetime of the stack. ADR-035 formalises the token-storage constraint that was implicit in ADR-015: the token must never be stored in `localStorage`; `sessionStorage` remains the accepted store per ADR-015's original decision. ADR-015 is amended to cross-reference ADR-035 and to record that the plaintext-transport consequence is now closed. ADR-012 and ADR-017 are amended to remove stale "TLS is still the fix" and "port 80" claims. `README.md`, `.env.example`, `test/k6/run.sh`, `test/k6/health-check.js` and `.github/workflows/ci.yml` are updated to reference `https://localhost` and port 443. `infra/variables.tf`, `infra/network.tf`, `infra/main.tf` and `infra/outputs.tf` are updated to reflect the port change from 80 to 443.

Working code and tooling that exists in the repository today.

- Walking Skeleton: Spring Boot 4.1.0 + Java 21 + `GET /health` (ADR-002)
- Continuous integration: GitHub Actions running `./mvnw verify` on push and PR, uploading the built artifact, then building the container image, smoke testing it against a real PostgreSQL and publishing it to GHCR on `main`
- Front end: React + TypeScript + Vite under `ui/` with GOV.UK Design System CSS (ADR-009). One page — the application shell plus the card catalogue fetched live from the API. Both calls to action are visibly disabled, because a button that looks live and does nothing is worse than one that admits it
- Single-origin serving: Caddy serves the built front end and reverse-proxies `/api/*` and `/health` to the application on the same origin (ADR-017). The application container publishes no host port at all, so there is no cross-origin request anywhere and therefore no CORS configuration to maintain
- Front-end quality gates: a dedicated `ui` CI job runs type checking, linting, unit tests and the production build as four separate steps. It is deliberately separate from the Java `build` job — the single required status check protecting `main` stays Java-only and fast, and a front-end failure reports as a front-end failure
- Container images: multi-stage `Dockerfile` producing a non-root JRE image and `ui/Dockerfile` producing a Caddy image carrying the built front end, `compose.app.yml` running both alongside containerised PostgreSQL, published to `ghcr.io` by CI with no repository secrets (ADR-012, ADR-017)
- Deployment infrastructure: Terraform under `infra/` describing a single `t3.small` EC2 instance in a dedicated VPC with an Elastic IP and a separate encrypted EBS data volume (ADR-012).
