Validated with `terraform validate`; no `apply` has run yet because no AWS account has been provisioned, and cloud deployment is deferred in favour of running locally (see the ADR-012 amendment)
- Local container runtime: Colima provides a Docker-compatible daemon from Homebrew formulae with no administrator rights (ADR-016). The whole stack — application and PostgreSQL — now runs on a developer machine with the same `docker compose -f compose.app.yml up -d` that CI and the EC2 bootstrap script use
- Build quality gates: Checkstyle (`checkstyle.xml`), SpotBugs, JaCoCo 80% instruction coverage, Enforcer (ADR-006)
- Database migrations: Liquibase with a master XML changelog, H2 in the default profile, PostgreSQL in `prod` (ADR-008). The first migration creates the `card` table; the second replaces the placeholder deck with the real one
- Card catalogue: `GET /api/v1/cards` and `GET /api/v1/cards/{cardId}` serve the deck as read-only reference data (EOP-6)
- The real Elevation of Privilege deck: 74 cards matching the printed game — Tampering starts at rank 3, Elevation of Privilege starts at rank 5 — transcribed from Microsoft's published deck, trimmed to the printed card count by EOP-69, and seeded by additive data-only migrations (EOP-13, EOP-69). © 2009 Microsoft Corporation, licensed CC-BY-3.0 US — attribution is the only obligation the licence imposes and it is discharged in the running application, not merely in the repository.
- Trick-taking rules as pure domain types: `Hand`, `Hands`, `Trick` and `TrickPlay` under `org.maglez.eop.entity`, with zero framework imports, carrying the whole rule set the game turns on — dealing, following suit, turn order, and who takes the trick (EOP-14, ADR-023). Four decisions are worth naming. **The opening lead is derived, never written down:** the seat holding the lowest-ranked Tampering card *actually dealt* leads, because the 74-card deck starts on the three of Tampering — a literal `3` anywhere in this code would be a defect against any future deck variant that starts differently, so `Hands.openingLeaderSeat()` computes it and `NoTamperingCardDealtException` covers the one case the rule cannot answer rather than falling back to an arbitrary leader. **Every card is dealt**, with the remainder going to the lowest seats, so at 74 cards all four supported table sizes (3, 4, 5 and 6 players) produce unequal hands and a short final trick; setting the remainder aside instead would have been tidier but can orphan the card the opening-lead rule needs, turning a derived rule into a hidden fallback reached only at certain player counts. That in turn makes a trick *one card from each seat that still holds cards* rather than a fixed count, so turn order is "the next seat clockwise still holding a card" and not the leader's seat plus the number of cards played — the simpler formula is correct for every trick except the last one at every player count, which is why ADR-019 now carries a dated amendment where an implementer would otherwise copy it. **A card played with no threat linked is a legal, ordinary play:** it still competes for the trick and simply scores nothing, so `TrickPlay.threatLinked` is a fact about a play and not an error, and a linked threat naming no component is accepted too — a facilitation tool that refuses a play in a live session because a text box is empty gets in the way of the conversation it exists to support. **A card named in a request is resolved out of the hand before any rule is applied to it,** and there is one guarded way in: `Trick.acceptPlay` checks the turn, resolves the card, checks follow-suit against the *resolved* suit and appends the play, with `Trick.play` reduced to package-private so nothing outside the domain can reach the unguarded mutator. A card is named by identifier, so its suit and rank travel as the caller's *claim*; checking possession by identifier and then reading the suit off the submitted object let a holder of the two of Tampering submit that identifier labelled ace of Elevation of Privilege, pass possession, escape follow-suit because the claimed suit was trump, and take the trick with a card that was never dealt. Three public checks a caller was merely *documented* to call in the right order were two optional guards in front of an open mutator, not defence in depth. **The acting seat is a parameter in its own right and is never read from the payload:** `acceptPlay(int actingSeat, TrickPlay candidate, Hands hands)` takes the seat the caller has authenticated, refuses the play with `NotYourSeatException` if it describes a different seat, and then *derives* both the hand and the set of seats still holding cards from `hands` rather than accepting either. An earlier revision took the seat from the submitted play and cross-checked only the hand against the player, which is a two-way check on a three-way fact — seat, hand and player — and `Hand` deliberately carries no seat number, so the binding could not be made from those arguments at all. Because the session state already publishes every player's identifier *and* seat to every other player, a player really sitting at seat 2 could submit its own identifier with someone else's seat number and its own hand: the play was accepted out of turn, the victim's seat was marked as having played so they were locked out of the trick for good, and the attacker could then play a second card from the same hand at its real seat and take the trick. The player identifier remains the one caller-supplied claim, and it is checked against the seat's occupant rather than believed. **The seat is settled before anything else is looked at** — whether it is a seat at this table at all, whether the play claims that seat, and whether the player the play names is the one occupying it — because all three are answered entirely from facts the requester already sent, so a refusal discloses nothing it did not already know. A play naming a seat's non-occupant is refused with `PlayerMismatchException`, its own type rather than a bare `IllegalArgumentException`, so the boundary can tell impersonation from a malformed argument without matching on message text and can carry both player identifiers as fields instead of interpolating them into a message a caller reads. Turn order comes next, before the card is looked at, so a player who is not to play learns nothing about their own cards from the refusal, and `Trick.nextLeaderSeat(seatsHoldingCards)` passes the lead on when the winner has run out of cards — a winner's seat handed back unconditionally would open a trick nobody could play into, which is reachable at every player count and would have stopped the game with no exception to log. Free text a player types is bounded and refused if it carries control characters or bidirectional overrides, because a NUL byte cannot be stored in a PostgreSQL `text` column, CR and LF in text that reaches a log let a player forge log lines, and a bidi override lets a component name display differently from the one stored. On rendering: `Hand`, `Hands` and `TrickPlay` print only counts, because knowing an opponent's cards is knowing what they can and cannot follow with, and the default rendering would leak a hand — or a player's notes — into any log line that interpolated it. `Card.toString` drops its threat prompt as bulk rather than as a secret; the deck is published, so what is confidential is the *association* between a player and an unplayed card, and that guarantee lives in the redacted containers and in `Hand.cards()` being documented as confidential rather than in `Card` itself. A `Trick` also refuses, at construction, a play list in which one player holds two seats or two plays share an identifier, and refuses plays that do not run clockwise from the leading seat — the first of those is the stored form of the seat-impersonation defect above, and all three are checks a trick can make from the plays alone. What it cannot check is follow-suit, because that is a question about a hand and a trick holds no hands, so `Trick.reconstitute` is documented as **not** a validating gate for the rules of play and Slice B's persistence adapter must be trusted to store only what `acceptPlay` produced. `Hands.deal` refuses two seat assignments sharing a seat order, which previously collapsed silently on insertion and left one seat holding two round-robin shares of the deck. `docs/requirements/PRD-eop-card-game.md` §3.3 now carries dated deviation notes against the two sentences the implemented rules depart from — "one time around the table" and "the winner of a trick leads the next trick" — each linking ADR-023, because both are the shipped instruction card's wording and both are right for a game in which every hand empties together. All six of the story's domain refusals are now mapped to problem details and unit-tested, as the error-handling rules require of every known exception: a play claiming a seat its requester does not occupy and a play naming a player who does not hold that seat are both **403**, being impersonation rather than malformed input; a play out of turn is **409**; a play that breaks follow-suit, or names a card the hand does not hold, is **422**, because every field parsed and the refusal came from a rule of the game. Two of those mappings deliberately do not echo the exception's message: the card refusal reports only the card identifier and not the identifier of the hand, and the player-mismatch refusal returns a fixed sentence, its message having been reduced to name a seat and nothing else so that logging it cannot leak either player. A deck with no Tampering card is the one **500** here, because the deck is seeded reference data and every valid deck contains the suit. Nothing is wired up yet — no schema, no endpoint, no dealing on session start — and scoring is EOP-15
- Storage for the trick-taking domain: migration `004-trick-play-schema.xml` creates `hand`, `hand_card`, `trick`, `trick_play` and `trick_play_component`, and adds `game_session.current_leader_seat` (EOP-14, ADR-023). It also adds `uq_player_id_seat` to `player`, a table it does not create. The schema lands before the JPA entities and adapters deliberately, because `ddl-auto: validate` fails startup on an entity with no matching table. Six decisions are worth naming. **`trick_play.components` becomes a child table with an explicit `ordinal`, and the 20-element cap is enforced three times over** — once in the domain constructor, once in storage as `PRIMARY KEY (trick_play_id, ordinal)` plus `CHECK (ordinal >= 0 AND ordinal <= 19)`, and once at the boundary in the slice that adds the request DTO. That is defence in depth rather than duplication: the domain stops bad input, the constraints stop a bad write from a future adapter bug or a hand-run statement. `(trick_play_id, component_name)` is deliberately *not* unique, because the domain accepts repeated component names and a stricter constraint would turn a legal play into a 500. **`hand_card` gets no ordinal**, which looks inconsistent next to the components table until you notice that `Hand` sorts its cards in its own constructor so that two hands holding the same cards compare equal whichever order they arrived in — dealing order carries no meaning, whereas the order a facilitator typed their component names in does, so one is canonicalised and the other preserved as typed. **`trick` gets no `led_suit` column**, because `Trick.ledSuit()` derives it from the first play and a stored copy would be a second authority no write path populates. **The three `trick_play` unique constraints carry the invariants through a reload**, so they survive rather than living only in whichever entity happened to be in memory — but only one of them is the stored form of a Slice A security defect. `(trick_id, card_id)` closes card forgery within a trick. `(trick_id, player_id)` stops one player occupying two seats in a trick, which is a *different* exploit from the seat impersonation Slice A had to fix twice, and an earlier draft of this entry claimed otherwise. **Seat impersonation is closed instead by binding a stored seat to the seat its player actually holds:** `uq_player_id_seat` on `player (id, seat_order)` gives a composite foreign key something to reference, and `fk_hand_player_seat` and `fk_trick_play_player_seat` then make a hand or a play at a seat its player does not hold structurally unrepresentable, rather than merely unlikely. That reverses a deferral taken earlier the same day: the seat and cross-session protections were argued as one problem and a sound objection to the expensive half — denormalising `game_session_id`, a copy that can disagree with its parent — was allowed to carry the cheap half with it, when in fact `seat_order` was already on both tables and the binding needed one unique constraint that adds no invariant of its own. The single-column keys to `player(id)` are subsumed by the composite ones rather than omitted, and that subsumption holds only while both key columns stay `NOT NULL`. **What is deliberately still not enforced is session scoping**, and the honest consequence is that the composite key proves a player holds that seat *somewhere*, not that they are at this trick's table — seats are numbered from zero in every session, so a player at seat 2 in another session still satisfies it and can still take this session's seat-2 occupant out of play. Narrowed to cross-session attackers, then, not closed; it is owned by Slice C's play use case, which resolves the acting player from the identity token, and ADR-023 records both the reversal and what remains. **Foreign keys were missing on `trick_play.card_id` and `trick.winner_play_id`** and are now present; an unconstrained `card_id` in particular left card forgery re-openable one layer below the code that had just been hardened against it. `fk_trick_winner_play` is `ON DELETE SET NULL` rather than the `NO ACTION` first written, because measurement showed NO ACTION made a resolved trick undeletable outright — at the cost that deleting a winning play directly now silently unresolves its trick, which nothing but a comment currently prevents a later slice from doing. The concurrency test ADR-023 places in this slice rather than a later one — before the constraint exists there is nothing to assert against — drives one player racing itself through separate JDBC connections, which is the ordinary double-tap a real client produces; a second, sequential test uses two players from different sessions who both legitimately hold the same seat number, because that is the only construction left in which `uq_trick_play_trick_seat` is the sole constraint that can fire, and it doubles as the recorded proof of the cross-session gap above
- Range bounds on every seat and sequence column: migration `005-seat-and-sequence-bounds.xml` adds `chk_player_seat_order`, `chk_trick_sequence` and `chk_game_session_current_leader_seat` (EOP-14, ADR-023). This closes a gap found by measurement rather than by reading: against the merged Slice B schema, `player.seat_order = -7`, `trick.sequence = -5` and `game_session.current_leader_seat = 9999` were all *accepted* by the engine, while the domain refuses all three. Three points are worth naming. **One CHECK on `player.seat_order` bounds three tables**, because `fk_hand_player_seat` and `fk_trick_play_player_seat` bind `hand` and `trick_play` to `player (id, seat_order)` — so the seat range on the child tables is inherited, and the composite keys that carry it prove consistency with the parent but never validity, which is exactly why the parent needed a bound of its own. **`trick.sequence` gets a lower bound only**, because no upper bound is derivable — a three-player deal runs to `ceil(68 / 3)` = 23 tricks and no domain constant caps it — so `>= 1` is the whole of what is known and inventing a ceiling would reject legal play. **The bounds are pinned to `GameSession.MAXIMUM_PLAYERS` by a test that reads the changeset XML**, since Liquibase cannot read a Java constant and the literal `5` is a hand-copied counterpart: raising the player cap without editing the migration would leave storage stricter than the domain and turn a legal join into a 500. Each constraint is tested in both directions, because a test that only proves rejection would pass just as well against a CHECK that rejected every value, legal ones included. Adding a constraint to `player` from a later changelog file rather than editing `003` is the only lawful option — merged changesets are immutable (ADR-008) — and follows the precedent `004` set when it added `uq_player_id_seat` to the same table. Writing all three as raw `<sql>` is likewise forced: `addCheckConstraint` is a Liquibase Pro change type and is absent from the open-source `liquibase-core` this project depends on. Landing `005` also exposed a latent defect in Slice B's own round-trip test, which rolled back a hard-coded nine changesets to undo `004` — correct only while `004` was the last changelog file, since Liquibase counts backwards from the last change *applied*. It now derives the count from `DATABASECHANGELOG`, so `006` cannot re-break it
- An error vocabulary for trick play: seven domain exception types and their problem-detail mappings — `PlayerNotInSessionException` (404), `HandAlreadyDealtException`, `HandNotDealtException`, `TrickAlreadyOpenException`, `AlreadyPlayedInTrickException` and `CardAlreadyPlayedException` (409), and `WinningPlayNotInTrickException` (422) — landing ahead of the use cases that will throw them (EOP-14, ADR-023). Five decisions are worth naming. **A caller who is not in the session is answered exactly as a session that does not exist**, byte for byte and not merely with the same status: a 403 would confirm to a stranger holding a guessed or leaked identifier that it guessed correctly, and two 404s with different titles would be as good an oracle as a 403, so the test asserts equality of the two whole `ProblemDetail` bodies. The parity is held in place by the exception itself, which builds the same message as `SessionNotFoundException` for the same identifier and carries the session id **and nothing else** — a field naming the player, the seat or the reason would eventually be logged into a response, and a field that does not exist cannot be. This follows the fixed-body reasoning already written for an unknown join code, with the one deliberate difference that a join code is blanked because roughly thirty bits are guessable while a caller-supplied session identifier can be echoed back safely. **The candour argument that licenses naming a missing session is now scoped in the code that makes it**: the Javadoc on `SessionNotFoundException` and its handler argued from a 122-bit identifier, which is sound about whether a session exists and says nothing about who may see it, so both now cross-reference the membership answer rather than leaving the next reader to generalise a correct argument past its premise. **A winner from another trick is 422 and not 409**, because a conflict entitles the caller to retry once the state moves on and no state this application can reach makes a play from one trick the winner of another; it is also logged at warning level with both identifiers and returns neither, since ADR-023 records that this check will never have a storage constraint behind it — the composite key that would provide one collides with the rule that lets a winning play be deleted — so the log line is the only evidence that will ever exist. **The two conflicts on a trick are kept apart**: one refuses a second play from a seat, the other one card appearing twice whichever seat played it, and a test pins the titles as unequal so that collapsing them into a single "conflict" cannot pass. **The two states of the deal are kept apart on the same reasoning, and naming the second of them is what took a seventh type**: `HandNotDealtException` is the mirror of `HandAlreadyDealtException`, the two being opposite states of one column — `game_session.current_leader_seat` — and a vocabulary naming only one of them forces the other to be reported as something it is not. Before it existed, that branch of `TrickPlayRepositoryAdapter.sessionMoved` answered `SessionNotJoinableException` carrying `IN_PROGRESS`: the right status with an explanation that contradicted itself, telling a caller a session was not joinable while handing back the status saying it was in progress. @architecture-guardian found it in this slice's architecture review, and the fix was a type that can name the state rather than a different status code — the branch is reached by asking to open the first trick before the deal, which is an ordinary caller mistake rather than a broken invariant, hence a 409 and not a 500. The two 409 titles are deliberately unequal, and a test pins that they do not collapse into each other, because both are conflicts on one column and the title is the only thing saying which way it went. The translation table in ADR-023 was also one row short and is amended here: `uq_trick_session_sequence` is reachable by two requests racing to open the same trick, and without a name for it that race answered 500. What C1 cannot yet test is the status over the wire, because it adds no route — the handlers are proved as pure functions here, exactly as the existing mappings are, and the wire-level assertions arrive with the endpoints
- Persistence mappings for the trick-play tables: JPA entities for `hand`, `hand_card`, `trick`, `trick_play` and `trick_play_component`, plus the `current_leader_seat` column on the session entity (EOP-14, ADR-023). The point of the commit is not the eight new classes but what mapping them switches on. `spring.jpa.hibernate.ddl-auto` is `validate`, and until now that setting was nearly free: three tables were mapped, the five created by Slice B were not, and Hibernate validated nothing about them — so a mapping could disagree with the migrations indefinitely and only a query that happened to touch the wrong column would ever say so. Mapping them means every column and type on all eight tables is now checked at every context start, on H2 in the suite and on PostgreSQL in production. Because that check is silent when it passes, it gets a test of its own rather than being left as something the other integration tests catch by accident: `MappedSchemaValidationIntegrationTest` asserts the effective `ddl-auto` value, so switching it to `none` fails loudly instead of quietly removing a guard, and then issues a real bounded `SELECT` of every mapped column of every entity, which is a stronger check than startup validation and one that names the entity when it breaks. The test was confirmed non-vacuous by measurement — renaming a single mapped column to `seat_ordering` turned all nine cases red — because a schema test that cannot fail is worse than none. Three mapping decisions carry reasoning. **No collection mappings anywhere**, following the session entity's existing refusal of `@OneToMany`: a played card is one delete and a play is one insert, and a cascade or orphan-removal mapping would turn each into a diff of two collections, which matters here more than it did for seats because the statement issued has to be exactly the statement it looks like — the row lock taken before it is the concurrency guard. **The two link tables use `@IdClass` rather than `@EmbeddedId`**, since both have a composite primary key that genuinely is their identity and no surrogate to invent; `@IdClass` keeps the key columns as ordinary fields, so a query reads `deleteByHandIdAndCardId` instead of routing every predicate through an embedded object. **`current_leader_seat` is mapped on the row and deliberately nowhere else** — the domain `GameSession` gains nothing, because the current leader is a fact about the trick in progress and `Trick` already derives whose turn it is from its own plays, so a copy on the aggregate would be a second authority on it. It is stored because it is the compare-and-set witness the conditional writes name, it is `Integer` rather than `int` because null is meaningful and is how "no cards dealt yet" is stored, and it advances once per trick rather than once per play. A play reads it and takes the row lock without changing it
- Storage for dealt hands and played tricks: the `HandRepository` and `TrickRepository` ports, five Spring Data interfaces, one `TrickPlayRepositoryAdapter` implementing both, and three further conditional updates on the session row — `claimDeal`, `touchWhileLeaderSeatIs` and `advanceLeaderSeat` (EOP-14, ADR-020, ADR-023, ADR-024). Five decisions carry the design and govern the first half of this entry; what follows them records what shipped, and the argument that used to fill the rest now lives in an ADR of its own. **There is no `save`**: each write is narrow and conditional on the state the caller observed, because a blanket save would have to diff an immutable aggregate against storage to learn which rows to write and could not be conditional on anything — which is precisely the compare-and-set turn order depends on. **Both ports are implemented by one class**, because playing a card deletes a `hand_card` row and inserts a play and its components and those must commit together; the two decompositions that keep one adapter per port either admit that torn state or put `@Transactional` in the use-case package. *(Retracted — see ADR-024's amendment and the next entry: those are not the only two decompositions. A `@Transactional` method in the outer ring composes two adapters through the `REQUIRED` propagation they already carry, with no import inside `usecase`. One class per aggregate survives on ownership grounds, not on a false dichotomy.)* **Every write begins with a conditional update on the session row**, which is both the compare-and-set whose zero-rows answer means the world moved and the row lock taken in the order the ADR requires (session, then hands, then tricks). Zero rows is never success and never a server fault: one helper spends a single extra read on the already-failing path to answer that the session is missing, is not in progress, has already been dealt, has not been dealt at all, or that the caller's leader-seat snapshot is stale — the last being an out-of-turn play, since the leader seat is the value turn order is computed from, and a double resolution lands there too because the first resolution moved the seat. **Hands are read whole or not at all**, since `Hands.reconstitute` re-runs a cross-seat invariant no database constraint stands behind and a per-seat read would satisfy it vacuously, switching off its only enforcement; writes stay a one-seat delta. Plays are ordered by rotation from the trick's leader seat rather than by `played_at`, because the domain reads the led suit from the first play and a timestamp can tie. **The seat foreign keys are a backstop rather than the check**: the adapter reads the session's seats before inserting and refuses a wrong seat itself with 403, or an unknown player with the same 404 a missing session returns, and a key that fires anyway is logged at warning level with its constraint name and rethrown as a server fault, because it means a check above it was missed. Checking first is sound only because a seat never moves once seated — the column is mapped non-updatable — so unlike seat *allocation* there is no window to narrow. Each index maps to one exception, proved by driving the adapter through a live schema rather than a stub: `uq_hand_session_seat` becomes `HandAlreadyDealtException`, `uq_trick_session_sequence` `TrickAlreadyOpenException`, `uq_trick_play_trick_seat` and `uq_trick_play_trick_player` both `AlreadyPlayedInTrickException` — one player makes one play whichever index catches the second — and `uq_trick_play_trick_card` `CardAlreadyPlayedException`. Three details of that test are load-bearing: cards come from the seeded catalogue, because the domain's deck fixture mints synthetic identifiers `fk_hand_card_card` refuses; the class is not `@Transactional` and cleans nothing up, since the first violation marks a test-managed transaction rollback-only and would poison every later assertion, so each case mints its own identifiers and commits as production does; and two indexes must be reached deliberately, `uq_hand_session_seat` by nulling the leader seat *and* dealing fresh hand identifiers, because reusing them makes `saveAndFlush` merge and silently succeed, and `uq_trick_play_trick_card` by inserting a `hand_card` row by hand, since the adapter's own delete refuses the second play first. The negative controls are that a deal naming an unseated player, and one naming a seated player at somebody else's seat, each write *nothing*; and what the test cannot reach is recorded in it — the missing-card server fault, while the foreign keys make a referenced card undeletable, and both seat backstops, because the adapter refuses first, so a fired backstop's warning line is untested by construction. Two reads also turn out to throw `InvalidDataAccessApiUsageException` and not the JPA-specification `IllegalStateException` they appear to, because the adapter is a `@Repository` and Spring translates on the way out; both still fall to the catch-all 500, and the assertions pin that, since an `IllegalArgumentException` would render as a 400 and blame the caller for a fault of ours. Reading the leader seat revalidates the column against the domain's seat count, and a seat bound for a column is refused at the boundary rather than left to the CHECK; both guards are pinned as the pure functions they are, the read guard because it cannot be reached end to end at all — this slice's range constraint refuses the very row the guard exists to survive — and the write guard because only its negative limb is out of reach. The three range constraints carry preconditions that halt with the query to run instead of an engine error naming neither the row nor the reason. A security review rejected the slice for documentation claiming an authorisation this layer does not perform: it authorises nobody, none of its statements takes a player identifier, and the seat check is a check on the *row* rather than on the requester. [ADR-024](docs/adr/ADR-024-trick-play-persistence-boundary.md) now carries that decision, the argument behind it, and the obligation it puts on Slice C2 — authorise the requester first, in every use case, because no test here fails if you do not. This slice's documentation work landed with it: ADR-024 was created, and ADR-023, ADR-020, ADR-013, `docs/adr/README.md`, `docs/architecture/C4-Diagrams.md` and `docs/architecture/runtime-view.md` were corrected, because mapping the five tables falsified standing claims in three documents that the tables were unreachable — the containment is now the absence of a use case and a route rather than the absence of a mapping
- The use cases that drive trick play: `DealHandsUseCase`, `PlayCardUseCase` and `ResolveTrickUseCase`, plus the `DeckShuffler` port and its `SecureRandomDeckShuffler` implementation, a third `findWholeDeck()` method on `CardRepository`, and two new domain exceptions with their 409 mappings — all three use cases wired in `UseCaseConfiguration` behind `eop.features.trick-play`, off by default (EOP-14, ADR-025). Slice C1 shipped ports that could reach five trick-play tables and no bean that did; this is the slice that adds the beans, which is why the flag is a merge precondition rather than a nicety, and `TrickPlayDisabledIntegrationTest` asserts all three beans are *absent* with the flag off rather than asserting 404s, because a route does not exist yet and asserting absence pins the mechanism instead of a symptom. Nine decisions are worth naming. **Dealing is its own use case rather than a step in starting a session**, and this is a chosen seam rather than a forced one — an earlier version of this entry and of ADR-025 claimed it was forced, and an architecture review refuted the claim. What is true is that `recordDeal`'s conditional update requires the status transition to have been *written* first, not committed, because a transaction reads its own writes; and that the use-case layer may not demarcate a transaction itself, since that would need `org.springframework.transaction` in a layer required to have no Spring import at all. What does not follow is impossibility: a `@Transactional` method in the outer ring calling start and then deal joins both adapter writes into one transaction through the `REQUIRED` propagation they already carry, with no import added to `usecase`, which is where Clean Architecture puts demarcation anyway. That composer is declined on cost, and the cost is nothing to do with imports — `StartSessionUseCase` publishes `GAME_STARTED` over SSE during its own execution, so a composed transaction broadcasts a start that a rolled-back deal then unmakes, trading a recoverable inconsistency for an unrecoverable one; making it safe needs publish-after-commit, which is a larger design than the window it removes and belongs in its own ADR. The started-but-undealt window that leaves is accepted and recoverable, because `recordDeal` writes the leader seat only where none is recorded, so a retry is free and idempotent — recognising that state is the route slice's work, and ADR-025 records the alternative of one start-and-deal endpoint as deferred rather than rejected, which stays available precisely *because* the deal is its own class. **Authorising the requester is the first port call of all three**, since the ports take no acting player and say so: beneath them a conditional update that matches no rows re-reads the session and answers one of five distinguishable states, which is the right candour for a member and an oracle for a stranger, so each use case has a test asserting a stranger is refused **before any hand is read** — the assertion is on the repository double's read log, because a refusal that has already read is a refusal that has already leaked timing. **`PlayCardCommand` carries no seat, no player identifier, no suit and no rank.** Each is derivable from something the caller cannot choose — seat and player from the identity token, suit and rank from the deck row — and the two defects a security review found in Slice A were exactly a caller-supplied seat and caller-supplied suit and rank, so they are now inexpressible rather than validated, which is a stronger guarantee than a rule somebody has to remember to write. **No use case pre-checks a state its conditional write already arbitrates**: a status or already-dealt check would read state, let go of it, then write, which is an illusion of safety and duplicates ADR-020's compare-and-set — the defence in depth that is real here is *the use case authorises identity, the port enforces state atomically*, two different questions rather than one asked twice. **Two pre-flight guards exist only to order a refusal before a commit**, and neither is an authority: `hand.resolve(card)` and a leader-seat comparison run before `openTrick`, because `openTrick` commits a trick row and `Trick.acceptPlay` would refuse *afterwards*, leaving an open, empty trick nobody asked for and no later slice knows how to clear. At the end of a hand every hand is empty, so without the first guard a caller would receive an `IllegalStateException` — a 500 — for what is properly a 422. `Trick.acceptPlay` still re-checks both and still owns the answer. **The deal returns `void`**, because a result carrying every hand is precisely the shape that leaks private information — the reason `Hands.toString()` names no card — and a facilitator has no more right to see the table's cards than anyone else; a player reads their own hand through a query of its own in a later slice. **Only the facilitator may deal, but any seated member may resolve a trick**, because resolution is a mechanical consequence of the last card and gating it on one person would stall a table whose facilitator's connection dropped. **None of the three broadcasts anything, which makes them the first writes in the system that change what every player can see and tell nobody.** No use case here takes a `SessionEventPublisher`, and no `SessionEventType` constant is added, deliberately: that constant carries the name appearing in the `event:` field of an SSE frame, the name is fixed by `docs/api/openapi.yml` under contract-first (ADR-004), and minting one in a slice that publishes no contract would only guarantee a rename when Slice D writes it. Until the broadcast is wired a client learns of a deal, a play or a resolution **only by re-reading**, so the stream stays silent for the whole of a trick — Slice D owned the wire names and Slice E passed the publisher in, so all three names are emitted as of that slice; nothing in the repository failed while it was missing, which is why it is a numbered decision in ADR-025 and not only a line in a handoff. **The pre-flight ordering above is an argument rather than a mechanism, and its scope is now written down**: it holds by exhaustion over the refusals `Trick.acceptPlay` has today, since on an opening play no suit has been led and the trick is empty, and the refusals that could still fire are each pre-flighted before the write. Any refusal added to `acceptPlay` — or to `Trick`'s constructor, which carries nine of its own — reopens the orphan-trick window silently, from a class that never mentions trick rows; the durable fix is to persist the trick and its first play in one call, which changes the `TrickRepository` contract. Two smaller things carry reasoning too: `NoTrickToResolveException` and `TrickNotCompleteException` are separate types answering 409, joining `HandNotDealtException` to make three too-early states with three titles, because a client that says "waiting for the deal" rather than "waiting for the lead" rather than "waiting for a player" cannot tell them apart from one shared answer, and all three are retryable; and the shuffle is a port rather than a `java.util.Random` parameter so the security-relevant choice of source is made once in an adapter and cannot be weakened by a caller passing a seeded generator, with `Hands.deal` staying deliberately pure so every use-case test above it gets a permutation it can predict. `ResolveTrickUseCase` also gives `WinningPlayNotInTrickException` the only thrower it will ever have — unreachable through `Trick.resolved()` today, written because `fk_trick_winner_play` confines a winning play to the `trick_play` table and nothing narrower, and the composite key that would confine it cannot be expressed in Liquibase. Where a hand has run out of cards the resolution wrote the winning seat as the next leader, because the port took an `int` and rejected anything outside seat range; it was harmless while no seat holds a card, both branches were pinned by tests, and Slice E was the slice that learned to recognise the end of a hand — the parameter is now an `OptionalInt` and a played-out hand records no leading seat at all
- The HTTP routes for trick play: `POST /api/v1/sessions/{sessionId}/deal`, `GET .../hand`, `POST .../plays` and `POST .../tricks/current/resolve`, with `TrickController`, four transport records and a fourth use case, all gated on `eop.features.trick-play`, which stays off (EOP-14, ADR-004, ADR-013, ADR-015, ADR-020, ADR-023, ADR-026). Slice C2 shipped three use-case beans nothing could reach; this is the slice that makes them reachable, and the contract was written and committed **before** any controller code as ADR-004 requires — 476 lines of `openapi.yml` in a commit of its own, so the promise is on record before the code that has to keep it. Eight decisions are worth naming. **The route count is four at this slice, not the three the handoff asked for**, and the extra one is declared rather than slipped in: Slice C promised "an endpoint returning the caller's own hand" and neither C1 nor C2 shipped it, so a player who had been dealt a whole hand of cards had no way to see them, and without it the flag could never be flipped no matter what Slice E adds. That needed a fourth use case, `ReadOwnHandUseCase`, and no new port method — `HandRepository.findBySessionId` already returns the whole deal and the seat comes from the token. **The path is singular on purpose**, `/hand` and not `/hands`, breaking the plural-noun convention in `api-design.md` deliberately: it is a singleton sub-resource meaning *the caller's own hand*, and a plural would advertise a collection of every hand, which is precisely the operation that must never exist. There is no DTO for another player's hand either, because there is no operation that returns one. **The acting seat is never read from a request.** `PlayCardRequest` carries a card identifier and three annotation fields and nothing else; the seat and the player come from the identity token and the suit and rank from the deck row named by `cardId`, closing the two defects Slice A recorded — a caller-supplied seat let a player play out of somebody else's hand, and a caller-supplied suit and rank let a forged card take a trick. The test that pins this sends a body naming an impostor's `seatOrder` and `playerId` and a forged `suit` and `rank`, gets a 201, and asserts all four values in the response came from the token and the deck instead. **The trick published back says nothing about whose turn it is, whether the trick is complete, or which seat leads next**, because all three depend on which seats still hold cards, which is not part of a trick and which the use cases do not return; reporting them from a controller would mean either guessing or reaching past the use case into a repository. The consequence is stated rather than hidden: **the flag cannot be flipped until a later slice adds a read that exposes them**, because with only one's own hand and the trick returned from one's own play, a client cannot learn whose turn it is. (2026-08-14, EOP-14 Slice E: that consequence is discharged, and the sentence above is kept because it records why the flag stayed down through Slice D rather than because it is still true. The bullet below adds `GET /api/v1/sessions/{sessionId}/tricks/current`, which publishes all three of those answers and `handComplete` besides. Three reasons to keep the flag down remain, and none of them is a missing read: ADR-026, EOP-48 and EOP-15, per ADR-028.) **`threatLinked` had to be boxed.** The contract publishes it as optional defaulting to `false`, and the deserialiser refuses a record with an absent primitive `boolean` — so `{"cardId":"..."}` answered 400 and the server broke a promise it had published two commits earlier. It is `Boolean` now, with a compact constructor collapsing absent and explicit null to `false` so nothing downstream sees a third state, and the same constructor copies the component list, which refuses a null entry at the boundary — the one shape `@Size` waves through. Both were found by a test asserting the contract, not by the compiler. **The controller logs nothing, and that is the deliberate choice ADR-026 asked for.** These are the first routes through which dealing and playing can be invoked, so the observability gap stops being theoretical, but ADR-026 is `Proposed` with four arrangements still to weigh and one of them is "log at the boundary only" — adding a logger here would adopt that option inside a feature slice and make one controller the sole observed entry point. The ADR now records that the gap is reachable rather than merely present. **The token header constant is referenced, not extracted.** `TrickController` uses `SessionController.PLAYER_TOKEN_HEADER` rather than a shared constants class, because a class whose only members are a compile-time `String` and a throwing private constructor has no reachable instruction for JaCoCo to cover and the gate admits no per-class exclusions. **Three server-sent-event names are minted without an emitter**: `hand-dealt`, `card-played` and `trick-resolved` exist in the contract enum and in `SessionEventType`, reserved and fixed, because the name is the part clients depend on and a later release may begin emitting a reserved name but may never rename one. A test reads the contract as text and fails if the Java enum and the YAML enum drift. Two things are recorded honestly rather than fixed here. Unknown request fields are **accepted and ignored** repo-wide, not refused — refusing them would be stronger, but the setting is global to every endpoint and belongs to a decision of its own; the defence in the meantime is that no forged field is read, which the 201 test proves field by field. And `NotFacilitatorException` answers a refused deal with the title `Only the facilitator can start play`, which is accurate about the rule and vague about the action. One flag defect found in review was fixed rather than recorded: the controller's condition carried no `havingValue`, so it matched any value that was not literally `false` while its four use-case beans at that date required `"true"`, meaning `trick-play: yes` would have registered the routes with none of their use cases and failed at startup. It fails closed, but a flag whose own wiring disagrees with itself is a trap for whoever reaches for it during an incident. `SessionController` still carries the loose form and is left for a follow-up — and it is the worse of the two, because its use-case beans are unconditional, so nothing would fail the context: `session-lifecycle: off` or `no` or `0` would silently *enable* routes an operator believed were off. Fail-open, not cosmetic. Eighty-six tests cover the slice, sixty-four of them in three new classes, including the race the storage layer already proved at the JDBC level driven this time **through HTTP** — two simultaneous plays for one seat on two threads, two different cards, yielding exactly one 201 and one 409 and the loser's card back in its hand, which is the only test that proves the whole stack turns a lost race into an RFC 9457 refusal rather than a 500. That test asserts a *property* of the refusal rather than the identity of the rule that produced it: a problem document, whose `status` field agrees with the status line, carrying a title. It deliberately does not name the title. Both racers are the leader with no trick open, so where the loser has got to when the winner commits decides which rule stops it — the trick's own sequence constraint while opening it, the seat constraint while appending to it, or the domain's turn check on re-reading a trick that is now open — and a fourth route through the same window may exist. Naming the rule was wrong three times: once with a single title, once with two, and once more when a third appeared on a machine that was not the one the enumeration had been written on. An enumeration of a race is a guess that passes until the machine is faster. The rule that fired is diagnostic rather than contractual, and all three exact titles stay pinned deterministically by the exception handler's own unit tests and by the single-threaded out-of-turn test, so declining to guess here loses nothing
- The state of play, the end of a hand and the trick-play broadcast: `GET /api/v1/sessions/{sessionId}/tricks/current` with `GetTrickStateUseCase`, the `TrickState` result and the `TrickStateDto` transport record, publishing `seatToPlay`, `complete`, `nextLeaderSeat` and `handComplete` (EOP-14, ADR-014, ADR-020, ADR-023, ADR-027, ADR-028). This is the read the flag could never have been turned on without: `TrickDto` deliberately answers none of those four questions, because all four depend on which seats still hold cards, which is not part of a trick, so before this slice no client could find out whose turn it was except by attempting a play and being refused. Its two seat answers come from different authorities — `seatToPlay` from the trick while one is open and otherwise from the seat the session row records as leading, `nextLeaderSeat` derived by the domain from the cards — and nothing reconciles them on the way out, because a disagreement between the row and the cards is a defect a client is entitled to notice rather than one the server should hide. The contract was committed alone and first, in `b39e6a7`, touching only `docs/api/openapi.yml`. `TrickRepository.recordResolution`'s next-leader parameter widened from `int` to `OptionalInt` so that a played-out hand records `current_leader_seat = NULL` rather than a winning seat with nothing left to lead — Slice C2 sent the winning seat there as a placeholder because the port had no value meaning nobody — with `advanceLeaderSeat`'s parameter boxed to `Integer` and no new changeset, because changeset `005`'s `chk_game_session_current_leader_seat` already permits NULL. That makes NULL carry two meanings, so `claimDeal`'s `current_leader_seat IS NULL` predicate is no longer the whole deal-once gate: for a played-out session the claim now succeeds and `uq_hand_session_seat` refuses the second deal one statement later, as the same `HandAlreadyDealtException` and the same 409, rolled back inside the same transaction so the caller cannot tell which check did the work (ADR-020 amended, twice). `HandCompleteException` is the third state of that column to earn a name, mapped to 409 with its own title `Hand complete` and guarded at the top of `PlayCardUseCase` through `Hands.allEmpty()`, which gains its first caller: without it a play into a spent hand was refused by `Hand.resolve` as a 422 saying the caller had named a card it did not hold, when the honest answer is that it holds no cards at all. The shared `sessionMoved` helper gained a sixth answer, telling a played-out hand from an undealt one by a new derived `existsByGameSessionId`; nothing in the product reaches that branch today, because for the last trick to have been resolvable every seat still holding a card had already played, and it is written anyway because the alternative is an unreachable branch that answers a lie. The three event names minted but never emitted in Slice D — `hand-dealt`, `card-played` and `trick-resolved` — are now published by `DealHandsUseCase`, `PlayCardUseCase` and `ResolveTrickUseCase`, each after its write returns so a refused operation announces nothing, each carrying a type, a session and an instant and nothing else so no hand and no play is disclosed to the table, and none of them guarded at the call site because `SessionEventPublisher` places the obligation not to fail a request on its implementation. Two things were decided not to do (ADR-028): `eop.features.trick-play` stays `false` on merge, so all five slices ship complete and unreleased and turning the flag on becomes its own story whose predecessors are ADR-026, EOP-48 and EOP-15; and the session does not move to `COMPLETED`, because that word is documented as a hand whose score is final and this story computes no score, so EOP-15 owns the transition and a client watching only `GET /api/v1/sessions/{sessionId}` still never sees a session finish. A duplicate off-position test written during this slice was deleted rather than kept: `TrickPlayDisabledIntegrationTest` already asserts the beans and the routes in one class on purpose, no off-position gap ever existed, and it gained two tests for the new bean and the new route instead of a sibling.
- The scoring rule as pure domain types: `ScoredPlay`, `Standing` and `ScoreSheet` under `org.maglez.eop.entity`, with zero framework imports, computing the score of a game from the tricks already played (EOP-15, ADR-030). No schema, no route and no use case ships with them — this is the rule on its own, provable without any of that, and `ScoreSheet.of(players, tricks)` is a pure function whose whole input is a seating and a list of tricks. Seven decisions are worth naming. **The score is derived and never accumulated.** There is no running total anywhere, no counter column and no increment on the play path, because a stored total is a second authority that can disagree with the plays that produced it, and the moment it does there is no way to tell which of the two is the game. Deriving it also settles what the ticket's "computed and stored server-side" is really protecting: the constraint is about *authority* — the client never asserts its own score — and a derivation satisfies that more completely than a stored figure a write path could get wrong. **The rule is one point for a threat connected to your own card plus one for taking the trick, and it must not be embellished.** The richer system the deck's author tried — four for a threat on your own card, three, two and one for threats on other people's, plus bonuses for face cards and aces — was abandoned because playtesters could not compute it at the table and looked to the facilitator at the end of every round to be told who had scored. That constraint does not bind a server, so a richer rule is cheap to add later and is recorded as an option; it is deliberately not built here, because shipping the simple rule first is what makes the digital form's advantage measurable rather than assumed. **A linked threat that names no component still scores.** `TrickPlay`'s Javadoc left this open for this story to settle, and it settles it in favour of scoring: the written rule keys on the threat being connected, not on a text box being filled; the application holds no model of the system under discussion (PRD §3.4) and therefore *cannot* validate a component name, so making a point conditional on one would make the score depend on unvalidated client free text, which is exactly what "a client must never assert its own score" forbids; and the printed Score Card's Component(s) column is legitimately blank on plenty of real rows. **The two points a row can earn stay separate rather than pre-summed.** `ScoredPlay` carries `threatPoint` and `trickPoint` as distinct booleans because the Score Card is read by humans mid-game who need to see *why* a row scored — a row scoring one point because the threat landed looks nothing like a row scoring one point because the card took the trick, and a single `points` field makes the two indistinguishable at exactly the moment somebody queries the total. A play with no threat linked that still takes the trick is an ordinary row, not an anomaly. **Ties are shown as ties and never broken.** Standings use competition ranking, so four players on 7, 5, 5 and 2 hold positions 1, 2, 2 and 4, and `Standing.tied` is published even though a caller could derive it, because the interesting fact about a shared position is that it is shared. There is no tie-break rule to get wrong later because none is invented now. **A display name is never a key.** `DisplayName` is free text, unverified and explicitly not unique — two people at the same table may choose the same name and the humans on the call disambiguate them — so every row and every standing keys on `playerId` and carries the name for rendering only. `pointsOf` takes a player identifier and refuses a stranger rather than answering zero, because zero is a legitimate score and silently returning it for somebody not in the game would be a plausible lie. **An unresolved trick contributes threat points but no trick point.** The trick list may end with a trick still on the table, and the sheet scores its plays' threats while awarding nobody the trick, which makes a running score monotone as play proceeds — a total can never fall — and means the same class serves the mid-game read and the final standings with no second code path. Deciding that the game has *ended* is not this class's job and it does not try: it reports the score of the tricks it was given, and the session owns the transition to `COMPLETED`
- Reading the score: `GET /api/v1/sessions/{sessionId}/score` answers any seated player with the Score Card's five columns — name, points, card, component(s) and notes on threat — one row per play and one standing per seat (EOP-15, ADR-031). The contract was hand-authored into `docs/api/openapi.yml` before the controller existed. `GetScoreUseCase` reaches only two collaborators, because resolving the caller's token already yields the session and the players the sheet needs, and because a token is only ever matched against the players of the session it was presented for, that resolution *is* the membership check. The single new port method returns the session's whole trick history and filters nothing, so an unresolved trick still contributes its threat points and a running score stays monotone; its adapter batches four reads for the whole session instead of three per trick. There is deliberately no `409`: before the deal, everybody on nothing is a true answer rather than a missing one, which is why this read needs neither the hands nor a session status. ADR-027's prohibition on a collection of every player's state is answered rather than assumed — it protects *private* state, and a score names only cards already face up. Slice A's eight `IllegalArgumentException` refusals became a single `ScoreNotDerivableException` carrying a typed reason and mapped to 500 with a body indistinguishable from any other server fault, so a contradiction in the stored game can no longer be reported as the caller's mistake nor echo an identifier back to whoever guessed a session. The route ships behind `eop.features.trick-play`, still `false`, with the flag pair now pinning eight beans absent and six routes 404 in the off position and eight beans present in the on. Deciding that a game has *ended* remains the session's job and arrives with the third slice.
- API contract: the hand-authored OpenAPI 3.1 specification at `docs/api/openapi.yml` (ADR-004), written before the controllers that implement it
- Error handling: a single `@RestControllerAdvice` rendering RFC 9457 problem details, with a unit test for every mapped exception (ADR-005)
- Feature flags: Spring configuration properties under `eop.features.*`, gated with `@ConditionalOnProperty` and no third-party library (ADR-013). Two flags exist — `eop.features.session-lifecycle`, shipped with the session lifecycle (EOP-10) and **on by default as of EOP-25 (2026-08-16)**, and `eop.features.trick-play` (EOP-14), still off by default. Both are pinned in both positions by tests. The second is worth a note because what it withholds grew across its slices: it arrived gating three use-case beans and no route, since its slice shipped none, and gating the beans is what made the claim that nothing calls the ports which write a hand, a trick or a play true rather than merely intended — the adapter behind those ports is unconditional, so what the flag withholds is every caller of it rather than the capability itself. It now gates six use-case beans and two controllers together, and the disabled test asserts all eight are absent as well as asserting the six routes answer 404, because the status is what a client is promised while the absence is what pins the mechanism, and a test asserting only the status would pass against a design the flag rule forbids
- Real-time transport and player identity: decided from a time-boxed spike that ran a real server-sent-events endpoint against the application (EOP-8). Server-sent events carry state to every connected player (ADR-014); a server-issued opaque token in per-tab session storage identifies a player (ADR-015). Both are decisions only — the spike code was deleted and no production code graduated from it
- Load testing: k6 with SLO thresholds (p95 < 200ms), run against the container through the reverse proxy — the path a real user takes. The health endpoint measures p95 9.85ms against the 200ms threshold with 663 of 663 checks passing. The earlier 5.77ms figure measured the application's own port, which no longer exists, so `docs/performance/TRENDS.md` records a deliberate baseline reset rather than a regression. The InfluxDB + Grafana stack starts and is provisioned; k6 results did not reach it until 2026-08-23 — see the EOP-154 entry under Fixed
- GitHub MCP integration for agent-based PR and repository management, read-only at the server (ADR-003)
- Graphify knowledge graph exposed as a repo-local MCP server (ADR-011)
- Multi-agent system: 15 agents in `.opencode/agents/` — 11 delivery agents and 4 advisory experts
- Continuous flow over fixed sprints as the delivery model (ADR-010)
- `AGENTS.md` plus `.opencode/rules/` covering Clean Architecture, Security by Design and project conventions
- Product requirements: `docs/requirements/PRD-eop-card-game.md` defines the game, its scope, its trick-taking domain model and its open risks, and is backed by epic EOP-5. The rules are sourced from Microsoft's shipped instructions, score sheet and the author's whitepaper, all committed under `docs/EoP_Microsoft_Docs/` with their CC-BY-3.0 US licence recorded
- Commit convention enforcement: `.githooks/commit-msg` requires `[EOP-NNN] <type>: <summary>`, activated per clone via `core.hooksPath`
- Tool governance: explicit deny-then-allow-lists for `github_*` and `graphify_*` in `.opencode/opencode.json`. Jira writes run unattended for the two write-capable agents — the confirmation prompts were removed on 2026-08-12 because they stalled `/goal` runs, and the real guarantee is the bot account's revoked delete rights rather than a human clicking approve (Blueprint §7.2)

- **The remaining three `CardPage` contract fields asserted in the k6 cards canary (EOP-203).** `test/k6/card-catalogue.js` checked `content`, `content.length` and `totalElements`, leaving three of the five fields the `CardPage` schema declares required (`docs/api/openapi.yml`) unasserted. It now also checks `page === 0`, `size === 1` and `totalPages === totalElements`, bringing the canary's coverage up to the whole envelope. Asserting `totalPages` matters because it is computed rather than stored, so it exercises `Page.getTotalPages()` — a path nothing else in the canary reaches. All three are written as invariants that hold at `size=1` for any deck size rather than as literals, so a legitimate catalogue change does not have to be applied in two places and the CI smoke step remains the single owner of the seeded deck size; they still fail if `getTotalPages()` breaks or if the pagination parameters stop round-tripping. A negative run confirmed the predicates discriminate rather than being tautologies: at `size=2` the same endpoint reports `totalPages=34` against `totalElements=68`. No threshold changed, so `EXPECTED_THRESHOLD_COUNT` and `K6ThresholdCouplingTest` are untouched.

### Changed

- **`@product-owner` may now transition a Jira issue, because the deny that said otherwise was reachable around and had been for a day (EOP-000, [ADR-066](docs/adr/ADR-066-jira-restored-as-the-tracker.md) as amended 2026-09-06, [ADR-065](docs/adr/ADR-065-agent-role-boundaries-at-the-permission-layer.md) as amended 2026-09-06).** ADR-066 shipped on 2026-09-05 granting the Product Owner `atlassian_jira_*` and then denying, individually, the delivery-side writes — `transition_issue`, `assign_issue`, `add_worklog`, the sprint and version tools, `delete_issue`, `remove_*` and `move_*` — and both the agent's prose and the ADR stated that it therefore could not move a ticket through the workflow. **It could.** `atlassian_jira_update_issue` is granted by the same wildcard and carries no deny, and the MCP server routes a status change straight through it: the tool parses the caller's `fields` JSON and splats it into `jira.update_issue(**kwargs)`, that method intercepts a `status` key and delegates to `_update_issue_with_status`, which resolves the transition by name or id and calls `set_issue_status_by_transition_id`. The tool's own docstring says "including changing status", so `fields='{"status": "Done"}'` was never an exploit — it is the ordinary way to edit a field, and it happened to be the one field that was supposed to be off limits. **Closing the hole was the alternative and it was rejected**, because the only available deny is on `update_issue` itself, which is the tool ADR-066 exists to grant: without it the agent can file a ticket and never refine one, which is most of the requirements role. The permission layer matches tool names and cannot see a key inside a JSON argument, so no narrower deny exists. **The boundary therefore narrows by exactly one capability rather than dissolving:** `assign_issue`, `add_worklog`, the sprint and version tools, `delete_issue`, `remove_*` and `move_*` stay denied, the read-only `bash` block that closes the EOP-117 breach is untouched, and `edit` outside `docs/requirements/**` is still denied — the agent still cannot edit source, run a build, commit, push or open a pull request. Three prose sites that asserted the old position were corrected with it, and the ten review gates were checked rather than assumed: each denies `atlassian_jira_update_*` as well as `transition_issue`, so `tech-lead.md`'s claim that the decision to advance a story is the orchestrator's alone remains enforced and unchanged. **The lesson is ADR-065's, met a third time from a new direction.** Its earlier amendments recorded that a permission block and the prose beside it can disagree, and that both can disagree with the tracker; this one records that *two entries inside a single block* can disagree with each other, the allow winning silently — not by precedence but by circumvention, since the deny and the allow reach the same endpoint through different tool names. The operational rule that follows is to ask of every deny not whether it is present but whether a sibling allow reaches the same place by another route, and to delete a deny that fails that test rather than leave it standing as documentation of an intention a reader will mistake for a boundary.

- **`@tech-lead` may now `git push` a topic branch and `gh pr create` the pull request for it, so the ADR-065 publishing boundary moves from the push to the merge (EOP-000, [ADR-065](docs/adr/ADR-065-agent-role-boundaries-at-the-permission-layer.md) as amended 2026-09-05).** ADR-065 shipped on 2026-09-04 with `git push*`, `gh pr create*`, `gh pr merge*` and `gh release*` all denied on the orchestrator, and stated the chain as "delivery agents produce changes, the Tech Lead commits them, and only the operator publishes." The first two are now allows. The reason is that under `/goal` a green seven-gate round stalled on two mechanical operator steps — push the branch, open the PR — with no human necessarily watching, so an otherwise autonomous run ended in work that existed only in a local worktree. **The boundary is moved, not removed, and where it now sits is the load-bearing part:** `gh pr merge*` and `gh release*` remain denied, so nothing reaches `main` and nothing ships without a human, and the test for which side of the line a step falls on is reversibility — a topic branch can be deleted and a pull request closed, a merge and a release cannot. Branch protection on `main` is unchanged and is the real backstop; it is what refused the EOP-117 push that ADR-065 was written from, and no permission here ever did. **Two denials were added rather than removed.** `git push --force*` and `git push -f *` are repeated from the global ruleset inside the agent's own block, because a per-agent block **replaces** the top-level one rather than merging with it, so the broad `git push*` allow would otherwise have silently granted a history rewrite — a strictly worse grant than the one asked for — and they sit *after* the broad allow because opencode evaluates the last matching rule. Three things this does not touch: the eleven delivery agents keep their full blocklist including `git commit`, so the Tech Lead is still the only agent that commits and now the only one that can push; `@product-owner` keeps the read-only `bash` that closed the EOP-117 breach at the tool layer; and `AgentPermissionDeclarationTest` is unaffected, because it proves a key is *declared* and never judges its value — which is exactly the bound its own Javadoc states, so this change passing the build is not evidence the build agrees with it. **The honest cost, recorded rather than argued away:** the blast radius of a mistaken autonomous run now reaches the remote, a wrong branch and a wrong pull request being things the orchestrator can create unattended, and ADR-065's "speed bump, not a boundary" caveat applies to the two remaining denials exactly as it applied to the four. Reverting means flipping the two allows back to `deny`. Prose was corrected in the three places that contradicted the new position — the `bash` block's own comment, the Jira-protocol line in `tech-lead.md` that read "A release is a human act, exactly as `git push` and `gh pr create` are", and Blueprint §7.8's shape table plus the sentence that used `"git push*": deny` as its worked example of a speed bump, which for this agent is now an allow and so no longer an example of anything.

- **`dependency-cve` is now a required status check on `main` too, so the required set is four jobs rather than three (EOP-149, [ADR-050](docs/adr/ADR-050-dependency-cve-scanning.md) as amended 2026-09-05).** The job landed on 2026-08-22 deliberately advisory, and ADR-050 §2 named promotion as the intended end state behind four preconditions that EOP-149 was explicit should not be skipped. All four are now met, and the evidence is worth stating rather than asserting. **A real CRITICAL has been handled in anger:** on 2026-09-03 three freshly disclosed CRITICALs against `org.apache.tomcat.embed:tomcat-embed-core` (CVE-2026-65182, CVE-2026-65905, CVE-2026-68525) turned `main` red, and were closed by a one-line `<tomcat.version>11.0.25</tomcat.version>` override under EOP-189 — the upgrade, which is the first step of ADR-050 §6's order of preference, not an allowlist entry. Two of the three were in fact unreachable, there being no authentication of any kind in this application ([ADR-015](docs/adr/ADR-015-player-identity.md)), and that was deliberately recorded as an exploitability note rather than argued as a reason not to upgrade. **The false-positive rate is measured, not estimated:** across all 60 `ci.yml` runs on `main` between 2026-08-22 and 2026-09-05 the job passed 58 times and failed twice — runs `33758809931` and `33792466074`, both the same genuine Tomcat disclosure — for nought false positives. The one *known* false-positive class, an H2 advisory gating falsely because the scan reads the resolved Maven tree and so does not honour [ADR-047](docs/adr/ADR-047-h2-excluded-from-deployable-artifact.md)'s exclusion of H2 from the deployable jar, has never once fired. Only two weekly `schedule` runs exist, so the cron population is thin; the push population is the richer evidence and is what the figures above come from. And the two blocking tickets are closed: EOP-147 gave the gating logic an extraction-based test harness, and EOP-146 — fixed in the commit immediately preceding this one — holds the 0/1/2 exit contract on every reachable path, a precondition that was considered for waiver and rejected, since waiving a broken exit contract on the very gate being made blocking is indefensible. **What this does not do is dissolve the hazard that kept the job advisory, and the Tomcat incident is that hazard rather than a counterexample to it.** The finding surfaced when `@dependency-vulnerability` rejected a *documentation-only* change; had the check already been required, an unrelated doc PR would have been blocked by someone else's release schedule. What changed is not the hazard's likelihood but the judgement about which risk is worse, and that judgement now has both halves measured: the hazard costs a blocked unrelated PR for the length of an upgrade, while a visible-but-unbinding gate cost `main` roughly seven hours red on 2026-09-03 with nothing stopping two further merges — the same failure ADR-060 recorded for `sonar-ratchet`, where it ran to about a day. **The escape hatch is the allowlist, not a person, and that is a decision recorded rather than a default inherited.** ADR-050's order of preference is now three steps: upgrade the dependency; failing that add a time-boxed `tools/supply-chain/accepted-cves.json` entry, which carries a reachability trace, a reviewer, a date and a mandatory `expires` field, so the suppression is reviewable in the diff and retires itself; and only as a last resort have an administrator lift the requirement, recorded in the pull request. Last because it is the only one of the three that leaves no artefact in the repository — the available token returns `403 Resource not accessible by personal access token` on both `GET` and `PUT` of the branch-protection endpoint, so a lift that was never reinstated would be invisible to every automated check. **Two caveats survive promotion.** Branch protection is configured in repository settings and not in `ci.yml`, so no comment, ADR or changelog entry here can make the job required or stop it being required; this entry records an intent that only the settings enforce, and the two can drift apart silently and undetectably. And the job carries no `if:` today, which is now load-bearing rather than incidental: a required check that can be skipped leaves a pull request permanently unmergeable, so adding a path filter or an event guard to `dependency-cve` would break branch protection rather than merely narrowing the job. Four stale statements are corrected in place — the `dependency-cve` comment block in `ci.yml` and its three neighbours that enumerate the required set, ADR-050 §2, the 2026-09-04 amendment to [ADR-060](docs/adr/ADR-060-sonarqube-issue-ratchet.md) whose "What stays advisory" paragraph named this job, and ADR-050's row in `docs/adr/README.md`. The corresponding claims in the entries below, at EOP-193, EOP-189 and EOP-89 and in the ADR-061 seven-gates entry above, are deliberately left standing as the historical record they are, and are superseded by this one.

- **Both SonarQube ratchets are now required status checks on `main`, so `build` is no longer the only one (EOP-193, [ADR-060](docs/adr/ADR-060-sonarqube-issue-ratchet.md) and [ADR-063](docs/adr/ADR-063-sonarqube-frontend-project.md), both as amended 2026-09-04).** Both jobs landed deliberately advisory, and `ci.yml` said why in terms: the failure mode nobody had lived with was the stale-report block, so the first few times someone pushed a one-line Java fix and was told to boot a container and rescan, that should be an annoyance rather than a merge blocker — "so the friction can be measured before it is mandatory". **The friction has now been measured, and it came back adverse in the direction nobody planned for.** PR #362 added four `.java` files and merged without a rescan; the freshness guard did exactly its job, refusing to compare 307 recorded source files against the 311 present and exiting non-zero — and blocked nothing, because the job was advisory. `main` then sat red on `sonar-ratchet` across two further merges for about a day (runs 33905648171, 33914462303, 33915481685) until someone happened to read a checks page. The anticipated cost turned out to be trivial when finally paid: one `tools/sonar/scan.sh`, a five-line diff to `sonar-report.json`, and the ceiling unchanged at 26/1/0 because those four files introduced no gated production finding. The cost actually paid was a gate whose enforcement was whoever next looked. **`sonar-ratchet-ui` is promoted alongside it, and that is a choice rather than a finding** — the front-end project never suffered the failure; the symmetry exists so a front-end regression cannot merge while the identical Java one is blocked, for no reason a contributor could infer. **One precondition is now load-bearing: neither job carries an `if:`.** A required check that can be skipped leaves a pull request permanently unmergeable, because GitHub waits for a status that never arrives, so adding a path filter or an event guard to either job while it is required would break every merge in the repository — the one way a well-meant edit to `ci.yml` could do that. What stays advisory stays advisory for reasons that have not changed: `dependency-cve` can go red on a third-party CVE publication against unchanged code, so requiring it would let an unrelated pull request be blocked by someone else's release schedule, whereas a ratchet's inputs are two committed files and a hash of the tree, so it only goes red because of the change in front of it. **The honest caveat is that none of this is enforced by anything in the repository.** Branch protection lives in repository settings, so `ci.yml`, the two ADRs and `docs/adr/README.md` record an intent that only the owner can apply, the two can drift apart silently, and no agent can even read it back to check — the token returns 403 on both `GET` and `PUT` of `/repos/…/branches/main/protection`. Red was always visible either way; "required" changes only whether it blocks.

- **Agent role boundaries are now enforced by each agent's `permission` block instead of by prose it can override, after `@product-owner` twice did delivery work its own briefing forbade (EOP-000, [ADR-065](docs/adr/ADR-065-agent-role-boundaries-at-the-permission-layer.md)).** The second occasion is the measurement: the Product Owner implemented a structured-logging story end to end — seven files, 560 insertions of Java, XML and YAML under `src/`, `./mvnw verify`, a commit, a branch, a push and a pull request — while `product-owner.md` already said in three places not to, including "You do not start delivery yourself" and "Never use it to start delivery." The operator's question was "how do I enforce it with guarantee that it won't be bypassed", and the honest answer is that a system prompt cannot: presented with a context that looked like work already in flight, the model continued it, and nothing at the tool layer intervened. **The root cause is that omitting a permission key affirmatively grants it.** The effective ruleset begins `{"permission":"*","action":"allow","pattern":"*"}` and the last matching rule wins, so `product-owner.md`, which declared neither `edit` nor `bash`, held both at `allow` — and **not one of the seventeen agent files declared a `bash` key at all**, so every agent in the system could `git commit` and `git push`. The GitHub MCP was never the vector and is not touched: it is already `github_*: deny` plus a read allowlist plus the `X-MCP-Readonly: true` header, and the breach used the local `gh` CLI through `bash`. Four shapes now apply. The **four `expert-*` advisers** move from `edit: deny` to an allow-list — `"*": deny` then `read`, `grep`, `glob`, `list` — because `edit: deny` alone had left them holding `bash`, contradicting the sentence that justified the deny; an allow-list rather than a longer deny-list because a list of today's tool names silently grants tomorrow's. The **Product Owner** gets a path-scoped `edit` (`"*": deny`, then `"docs/requirements/**": allow`), a `bash` denied except `git status*`/`git log*`/`git diff*`/`git show*`, and the mutating scheduler tools denied.
