The **eleven delivery agents** keep `bash: {"*": allow}` — they must run `./mvnw verify`, both SonarQube ratchets, `npm run verify` and the Trivy scan, and their Sign-off Contract obliges them to paste real command output — minus `git commit*`, `git push*`, `git reset*`, `git checkout*`, `git restore*`, `gh pr create*`, `gh pr merge*` and `gh release*`. **`@tech-lead`** loses the same publishing commands but keeps `git commit`, which makes it the only agent that may commit: delivery agents produce changes, the Tech Lead commits them, and only the operator publishes. **A fourth vector was found during the work and had not been documented anywhere:** plugin tools are unaffected by `bash` and `edit`, and `run_job` accepts `agent`, `prompt`, `command`, `model` and `files` overrides while `schedule_job` cron-runs an arbitrary prompt, so an agent holding `bash: deny` and `edit: deny` still reached arbitrary execution *under a different agent's identity* — confirmed live by a probe that enumerated the scheduler tools with both denials in force. The six mutating job tools are therefore denied per agent, with `@performance-engineer` the deliberate exception (it owns the scheduled k6 run, so it keeps the whole job lifecycle and denies only `cleanup_global` and `install_skill`) and `@tech-lead` the other. A blanket global deny was rejected precisely because it would have broken that load test. **Two mechanics were verified before anything was relied on, both undocumented upstream.** A `mode: primary` probe with `edit: {"*": deny, ".tmp/permtest/**": allow}` wrote inside the glob and was refused outside it, so `edit` patterns match worktree-relative *file paths* and last-matching-rule-wins holds for `edit` — which is what makes the Product Owner's PRD carve-out possible rather than forcing a blanket `edit: deny`. A top-level `permission: {"*": deny}` catch-all is honoured. Two traps came with them and are recorded so the next person does not repeat them: `opencode run --agent` **silently falls back to the default `build` agent** when handed a `mode: subagent` file, so the first probe measured the wrong agent's permissions and looked like total failure; and a permission check must assert an observable side effect, never ask an agent to enumerate its own tools — a probe asked to do that refused as unverifiable introspection while narrating a `read` call it had not made. **Stated plainly, because the two classes must not be cited as one: the flat denials are boundaries and the `bash` command patterns are not.** A flat `deny` removes the tool from the roster entirely, so there is nothing to refuse and nothing to talk past. A `deny` on a command pattern is still pattern matching over command text and inherits every limitation of the `ask` patterns it sits beside, including the three ways the friction lifts — answering "always", the `--auto`/`--yolo`/`--dangerously-skip-permissions` flags and the TUI auto-approve toggle, and upstream #39001's non-deterministic matching. That makes the `bash` blocklists an argued departure from `security.md`'s preference for allow-lists, on §7.8's own reasoning that a control which makes ordinary work unbearable gets switched off within a day and a control that is switched off protects nothing. Three further limits: `read` is unrestricted throughout, so this scopes what an agent may **do** and not what it may see; `@` invocation by a human is unaffected, as it always has been; and because the fix is per agent, **a newly added agent inherits every permission it does not state**, which nothing in `./mvnw verify` checks — a test asserting every file under `.opencode/agents/` declares `edit` and `bash` is a reasonable follow-up that does not exist today. Prose was kept rather than deleted, with its job changed: `product-owner.md` now carries the verbatim redirect to emit ("press **Tab** and switch this session to `tech-lead`"), so the boundary produces a helpful handoff instead of a confusing tool-denied error, and its instruction to *commit* a PRD — which it can no longer satisfy — is corrected to writing the file and handing the path to the operator. Blueprint §7.8 gains `#### bash — deny where delivery is not the role` and `#### The plugin-tool escape hatch`, and its `edit` subsection is rewritten because it had explicitly justified the Product Owner keeping an unscoped `edit` — true of the intent, false of the consequence. Two deliberate follow-ups are left unaddressed here to keep this change coherent: with Jira gone the replicated `atlassian_jira_*` blocks are dead config, and `product-owner.md` still describes a five-agent sign-off that has been seven since ADR-061. Both were then closed *for `product-owner.md` only* by the entry below; the other ten agent files still carry the dead Jira block.

- **`product-owner.md` still ran on Jira, which no longer exists — the tracker is GitHub Issues, and the agent cannot write to it (EOP-000).** The permission work above left the Product Owner coherent about what it may *do* and wrong about *where the backlog lives*: the file named Jira nineteen times, told the agent to "file the stories in Jira", used `PROJ-101`-style keys throughout, and carried four `atlassian_jira_create_*` allows. Jira was exported to `docs/jira-export/` and abandoned; the live backlog is GitHub Issues, titled `EOP-188 …` or `[EOP-168] [UI] …` and labelled `story`/`tech-debt`/`build-quality`/`tooling`. **The sharp part is that the agent cannot file an issue either, and no permission change would fix it** — `.opencode/opencode.json` denies `github_*` except the read tools, and the MCP server is pinned read-only by an `X-MCP-Readonly` header, so no issue-write tool exists for *any* agent to call. Its central documented duty was therefore unreachable through both trackers simultaneously. The resolution matches the PRD rule the permission work had already established: **the Product Owner drafts the issue body and the Prompter files it.** A new `## The Tracker` section states that, names the three read tools it does have (`github_issue_read`, `github_list_issues`, `github_search_issues`, enough to check for duplicates and read history), and adds two rules the shape makes necessary — never claim to have filed, updated or closed an issue, and never invent the next `EOP-NNN` key, because the keys are sequential and unmerged work is invisible, so ask or state which key you read it from. The four dead `atlassian_jira_create_*` allows are replaced by `atlassian_jira_*: deny`; they had granted nothing anyway, since the global config already allows every Jira tool, and the deny means a re-enabled MCP cannot quietly reopen a write path to a dead backlog. Three further corrections were needed once the tracker was right: the pre-merge defect rule named four gate agents when there are **seven**, and the Definition of Done listed six boxes with no SonarQube ratchet and no CVE line, so both now enumerate all seven with the gate that owns each; the Delegation Boundary said the completion audit forces a five-agent sign-off, stale since ADR-061; and "Bug Sub-task" became GitHub **sub-issues**, with the note that GitHub has no typed link relationships, so an escaped defect's `Caused by #398` must be explicit prose in the body rather than a link type. The feature-flag line in the story template was also rewritten to make the author choose a mechanism before naming a flag — `eop.features.*` plus `@ConditionalOnProperty` plus a registry entry for a flag that removes a Spring bean, versus `VITE_*_ENABLED` for one that only changes what the browser renders — because two tickets have already specified the back-end mechanism for a purely presentational feature and neither could have worked. **Scope limit worth stating:** this fixes one file. `AGENTS.md`, the Blueprint and the ten other agent files still describe Jira, and nothing in `./mvnw verify` walks `.opencode/agents/`, so that drift is reviewer-enforced.

- **The repository name's misspelling is corrected: `eop-threat-medeling` becomes `eop-threat-modeling` across every tracked file that is an instruction rather than a record (EOP-188).** Seventeen files, twenty-six occurrences: the Docker image tag (`README.md`, `compose.app.yml` ×2, `scripts/update-local.sh` ×4, `.env.example` ×2, `docs/adr/ADR-016-local-container-runtime.md:104`), the clone URL and directory (`docs/devops/local-development.md` ×2), the Spring application name (`src/main/resources/application.yml`, the only runtime-visible change and it affects log output only), the GHCR package path (`infra/terraform.tfvars.example`, `infra/variables.tf` ×2), the AWS `default_tags` `Repository` value (`infra/versions.tf`), the four `tools/jira-migration/*.py` `REPO` constants, the `REPO` constant that builds commit links on the published perf-trend page (`tools/perf/trend-page.html`), the CI build tag chain (`.github/workflows/ci.yml` ×3) and this file's `[1.0.0-SNAPSHOT]` link-reference definition. **Five of those seventeen were absent from the ticket's file table and were found by measuring rather than trusting it**, which matters because two are genuine post-rename breakages rather than cosmetic: `.env.example`'s `APP_IMAGE=` overrides `compose.app.yml`'s default, so correcting compose alone would have left a developer copying the example building one tag and running another; and `infra/variables.tf`'s GHCR defaults would have pointed at a package path CI no longer publishes to, since `ci.yml` derives that path from `github.repository`. **Four occurrences are deliberately left misspelled because they are historical records, not instructions** — the orphan Docker volume `eop-threat-medeling_influxdb_data` really does bear that name (`docs/adr/ADR-016-local-container-runtime.md:201`, and this file's EOP-154 entry), as do the `%eop-threat-medeling%` session-directory filter and the old image tag recorded in this file's EOP-000 and EOP-76 entries; the two `docs/jira-export/*.json` files (214 further occurrences) are an immutable export. Rewriting any of them would falsify what was true at the time. **The ticket's fourth acceptance criterion is defective in two ways and is recorded here as amended rather than as met**: it passes GNU-grep `--include=` flags to `git grep`, which rejects them with `fatal: unable to resolve revision` and a non-zero exit — so the naive `cmd && echo FAIL || echo PASS` idiom reports a spurious pass having examined nothing — and once rewritten with pathspecs it excludes only `docs/jira-export` and this file, not `ADR-016`, so it cannot be satisfied without contradicting the ticket's own leave-unchanged instruction. It is therefore read as "no matches outside historical records", with ADR-016:201 a fourth documented exception. Renaming the repository on GitHub and repointing each local `origin` remain human steps; GitHub's permanent redirect keeps existing clones and links working, and disappears only if a new repository is created at the old name
- **The SonarQube issue ratchet now gates production code only, and the three ceilings fell from 11 Reliability / 232 Maintainability / 0 Security to 1 / 31 / 0.** Not because anything was fixed: 211 of the 243 findings were test-code findings the gate no longer adjudicates. The friction ADR-060 predicted arrived — with 87% of findings in test code and a ceiling carrying no headroom, a routine new test file reddened a gate that had nothing to say about the product. Narrowing the gate was admissible only because the analysis is untouched: `sonar.sources` and `sonar.tests` are still unset, test code is still scanned on the same footing and still hashed by `source-hash.sh`, and its findings are still measured and recorded under `scope.TEST` beside a new `scope.ALL` carrying the whole-tree measures, which `MAIN + TEST` must reconcile with per quality or `write-report.py` warns. The gated inventory is harvested with `&scopes=MAIN` and the gated counts come from the `scopes=MAIN` facet, so "production" keeps one definition — SonarQube's own scope classification — rather than a second copy of the path knowledge in `source-hash.sh`. **The reason was not coverage and must not be restated as though it were:** SonarQube already classified `src/test/java` under `sonar.tests`, so coverage measured 95.1% before the change and 95.1% after, with `ncloc` and the test total likewise unchanged. What it costs is stated rather than hidden — a green `sonar-ratchet` now says nothing whatever about the 36,965 lines under `src/test/java`, and that limit is recorded in `ratchet.py`'s own "what this gate does not prove" list. `SeparationInvariantTest` narrowed `@sonarqube-expert` to `PRODUCTION_CODE`, which retired its three comparisons against the test-code authors and took `MINIMUM_COMPARISONS` from 27 to 24; the knock-on is that ADR-061's `MODEL_B`-is-structurally-ineligible argument lapsed, so that pin now rests on the ADR-046 capability floor and its recorded probe. (EOP-000)
- **`tools/agent-trace.py` now lists the ten most recent root sessions and closes with project-wide cost and time totals over every session ever recorded, subagents included (EOP-000).** The listing default fell from twenty roots to ten, and a footer was added reporting session counts, total cost and two separate elapsed-time figures. **Cost is a straight sum because `session.cost` is per-session rather than cumulative** — verified against the database: a root with no children still carries a nonzero cost, and a root's cost is independent of its children's — which is the assumption `report()` already made when it summed a trace. **Time is the part that needed a decision, and the footer deliberately reports two numbers rather than picking one.** Subagent spans nest *inside* the parent span that dispatched them, so adding all 1186 spans together multi-counts wall clock badly: the naive sum is 786.38h against a merged-interval union of 581.52h across 104 segments, and a roots-only sum of 589.73h is no better because sibling roots also overlap. So `total time` merges overlapping and nested intervals and is the honest calendar answer, while `summed spans` shows the naive figure alongside it — not as a second opinion but so the two reconcile visibly and nobody re-derives the larger number and assumes the smaller one is a bug. **Neither figure is effort or billed time, and the footer says so in two lines of caption rather than leaving it to be inferred.** A session's span is first message to last touch, so it counts every minute the window sat idle; the union covering ~93% of the 2026-07-29 → 2026-08-24 calendar window is the proof of that, not a coincidence. The session set is selected by `directory LIKE '%eop-threat-medeling%'`, factored into a new `project_pattern` helper now shared by `list_sessions`, `newest_root` and the totals, so **the footer cannot summarise a different set of sessions than the table above it** — the failure this would otherwise invite. That pattern deliberately matches two directories: the repository root (1184 sessions) and `.../eop-threat-medeling/.opencode/agents` (2 sessions, $33.68), the phantom-launch incident `AGENTS.md` warns about. Both are this project's spend and excluding the second would understate it. `format_span` always prints all four units (`0d 00h 00m 00s`) so figures stay column-comparable, and clamps negatives, though no row in the database currently has `time_updated < time_created`. `--last`, `--json` and explicit `--limit N` are unaffected.
- **`tools/agent-trace.py`'s footer now breaks the five Definition-of-Done gates out individually, reporting how often each was dispatched, how long it was in flight, what share of the project window that is, and what it cost (EOP-000).** The gate roster is `GATE_AGENTS = set(PIPELINE["2 gateways"])` — **derived rather than hand-listed**, following the precedent `MULTI_STAGE_AGENTS` set for exactly this reason, because the file already carried three copies of the five-gate list and a fourth would have let a sixth gate be reported by `conformance()` while going silently missing from the cost and time report. Aggregation groups on the existing `canonical()` helper rather than on the raw `agent` column, so history written before the `team-member-*` rename folds into the modern names instead of reporting one agent as two — that is not theoretical here, and the effect is visible in the output: `code-reviewer` reads 179 sessions rather than 176, and `security-auditor` 185 rather than 184. **The share is occupancy, and calling it anything else would be wrong rather than merely loose.** It is not a slice of a budget: the 235 non-gate sessions merge to cover 100.00% of the project window on their own, and gate plus non-gate unions come to 115.22% of it, because a gate is a child session running inside the span of the `tech-lead` that dispatched it. So a gate's share answers "how much of the project's wall-clock window had this gate in flight", and no reader may conclude that the remaining 85% went somewhere else. **The rows deliberately do not add up, and the footer says so in its own caption rather than leaving it to be discovered.** The five gates are dispatched in parallel, so summing their times overstates by 89% — roughly 7d against a merged 3d 17h — and summing their five shares yields 28.8% against a true merged 15.3%. Percentages invite that addition far more strongly than durations do, which is why the caveat is printed and not just documented. **Cost is the exception, and the asymmetry is the useful part:** `session.cost` is per-session, so the five cost figures add exactly to the merged row's `$1,638.44`, and the caption states that only the costs add. Read together the two columns say something the totals could not: the gates are 80% of all sessions and 33.7% of all spend but only 15.3% of calendar occupancy — short, dense and parallel. Two structural improvements came with it. The interval-union sweep is now a shared `merge_spans` helper that **sorts its own input** rather than relying on the caller's SQL `ORDER BY`, removing a hidden precondition that a reviewer had flagged as load-bearing on the previous change, and the timestamp guard is now `valid_spans`, whose dropped-row count is surfaced as an `undated` line printed only when nonzero — closing the previous round's finding that a row with a missing or inverted timestamp would be counted in the totals while falling outside the printed date range. Both the totals and the breakdown are computed from **one query and the same row list**, so a share's numerator and denominator cannot come from two different reads of a database that grows while you are reading it. **Three limits stated plainly.** `architecture-guardian` appears in both stage 1 and stage 2 of `PIPELINE`, so its row necessarily bundles its ADR-authoring dispatches with its gate reviews and the file's own comment already warned the arithmetic cannot tell the two apart; the footer discloses this rather than glossing it, and separating them would need a per-dispatch signal the database does not carry. `--json` is the trace path and remains unchanged, so the breakdown is available in the listing only. And `tools/` still has no Python test harness, so nothing in the build covers any of this — the finding both testing gates raised on the previous change stands, and every figure quoted above was verified by running the tool and checking that the costs and session counts add while the times do not.
- **`tools/agent-trace.py`'s two time figures now count root sessions only, so neither adds a subagent's time on top of the session that dispatched it** (EOP-000). The old `summed spans` line added up every session in the database, which counted the same wall clock once per dispatch and reported roughly 33 days against a real figure nearer 24 — about eight days of that was subagent time already covered by a parent span. It is replaced by `sessions sum`, the root sessions added up one by one. The change is grounded in a containment check rather than an assumption: every *completed* subagent row recorded for this project fell entirely inside its own parent's span, none extended beyond it, none was orphaned, and nesting was exactly one level deep because a subagent never dispatches a subagent. That is a property of the history rather than an invariant the tool enforces, and it holds only once a session has finished — a subagent still in flight can briefly report a timestamp past its parent's, because the parent's has not refreshed yet. A direct consequence is that merging every session yields a result identical to merging only the root sessions — the same total across the same number of segments — so `total time` was already free of subagent double-counting and is unchanged; the figure that was misleading was the naive sum beside it. `sessions sum` still exceeds `total time`, by roughly half a day, and that residue is worth reading rather than smoothing away: on the history as it stands it is accounted for entirely by *concurrent root sessions*, which is what two OpenCode windows open at once looks like in the data — and that happened repeatedly while this tool was being built. Cost is deliberately untouched and still counts every session, because `session.cost` is per-session and strictly additive — only the time semantics moved, and the footer now says in print that the subagent sessions are in neither time figure and why. The per-gate table is also unchanged and still correct: gates *are* subagents, so their rows remain per-gate interval unions measured against the same root-session union as before. Two limits stand. Neither time figure is effort or billed time — a span is first message to last touch, so idle minutes count — and `tools/` still has no Python test harness, so nothing in the build covers any of this.
- **`tools/agent-trace.py` now reports worked time measured from message timestamps, and demotes the two session-span figures it used to headline (EOP-000).** The old `total time` read over 24 days across a 27-day calendar window, which was not credible for an operator who took whole days off, and the cause was structural rather than arithmetic: a session's span is `time_created` to `time_updated`, first message to last touch, so a tab left open counts every intervening night. Three root sessions accounted for eleven of those days between them, the worst of them spanning five days across four on which no work happened at all, and the same effect recurred nightly at small scale wherever a session was opened in the evening and touched the next morning. Measured against the new figure at the shipped cutoff, roughly three quarters of what was reported as time was idle — the active figure is about a quarter of the span-based one. The footer had always carried a caveat saying the number was calendar coverage rather than effort, and that caveat was true and useless — nobody reads a line labelled as time as meaning how long a tab was open, so the honest disclaimer was shipped where the honest number belonged. **The evidence for the honest number was one table away the whole time.** `message` holds one row per message with its own `time_created`, some 48,000 of them for this project, and the new `sessionise` groups them into blocks of continuous activity, starting a new block wherever the silence between two consecutive messages exceeds a cutoff. Because it reasons about gaps rather than spans it needs no interval merging and no parent/child logic at all: a subagent's messages and its dispatcher's interleave in one ordered stream, so nesting and concurrent OpenCode windows both collapse for free. The default cutoff is **two minutes**, chosen by measurement rather than taste — the whole range from 30 seconds to an hour was computed and compared against the operator's own recollection of their working days, and two minutes was the one whose per-day figures matched it. It is deliberately tight: it keeps the pause while a subagent runs, which is genuinely working time, and drops nearly everything else. Because the result is cutoff-sensitive — an hour's cutoff reports well over half again as much as two minutes — `--idle-cutoff MINUTES` exposes it rather than burying it, since hiding a load-bearing parameter would repeat the original mistake in a new place. `total time` is renamed **`calendar span`** and keeps its old value, and a new **`active time`** line sits above it as the figure to quote. The two span-derived figures are retained deliberately rather than deleted: the per-gate share column divides one span-derived duration by another, and swapping the denominator for the activity figure while the numerators stayed span-derived would mix two bases and could produce a share above 100%. Three limits stand. The per-gate table is therefore still measured on the calendar basis, so a gate's `time` column is occupancy and not its own worked time — per-gate active time is computable from the same table and is simply not done here. Active days are bucketed in UTC like every other timestamp in the tool, so a session run late in a positive-offset timezone lands on the following day, and a block spanning midnight counts only against the day it began — so the figure counts days on which work *started*, not days touched. And `tools/` still has no Python test harness, so nothing in the build covers any of this; `sessionise` was verified by hand across nine boundary cases including an empty stream, a lone message, duplicate timestamps, a gap landing exactly on the cutoff (which must not split, since the test is strictly greater-than) and unsorted input. The cutoff itself is range-checked rather than merely tested for positivity: `argparse`'s `type=float` accepts `inf` and `nan`, neither of which a `<= 0` test rejects — `nan <= 0` is False — so both reached the millisecond conversion and aborted with a raw `OverflowError` or `ValueError` traceback, and a value like `1e20` was accepted outright and silently produced nonsense. Finiteness is now checked explicitly and the cutoff is bounded at one day, beyond which it stops describing a break and simply disables the measurement by collapsing every message into a single block.

- **Join codes are eight Crockford base32 characters rather than six, which is exactly forty bits of entropy rather than thirty (EOP-24).** `JoinCode.LENGTH` is the only switch that had to move: `SecureRandomJoinCodeGenerator` and three test helpers already derived their bounds from the constant, so they widened without being touched. Alongside it, a new dated changeset `2026-08-22--widen-join-code-to-8-characters.xml` widens `game_session.join_code` to `VARCHAR(8)` and pads existing six-character rows, `docs/api/openapi.yml` moves `minLength`, `maxLength` and `pattern` on both the path parameter and the `JoinCode` schema, and ADR-019 is amended with the attack model — with ADR-021, ADR-023 and ADR-033 amended where each quoted the superseded figure. The reason for the change is that the original arithmetic assumed the wrong attacker. Six characters is 32⁶, or 30.0 bits, and the per-code half of the join limiter never binds while that keyspace is being enumerated, because every guess is a *different* canonical code that lands in its own fresh bucket — so the only control actually in play is ten failures per minute per address, about 14,400 guesses per address per day. Against roughly a thousand proxied addresses and ten to fifty live lobbies, a real lobby turns up in one and a half to seven and a half days, not the centuries the limiter's own Javadoc used to claim. Eight characters is 32⁸, about 1.1 × 10¹², which is 1024 times more work and moves the same pool from days to years. **The limiter was deliberately not downgraded in the process:** length bounds how much of the keyspace a blind search must cover and the limiter bounds how fast any one address may cover it, so ADR-019 now calls it one of two controls rather than the primary one, and ADR-021's trusted-proxy fix is untouched and unaffected by length — an attacker who rotates `X-Forwarded-For` defeats the rate bound at any code length. The migration **pads** legacy rows rather than deleting them because `GameSessionJpaEntity.toDomain()` calls the strict `JoinCode` constructor: a surviving six-character row would throw `IllegalArgumentException` and turn the reconnect path into a 500, whereas padding keeps every foreign key intact, is injective so it cannot collide with `uq_game_session_join_code`, and leaves reconnect working. Codes already read aloud to players do stop working, which is intended and quiet — `JoinCode.parse` returns empty and the endpoint answers with its usual indistinguishable 404 rather than an error — and the exposure is bounded by ADR-036's twenty-four-hour `expires_at` TTL. Two unrelated defects were fixed in passing: `DeckTrimMigrationRoundTripTest` hardcoded `rollback(1)` and `rollback(2)`, silently assuming nothing would ever be appended to `changes/`, and now computes the depth with a `changesetsAppliedFrom(...)` helper in the idiom `SessionExpiryMigrationTest` and `TrickPlaySchemaRoundTripTest` already used, so the next migration will not break it; and the "measured in centuries" claim is gone from `InMemoryJoinAttemptLimiter`, since that figure quietly assumed the attacker owned a single address. Evidence: `./mvnw clean verify` passes at 1274 tests with zero failures and zero errors, and `npm run verify` in `ui/` passes at 223 tests including typecheck, ESLint and a production build. Two new tests pin the change rather than merely the character count — one computes the entropy from `ALPHABET` and `LENGTH` and asserts it is exactly 40 bits, and one asserts that a legacy six-character code no longer parses. `JoinSessionForm.tsx` was inspected and constrains only non-emptiness, so no front-end length change was required. What this is honestly worth is narrower than the headline: the ten-to-fifty-lobby assumption is load-bearing, so a near-empty demo is ten to fifty times slower to attack than the figures above, the prize is a seat in a card game, there is no authentication anywhere in the application (ADR-015), and rows the migration padded still carry only thirty bits — though those codes were already circulating. The direction is right and the change was cheap, which is why it was made; it was not an incident.

- **H2 no longer ships inside the deployable artifact, and the scope declaration deliberately did not move — [ADR-047](docs/adr/ADR-047-h2-excluded-from-deployable-artifact.md) (EOP-34).** EOP-27 closed the H2 web console as an autoconfiguration concern with three controls — `spring.h2.console.enabled: false` in `application.yml`, the same pin again in `application-prod.yml` as a deliberate second guard, and `H2ConsoleAbsentIntegrationTest`'s classpath tripwire — and @security-auditor recorded one gap all three miss: they guard the path where Spring registers the console for you, not the path where somebody registers it by hand. `com.h2database:h2` was `<scope>runtime</scope>`, and packaging `main` produced a 66,566,354-byte jar containing `BOOT-INF/lib/h2-2.4.240.jar`, whose `org/h2/server/web` package holds 13 classes including `JakartaWebServlet`, `WebServlet` and `WebServer` — the auditor's count of 13 was checked and is exact. The console is unauthenticated arbitrary SQL accepting a caller-chosen JDBC URL, the shape of CVE-2021-42392, with no Spring Security dependency that could stand in front of it. **The fix is an `<excludes>` block on `spring-boot-maven-plugin`'s `repackage` execution, not `<scope>test</scope>`,** and the distinction is the whole decision: EOP-34's own Scenario 1 is written about the jar ("absent from the repackaged jar") rather than about the word `test` in `pom.xml`, and hardening the artifact is *stronger* than hardening the scope, because it still holds if somebody later widens the dependency to `compile` for an unrelated reason. The 2,685,418-byte nested jar is gone from the artifact, and `unzip -Z1 target/*.jar | grep -cE 'org/h2/|^BOOT-INF/lib/h2-'` now returns 0 — stated as the size of the jar removed rather than as an exact delta between the two builds, because `pom.xml` is itself embedded in the artifact, so editing a comment in it shifts any such delta by a few hundred bytes and the figure goes stale on the next prose edit. **Placement on the execution rather than the plugin is load-bearing and was the one real hazard.** `excludes` is inherited from `AbstractDependencyFilterMojo`, which the plugin's `run` goal also extends, so a plugin-level `<configuration>` would have stripped H2 out of `./mvnw spring-boot:run` and broken local development in exactly the way this change exists to avoid; the `pom.xml` comment says "Keep it here; do not hoist it", and it was proven rather than reasoned about — `./mvnw spring-boot:run` on the default profile returned 200 from `/health` in 5s with `Database dialect: H2Dialect` in the log. **A bare move to test scope was rejected because the default profile is not incidental.** `application.yml` resolves `org.h2.Driver` and `H2Dialect`, and that path is named by `README.md`, `SETUP.md`, `docs/devops/local-development.md`, `AGENTS.md`, ADR-008 ("a fully migrated DB on `./mvnw spring-boot:run` with no manual steps"), ADR-021, ADR-035, and above all ADR-012, which says of the Swagger UI that it "remains available via `./mvnw spring-boot:run` on the default profile. This is a decision, not a defect, and should not be 'fixed' later." Moving the default profile to PostgreSQL was rejected for the same reason plus the loss of clone-and-run, and `spring-boot:run` with `useTestClasspath` was rejected because it would drag `src/test/resources/application.properties` onto the run classpath and silently repoint local development at `jdbc:h2:mem:eop-test` with test feature-flag values. Nothing pointed the other way: no deployed configuration has any use for H2, since `compose.app.yml` runs `SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prod` against a real PostgreSQL service on both a laptop and EC2, and `grep -rn "java -jar" --include="*.md"` finds no document running the jar at all — the sole tracked occurrence is `.env.example` describing `java -jar -Dspring.profiles.active=prod`. **The gate is a script rather than a test, and the reason is narrower than "no test could do it".** No *surefire* test can: surefire binds to `test`, which precedes `package`, so the repackaged jar does not exist when the suite runs. But `maven-failsafe-plugin` binds `integration-test` and `verify`, both after `package`, and is already resolvable at 3.5.6 from the Boot parent, so a failsafe IT reading the jar with `java.util.zip.ZipFile` would work and would run inside `./mvnw verify` — @code-reviewer established this during the gate round and it is recorded here rather than left as the broader claim first written. It was considered and rejected on one ground only: this project declares no failsafe execution, so adding one would make a seventh declared Maven plugin, and `tools/supply-chain/audit-plugins.sh` already establishes the committed-script-invoked-from-CI idiom for a gate of this exact shape. The cost of that choice is that `./mvnw verify` alone does not prove the property, so a developer running only Maven locally never exercises it — recorded as a negative consequence in ADR-047 rather than argued away. `tools/artifact/assert-no-h2-in-jar.sh` runs in the `build` job of `.github/workflows/ci.yml` immediately after `./mvnw verify` and *before* `Upload artifact`, so nothing unverified is published and the gate sits inside the only required check on `main`. Written in the `audit-plugins.sh` idiom, it asserts exactly one candidate jar (refusing to guess otherwise), then a **positive control** — `BOOT-INF/lib/postgresql-*.jar` must be present, so an empty or truncated jar cannot satisfy an absence assertion for the wrong reason — then no `BOOT-INF/lib/h2-*.jar`, then no `org/h2/**` entry anywhere, the last catching H2 arriving flattened, shaded or under different coordinates. Both directions were proven before commit: restoring `origin/main`'s `pom.xml` and repackaging makes it exit 1 with `FAIL: H2 is back inside the artifact: BOOT-INF/lib/h2-2.4.240.jar`, and pointed at a directory with no jar it also exits 1, so it can neither pass vacuously nor pass a regression. **EOP-34's second item, a `maven-enforcer` ban on `org.springframework.boot:spring-boot-h2console`, is declined,** on the auditor's own EOP-27 reasoning that such a ban "is a stronger *prohibition* but a weaker *control*… it also cannot be satisfied", whose predictable outcome is `-Denforcer.skip=true` or deletion, and that "a ban with no sanctioned escape hatch trains people to disable bans". The EOP-27 tripwire is the satisfiable control for the same risk — it fires at review time and a reviewer is its escape hatch — and declining keeps the declared Maven plugin count at six. **Two corrections to the register, both in the direction that makes the original claim smaller.** The ticket asserts a console is reachable "with zero dependency changes" via `new ServletRegistrationBean<>(new JakartaWebServlet(), …)` or `org.h2.tools.Server.createWebServer(…)`; neither compiles, because `runtime` scope keeps a dependency off the compile classpath — settled with a real compile that failed `package org.h2.tools does not exist`, not by reading a scope table, and note that `dependency:build-classpath -Dmdep.includeScope=compile` reports `h2-2.4.240.jar` and is misleading for this question. The reachable paths are reflection or a *second* reviewed change widening the scope, so this is future-proofing and should be described as such. And the accepted residual is that a developer laptop still has H2 on its runtime classpath, so a reflectively-registered console remains possible there against an in-memory database holding only that developer's scratch data: this gates the artifact, which is the part that reaches a server, and the script's header carries a "What it does NOT check, and must never be described as checking" section saying exactly that. One further honest cost — `./mvnw spring-boot:run` and `java -jar` now have different classpaths, a divergence against ADR-012's local-predicts-deployed thesis, bounded to one dependency the `prod` profile never loads and *visible* rather than silent, since running the jar on the default profile fails at startup with a missing driver.

- **The two review gates that audit `MODEL_A`'s own output moved to a new sixth tier, `MODEL_F` (EOP-000).** `@security-auditor` and `@architecture-guardian` were pinned to `MODEL_A` — the same model ID as `@tech-lead`, the primary agent and the global default — so production code the primary agent authored itself was cleared by two gates sharing its weights. Both now sit on `MODEL_F` = `amazon-bedrock/minimax.minimax-m2.5` (Zen route `opencode/minimax-m2.5`, present in that catalogue but **unprobed on that route**), a third family alongside the Anthropic tiers `MODEL_A`/`MODEL_B`/`MODEL_D` and the Qwen tiers `MODEL_C`/`MODEL_E`. That **closes the second exception to the Separation Invariant** rather than narrowing it: `MODEL_A`-authored production code is now reviewed family-independently by both gates, and tester-authored tests on `MODEL_B` gained a family-independent reviewer for the first time. **One exception remains** — test code, where `@code-reviewer` shares `MODEL_B` with the two testers it reviews — so the invariant must be cited with one exception from this date, not two. It is **one** tier holding two agents rather than two variables, which is the `MODEL_C`/`MODEL_E` precedent inverted on purpose: there, one model behind two variables so the front end can be repointed without disturbing the back end; here, two agents behind one variable because their requirements are identical and every extra variable multiplies drift across the tier tables. Five files moved together and had to: `.env` (both provider blocks), `.env.example` (both blocks plus its tier legend and invariant paragraph), the `.envrc` assertion — which now names all six variables, so direnv refuses to load without `MODEL_F` — and the `agent` block of `.opencode/opencode.json`, where the two gates were moved out of the `MODEL_A` group into a new `MODEL_F` group rather than having their values edited in place. `tools/agent-trace.py` needed **no** change, because it reads every pin dynamically from `opencode.json` instead of hard-coding tier names, and no agent definition in `.opencode/agents/` needed one either, since none carries a `model:` line and every pin lives solely in the config. Two operational caveats, both of which have already cost time here: a tier change takes effect **only after an OpenCode restart**, and editing `.envrc` invalidates direnv's allow record — whose key is a hash of the file's path *and* its exact bytes — so `direnv allow` must be run in the same breath or nothing in `.env` loads at all, including the sole working AWS credential. And `MODEL_F` is a **bare** Bedrock ID with no `eu.` inference profile, exactly as `MODEL_C`/`MODEL_E` already are: it therefore adds no residency posture beyond the one the repository already had, but it does not improve it either, and whether a bare ID keeps inference in-region is stated as unverified rather than assumed in either direction.
- **A Definition-of-Done gate is now pinned by a capability floor and a passing probe instead of a tier name — [ADR-046](docs/adr/ADR-046-gate-model-capability-floor.md), superseding ADR-022 rule 1 only (EOP-000).** Rule 1 read "any agent named as a Definition-of-Done gate is pinned to `MODEL_A` or `MODEL_B`", which is a whitelist of tier *names* standing in for a capability it never states — a proxy that breaks the moment a third capable tier is wanted, and one that cannot be widened by editing a table, because rule 6 of the same ADR forbids exactly that. The replacement states the requirement directly: reasoning-capable, structured tool calls, at least 40,000 max output tokens and 128,000 context, family-independent of every tier that authored the artefact under review, **and a passed two-stage probe whose verdict is recorded in Blueprint §3.4.1** — stage 1 invocability of the exact identifier, stage 2 truthful tool use requiring an *observed* tool call, no stream error, and an answer matching independently established ground truth. A pin with no recorded passing verdict is non-compliant. `MODEL_C`/`MODEL_E` stay ineligible, but as a *consequence* of the floor (neither is reasoning-capable) rather than by enumeration, which is what makes the rule extensible. **The probe clause is the substantive part, and it is there because the metadata floor alone was demonstrably insufficient.** Three candidates satisfied every documented capability clause on paper and were unusable: one whose reasoning stream OpenCode's Bedrock provider cannot parse at all, so it passes a trivial text prompt — which emits no reasoning block — and dies on every real audit, which does; one that made a genuine tool call and then reported `20` lines for a six-line file, inventing a heading the file does not contain and reproducing a different file's bullets as its contents; and one that answered correctly with no tool call whatsoever, which an unguessable re-probe exposed as a guess. Fabricated `file:line` evidence is strictly worse for a gate than the hollow `VERDICT: APPROVE` failures ADR-022 was written to stop, because it survives review. Rules 2 to 6 of ADR-022 are unaffected, and its original text was left standing with dated amendment notes rather than rewritten, per the index's own instruction not to edit a superseded decision. **Nothing enforces any of this programmatically** — no test and no script checks a gate's tier, its floor or its probe record — so the ADR's wording is the mechanism, and a reviewer is the only thing that applies it.
- **The front-end deal helper is renamed `dealCards` → `dealHands`, and the ticket that prompted it was already fixed (EOP-67).** EOP-67 reported that `LobbyScreen` started a session without ever dealing, so every player landed on "Could not load game — No hands have been dealt in session …". That defect is not present on `main` and has not been since `35c3ed0` (EOP-70), which added the helper to `ui/src/api.ts` *and* its call site in `LobbyScreen.handleStartGame` in one commit; `LobbyScreen.test.tsx` already covers the success path, the failure path and the call count by stubbing global `fetch` rather than spying on the module, so the parsers are genuinely exercised. What EOP-70 did not do was agree with the rest of the repository on a name. `docs/api/openapi.yml` declares `operationId: dealHands`, `TrickController.dealHands` carries it into the adapter and `DealHandsUseCase` into the application layer — three of four layers said `dealHands` and only the browser said `dealCards`, which is the shape of divergence that makes a grep for a behaviour miss the half of it that lives in the front end. The rename is therefore the whole of this change: the helper, its import, its call site, both Vitest suites, and the four documents that name it in **live** prose — ADR-045 §4's parser roster (which `.opencode/rules/error-handling.md` points at as authoritative for which helpers correctly have no parser), ADR-040's record of the non-atomic start-then-deal window, `C4-Diagrams.md`'s parser-exemption paragraph, and the ADR-045 entry above. No Java changed, no route changed, no request or response body changed, and the helper still reads no body and still gets no parser, which is correct rather than an omission: it is a `204`. ADR-045's one **historical** mention is deliberately left as `dealHands` *with* a parenthetical naming the old identifier, because a sentence describing what a test suite covered in August must stay true of that August and a reader meeting `dealCards` in `git log` needs somewhere to land. **Neither ADR status line is amended**, on the view that a nomenclature alignment is not a re-decision and that diluting the amendment convention costs more than it buys — a judgement put explicitly to the architecture gate rather than made quietly, and one with a mechanical consequence worth stating, since `AdrIndexConsistencyTest` holds `docs/adr/README.md` against every status line and an amendment here would have pulled the index into a rename. **The timing window ADR-040 records is unchanged and remains benign**: the SSE `game-started` event can mount `GameScreen` while `dealHands` is still in flight, and `GameScreen` already absorbs the resulting `409` as a waiting state rather than an error, with the subsequent `hand-dealt` event re-fetching the hand. That is the one thing worth being clear about, because it is why EOP-67's reported symptom cannot reappear from a lost race: a race loses into "waiting", not into "could not load".
- **The plugin supply-chain check is now a script and a CI job instead of a sentence asking someone to remember (EOP-000).** `tools/supply-chain/audit-plugins.sh` reads the `plugin` array of `.opencode/opencode.json`, compares it against the baseline in `tools/supply-chain/expected-plugins.json`, and fails on four conditions: a maintainer account change, a provenance change in *either* direction, a spec that has lost its exact version, or a high/critical advisory. Low and moderate findings deliberately do not fail it — the accepted residual is exactly four lows, and a job that goes red on a known accepted finding is a job that gets ignored. It is wired into `.github/workflows/ci.yml` as a **non-required** `supply-chain` job, so `build` stays about the code and a fresh transitive advisory is something to learn rather than a reason to block an unrelated merge. The workflow also gained a **weekly cron** and `workflow_dispatch`, and the cron is the substantive part rather than a nicety: every other job is a function of a commit, whereas an advisory is published by someone else against code that has not changed, so a check firing only on push cannot observe one arriving. Both directions were proven before committing — a clean run exits 0 and reports 152/152 verified registry signatures with 51 attestations, and a run against a deliberately perturbed baseline (an entry deleted, a maintainer changed, a provenance flag flipped) exits 1 with all three tripwires naming their own remediation. The baseline is documented as a **tripwire, not a policy**: it records what was true when a human last looked, so a *change* becomes loud, and it must be updated in the same commit that moves a pin and never to make a red job green. **The motivation is a measured failure, not a hypothetical.** The prose trigger this replaces fired four times and was honoured once, and one of the misses was the commit that *edited the paragraph recording the trigger as unhonoured* without honouring it.
- **Two claims about the plugin roster were wrong in the direction that flattered it, and one open question is now closed (EOP-000).** Checking all six pinned plugins rather than the three that had moved shows provenance is **3 of 6, not 5 of 6**: `@tarquinen/opencode-dcp`, `opencode-supermemory` and `@nick-vi/opencode-type-inject` carry SLSA attestations; `opencode-vibeguard`, `opencode-scheduler` and `opencode-goal-plugin` do not. So `opencode-goal-plugin` is **not** uniquely unattested as `SETUP.md` and Blueprint §12.8 both implied, and the company it keeps is worse than the correction sounds — `opencode-vibeguard` is the secret-redaction plugin, which makes the component asked to keep credentials out of prompts one of the least verifiable in the roster, and `opencode-scheduler` writes launchd/systemd units and so has the most persistent reach outside the editor process. This is stated as *less to verify against*, never as evidence of a problem: nothing suspicious has been found in any of them and maintainer continuity holds everywhere, an account handoff being the loudest single compromise signal. The closed question is whether plugin installation runs npm lifecycle scripts: **it does not**, verified against `anomalyco/opencode` at tag `v1.18.19` rather than assumed. `Npm.add()` in `packages/core/src/npm.ts` runs no package manager at all — it imports `@npmcli/arborist` in-process and constructs it with `ignoreScripts: true` positioned *after* the spread of user npm config, so an `.npmrc` cannot re-enable scripts, and arborist gates `preinstall`, `prepare`, `install` and `postinstall` on exactly that flag. Three caveats are recorded with it rather than dropped: `binLinks: true` is still passed, so `node_modules/.bin` symlinks *are* created and "no scripts" is not "no executables placed"; the guarantee rests on arborist being pinned to exactly `9.4.0`, which reads the flag from constructor options rather than per-call ones, so a future version merging per-call options would silently re-admit the `.npmrc` value OpenCode already forwards; and the scope is the install step only, since a plugin's own module code executes at import time by design — a far larger surface than any postinstall hook, and the reason the checks that matter are about *who published this*.
- **`noContinueWhileChildrenActive: true` is recorded as configured-but-unobserved after a deliberate attempt to provoke it failed for a structural reason (EOP-000).** A goal was set, a subagent dispatched as a live child, and `get_goal_history` read immediately after; it held only the `set` event. Deferral is recorded when an **auto-continue attempt is suppressed**, and auto-continue only fires once the session is **idle**, so a turn that dispatches a child and then inspects the history has not ended and no attempt has occurred. Observing the flag therefore requires a turn to *end* with a child still running, which cannot be forced from inside that turn. `AGENTS.md` and Blueprint §12.8 now say so plainly, along with the two practical consequences: it is the first thing to revert if `/goal` misbehaves, and the absence of a `deferred` event in a normal run is the expected reading rather than a sign the option is broken.

- **`autoUpdate` is now `false` in `.opencode/dcp.jsonc`, and the plugin roster has a measured supply-chain baseline for the first time (EOP-000).** Both changes answer one question — is pinning to *latest* safe when a compromised release is indistinguishable from a good one — and the honest part of the answer is that the preceding upgrade did not ask it. Pins were reviewed for behaviour, by reading changelogs and unpacking tarballs, and for supply chain only afterwards when a human challenged it; that is the wrong order and the standing instruction in Blueprint §12.8 now says so. **`autoUpdate` was flipped precisely because it was inert.** DCP never auto-updates a version-locked spec and ours carries an exact version, so at `true` the switch could not fire — but it *would* have started pulling whatever npm `latest` happened to be the moment anyone loosened the spec to a range or a bare name. A key with no upside and one failure mode is a trap to remove rather than a footnote to document, so `false` removes it; it had been `true` since `bbda911` purely as an unreviewed upstream default. **The baseline itself moved the risk in the opposite direction to the intuition that prompted it.** `npm audit` against a throwaway manifest of all six exact pins now reports **0 critical / 0 high / 0 moderate / 4 low**, superseding `SETUP.md`'s 2026-07-27 snapshot of **5 high / 4 low** — the highs were retired *by* the upgrade, so holding the old pins was the more exposed position, not the safer one. The four survivors are one chain (`@babel/core` arbitrary file read via `sourceMappingURL`, through `@opentui/solid`) whose only `fixAvailable` is a `isSemVerMajor` **downgrade** of DCP to 3.1.12, i.e. abandoning DCP; it is a build-time dependency of a dev tool and reaches no untrusted input. Three checks that `npm audit` cannot perform were run beside it and are the ones that matter for unsandboxed plugins that see every message and file: **maintainer continuity** held across all three bumped packages (`tarquinen`, `dhravya`, `williamricchiuti`), an account handoff being the loudest compromise signal and it is silent; **registry signatures** verified 152 of 152; and **SLSA provenance attestations** cover 51 packages including `@tarquinen/opencode-dcp@3.1.15` and `opencode-supermemory@2.0.12` — but **not `opencode-goal-plugin@0.8.1`**, which is hand-published by a single maintainer at six releases in ten days and is simultaneously the most privileged plugin loaded, spawning agents and reading whole sessions. State that as *less to verify against*, never as evidence of a problem, and note that provenance proves a tarball came from the named repository's CI and never that the code is benign. `SETUP.md` carries the reproducible procedure and drops the stale acceptance reasons, and records the one unmeasured gap: the audit installs with `--ignore-scripts`, while whether OpenCode's own `Npm.add()` path runs lifecycle scripts is unverified. **The re-check trigger is the real finding.** It has now fired four times and been honoured once, only because it was asked for — the third firing was an upgrade that *edited the paragraph recording the trigger as unhonoured* without honouring it. Prose does not run, so the entry now says this needs a CI job rather than another sentence.

- **Three OpenCode plugin pins moved to latest and the goal plugin gained a child-session guard; the request was "upgrade OpenCode" and the binary turned out to be the one thing already current (EOP-000).** `opencode` was already at `1.18.19`, which is `npm view opencode-ai dist-tags` `latest`, so `opencode upgrade` is a no-op — the staleness was entirely in the `plugin` array of `.opencode/opencode.json`. `@tarquinen/opencode-dcp` 3.1.14 → **3.1.15**, `opencode-supermemory` 2.0.11 → **2.0.12**, `opencode-goal-plugin` 0.6.7 → **0.8.1**. `opencode-vibeguard@0.1.0`, `@nick-vi/opencode-type-inject@1.5.2`, `opencode-scheduler@1.3.0` and `tools/graphify/`'s `@sentropic/graphify@0.17.1` were each checked against the registry and are already latest, so they are untouched; DCP's published `3.2.8-beta0` was declined because a `beta` dist-tag is not a pin this project takes. The two `@opencode-ai/plugin` dev pins (`.opencode/package.json` at 1.18.11, `~/.config/opencode/package.json` at 1.18.9) are deliberately left behind the binary: they are gitignored, typings-only, and used solely to author `.opencode/plugins/graphify.js`. **The one behaviour change is `noContinueWhileChildrenActive: true`**, an option 0.8.0 added, which defers auto-continue while a session has live children instead of prompting the orchestrator over work a subagent is already doing — directly relevant here because `@tech-lead` under `/goal` dispatches five DoD gate subagents, and a `/goal status` reading `deferred` now means *waiting on a subagent* rather than a hung loop. It fails open where children cannot be enumerated, so it can never be the sole explanation for a stalled goal. Its sibling `noInterruptOnUserMessage` was **not** enabled: it would make a human message steer a running goal instead of pausing it, removing the ability to halt one by typing. All eleven existing goal options are unchanged. Nothing in 0.6.8–0.8.1 relaxes the startup guard at `src/native-agent-config.js:27-31`, verified against the 0.8.1 tarball rather than assumed, so **"never create `.opencode/agents/goal-verify.md`"** still holds; `AGENTS.md` now says so with the citation attached.
- **Six documentation claims about the plugin roster were false, and three of them were falsified by this upgrade rather than by inspection (EOP-000).** Verification was done by unpacking the published tarballs with `npm pack`, because every one of these claims had previously been recorded from release notes and had rotted. (1) Blueprint §12.8 said goal-plugin `0.6.8` was "tagged and changelogged upstream while never published to npm — the registry returns 404"; it **is** published, so the multi-instance lease-contention gap it fixes is closed, and the entry is rewritten as the passive-goal-mode degradation it actually shipped — a losing instance now observes a goal read-only instead of hard-erroring out of plugin init, provided *every* participating process runs 0.6.8+. `docs/devops/local-development.md`'s troubleshooting entry for `ERROR goal persistence is already owned by pid <n>` is re-pointed at that behaviour and now reads as the pre-0.6.8 form of a symptom rather than the current one. (2) Two of §12.8's four line citations into the plugin's own source had rotted across four releases — `parseAuditVerdict` 3163 → **3773** and `requireVerifierOwnership` 3532 → **4591** in `src/goal-plugin.js` — while both citations into `src/native-agent-config.js` (`:27-31`, `:37-66`) are byte-for-byte where they were. That asymmetry is now recorded as a standing instruction: cite the small stable file by line and the 6000-line bundle by symbol, and re-grep on every bump. (3) Supermemory's bogus `Supermemory update available: v2.0.10 -> …` banner is **fixed** in 2.0.12, which generates `dist/version.d.ts` from `package.json` instead of hardcoding `PLUGIN_VERSION`; the banner had fired against both 2.0.11 and 2.0.12, upstream issue #64 is closed, and memory writes are no longer stamped with a wrong `sm_plugin_version`. The Blueprint instruction inverts accordingly — a future banner is genuine and should be believed — with the residual hardcoded `cli_version: "2.0.10"` in `dist/cli.js` noted as CLI-path-only. Three further corrections fell out of the same inspection: the plugin registers no auth slash command at all, so `/supermemory-init` and `/supermemory-login` both do not exist (the CLI has exactly `help`, `login`, `logout`, `status`); the claim that the OAuth flow is unrunnable "because no JavaScript runtime is on `PATH`" is retracted, since `node`, `npm` and `npx` are all present and `SUPERMEMORY_API_KEY` is kept for the different reason that a browser-OAuth dotfile credential is neither reproducible on a fresh machine nor visible to CI — and upstream commands written with `bunx` must be run with `npx` here, because `bun` is not on `PATH` and OpenCode's embedded bun exposes no passthrough; and the "stale vendored copy at `.opencode/node_modules/opencode-supermemory`" trap no longer exists, that tree having gone from 190 MB to 61 MB and now containing **no** plugin packages whatsoever, only the `@opencode-ai/plugin` typings and their transitives.
- **`autoUpdate: true` in `.opencode/dcp.jsonc` was inert, and has since been set to `false` — see the entry above, which supersedes the conclusion drawn here (EOP-000).** The key read as a second source of truth competing with the pin, against `SETUP.md`'s rule that the exact specs in the `plugin` array are authoritative "and nowhere else". DCP's own `README.md` settles it: the switch updates an npm-*installed* DCP and explicitly does not update version-locked plugin specs, and ours carries an exact version, so it never fires. Recorded in Blueprint §12.3 along with the reason not to loosen the spec to a range, and with the upstream signal that new context-management development has moved to **Sleev** — worth watching precisely because 3.1.15 exists largely to repair installation on "recent OpenCode 1 releases", the `1.18.x` line this repository runs, so a DCP that stops tracking the binary is a plugin that stops loading. `SETUP.md`'s `npm audit` re-check trigger has now fired a **third** time without being honoured, and the obstacle has changed shape: `.opencode/package.json` exists again but declares only the typings dependency, so auditing `.opencode/` audits the authoring toolchain and not the plugins that load. Reinstating it needs a throwaway manifest built from the pinned specs, and the trigger itself needs an owner or a CI job rather than another sentence of prose.

- **The Liquibase `contexts` mechanics moved out of `.opencode/rules/database.md` into ADR-043, and the directive stayed behind (EOP-35, ADR-043).** The `context` entry had grown to 1859 bytes — 40% of a 63-line file in a single bullet — and, decisively, it pinned a library version in a file with no Status field and no supersession mechanism, which is the failure class EOP-32 exists to remove. The rule file keeps what an agent must act on without following a link: omit `context` and `labels`, and the fact that a bare tag restricts nothing unless `spring.liquibase.contexts` names a real, non-empty context in *every* profile including the excluding one. Reducing it to a bare pointer was rejected because `ddl-auto: validate` walks only the mapped Hibernate metamodel and would never notice a mis-set filter, so "omit it" is the sole line of defence. The mechanics, the 21-encoding verification matrix, the three discard mechanisms, the two attribute spellings and the residual risks now live in [ADR-043](docs/adr/ADR-043-liquibase-contexts-are-not-used.md), which is the live statement of all of them; `LiquibaseContextGatingAbsentTest` fails the build if either half of the decision is broken, and [ADR-006](docs/adr/ADR-006-build-quality-gates.md) records it as a new class of gate.

- **Deck trimmed to 74 printed cards (EOP-69).** Tampering now starts at rank 3 and Elevation of Privilege starts at rank 5, matching the physical card game. A Liquibase migration deletes the four absent cards (TAMPERING rank 2, EoP ranks 2/3/4) with full rollback. Deal distributions updated: 3 players → 25/25/24, 4 → 19/19/18/18, 5 → 15/15/15/15/14, 6 → 13/13/12/12/12/12. The opening-lead rule ("lowest Tampering card dealt") is unchanged and naturally resolves to rank 3 with this deck.

- **ADR-029 records the global rule-loading decision that EOP-000 implemented.** The rationale for replacing the four-path `instructions` array with `.opencode/rules/*.md` — the unsatisfiable lazy-load trigger, the zero-intersection routing hole, the EOP-14 observability incident, the ui-builder propagation test, and the accepted ~20 KB always-on cost — was captured in the Blueprint §7.6 rewrite and the CHANGELOG entry above, but not as an ADR. ADR-022 established the precedent that agent-governance decisions belong in `docs/adr/` rather than only in living reference documents, because a rationale overwritten in place cannot be cited or superseded. ADR-029 follows that precedent (EOP-000)

- **Nine of the fifteen rule files reached no model at all, and the agent prompts said so in as many words.** `.opencode/opencode.json` loaded four cross-cutting rules globally and left the other eleven to a `## Required Reading` section at the end of nine agent prompts, to be read with the `read` tool "before you start work that touches them". That trigger cannot be satisfied: judging whether an *unread* file is relevant is guesswork, the cheap guess is always "not relevant", and nothing falsifies it, so nine of the fifteen routinely went unread for an entire story. The routing was also aimed away from the one agent that writes the code — `tech-lead` was pointed at `feature-flags` and `versioning`, while the union its five Definition-of-Done gates enforce is `api-design`, `build-quality`, `caching`, `error-handling`, `observability` and `resilience`: an intersection of exactly zero. During EOP-14 the Tech Lead dismissed `observability.md` as non-existent and two gates had to insist before it was honoured, which is what surfaced this. `security-auditor` and `ui-builder` carried no such section at all, though `ui-builder` writes production front-end code. The `instructions` array is now the single glob `.opencode/rules/*.md`, so all fifteen load for every agent and every subagent, and the nine `## Required Reading` sections are deleted rather than corrected — prose telling an agent a rule is absent from its context, when it is demonstrably present, trains it to distrust the context it has. This was verified before being relied on rather than after: `ui-builder` was dispatched with all tool use suppressed and asked to quote only what it could already see, and it returned the opening line of each of the four loaded rules and confirmed `observability.md` and `api-design.md` were absent — establishing both that `instructions` propagates into subagent sessions with full file bodies, and that the lazy eleven genuinely were not there. Two further properties come free: a new rule file is binding the moment it is committed, with no per-agent list to keep in sync — which is exactly where the old design drifted — and the directory must now hold rules only, because the glob takes everything matching. The narrower fix, adding the six gate-enforced rules to `tech-lead` alone, was rejected for relocating the judgement call instead of removing it, and for leaving `db-designer` authoring migrations that gates without `database.md` then review. The cost is real and accepted: roughly 20 KB (~5.1k tokens) of always-on instructions per session and per subagent dispatch, against upstream guidance to lazy-load references on demand — guidance that is correct for a large corpus and wrong for one smaller than several of the source files it governs. One consequence is that the claim in the profile-fiction entry below, that `configuration.md`, `caching.md` and `feature-flags.md` are "injected into every agent's context on every session", is true for the first time; when written it described the intent rather than the mechanism. Corrected in passing: the Blueprint compared the always-on budget against a stale ~12 KB, measured before four rule files grew the corpus by two thirds, and `.opencode/agents/devops-engineer.md` had an unterminated `` ```yaml `` fence at line 62 that had been swallowing every line after it, including the section this change deletes (EOP-000)

- **The replay that passes
 a compare-and-set is now a recorded decision, not a Javadoc comment.** Fixing the 500 above revealed something the design did not state anywhere: `advanceLeaderSeat` compare-and-sets the session row like the other four conditional writes, but it is **not** a replay guard, because when the seat that led a trick also wins it the statement writes the value it just compared against and is idempotent. Trick resolution is therefore serialised by `winner_play_id IS NULL` on the *trick* row — the one place in the design where the contended row is not the session row. An architecture gate observed that this slice had remediated one decision-in-a-Javadoc while introducing another, and that the new one qualified the very guarantee the remediation asserted; ADR-020 now carries both, including that the two second-resolution shapes (`OutOfTurnException` when the lead moved, `TrickAlreadyResolvedException` when it did not) do not subsume each other. Three comments that had inherited the false premise are corrected: `GameSessionJpaRepository.advanceLeaderSeat` claimed to be the double-resolution defence, `GameSessionJpaEntity` still said "the two writes that matter", and `TrickRepository.recordResolution`'s `@throws` contract claimed `SessionNotJoinableException` covered "the case where another request resolved this trick first", which it never did, while declaring neither exception that actually refuses a second resolution. A later review round found the same contract *incomplete* as well as wrong — `OutOfTurnException` and `HandNotDealtException` were undeclared on `openTrick` and `appendPlay` too, though the suite proved both thrown — so all three write methods now declare every exception their shared zero-rows helper can raise. Three further inherited counts were wrong and are corrected — `GameSessionJpaRepository` said "two update methods" where there are five, `GameSessionJpaEntity` named one statement of five as the one holding the row lock, and `GlobalExceptionHandler` said `handleOutOfTurn` returns 422 when it has always returned 409 — as is one more containment overstatement, this time the adapter class comment claiming `DataIntegrityViolationException` "stops at this class" when an unrecognised constraint name is deliberately rethrown so a constraint added later fails loudly. ADR-023's note beside the translation table stated its own count four times and got it wrong three: two, then three, then six. The answer was **nine** when this entry was written and is **ten** since EOP-14 Slice E, and it counts domain refusals rather than every exception those paths can raise — `IllegalStateException` and `IllegalArgumentException` are server faults, not refusals a caller earns, which is why it is ten and not twelve. Every miss had the same shape: the shared `sessionMoved` helper had five answers then and has six now, three kept being omitted while two were counted, and `OutOfTurnException` was omitted every time. The split is eight raised from a rows-affected count of zero (those six, plus a replayed resolution and a card no longer in the hand) and two from the `assertSeated` read; the six-count had also filed `CardNotInHandException` in the read group against its own stated criterion, when it comes from a conditional DELETE matching no row. Seven of the ten have an origin in ADR-020, not three as an earlier version said while naming the wrong three, and two of the ten are dual — both a row of the table and a non-constraint origin.

  Three careful readers wrote those counts and three careful reviewers passed them, so the invariant is now machine-checked. `TrickPlayExceptionOriginTest` **derives** the set from the adapter by position — classifying each construction by whether it sits inside a `DataIntegrityViolationException` translation or not — and asserts the ADR names *exactly* that set. The first version of that test did not: it compared the prose against a hard-coded list while its Javadoc claimed to derive from the source, so a tenth origin in the adapter would have left the suite green, and both its assertions ran the same direction so padding the prose passed. Three review gates caught the overclaim independently. A further round found that the second direction was still not equality — padding was checked against a closed list of four types, so a fabricated name passed — and that the correcting edit had **duplicated** the ADR paragraph rather than replacing it, leaving the exact sentence the previous round had rejected alive four lines below its own correction, with the new test anchored on the stale wording so deleting it would have turned the build red. Three gates found that independently. The duplicate is deleted, the test re-anchored, membership now read from the backticked tokens of the two blocks that enumerate the groups, and both groups located positionally rather than one being inferred by subtracting the other — which is how `CardNotInHandException` was mis-filed in the first place. Verified by mutation: a tenth origin added to `sessionMoved` fails with `Expected size: 9 but was: 10`; a fabricated name in a group block fails, where it previously passed; and removing a real origin from the prose fails. One limitation is recorded rather than claimed away — a type a block mentions twice, once enumerating and once in narrative, survives deletion of the enumerating mention alone, which is why the validating mutation removes every mention. A ninth round then found the whole approach one shape wrong, and the diagnosis is the part worth keeping: the paragraph asserts a **mapping** from exception name to mechanism, and every check built for it verified **aggregates** of that mapping — a total, group sizes, a union, a disjointness. Aggregates over a mapping leave every permutation green by construction, however many you add, which is why each round closed a real hole and left the next one open in the same place. The gate proved it by moving an origin between the two blocks with both headings untouched — the exact mis-filing the block records as its own past mistake — and the build stayed green. Each block's names must now equal its own derived group, which let the test method holding the aggregate assertions be deleted; the disjointness check among them was later reinstated, because per-group equality does not imply it. EOP-14 Slice E then ran that mutation for real rather than as an exercise: naming the third state of `current_leader_seat` added `HandCompleteException` as a tenth origin and an eighth member of the rows-affected group, and the build went red on `Expected size: 9 but was: 10` before a line of prose had been touched. The counts above are corrected to ten and eight and `EXPECTED_ORIGIN_COUNT` is now 10; a hard-coded list would have stayed green through exactly that change.

  Deliberately no tally here of attack shapes, assertion counts or which mutation trips what. Every such restatement in this entry has been wrong at least once, including the one that announced the counting problem fixed, and a tenth round found two more — a comment describing a disjointness check that had been deleted one commit earlier, and a claim that three attack shapes had previously passed when only one had. The arithmetic in the universe sentence was also wrong: nine domain refusals plus those two server-fault types is eleven, not thirteen.

  The adapter class comment was wrong in the same area — it claimed "every violation this class has been taught to name is translated, and no other", when the rule is three-way: five of the seven names it knows are translated, the two seat foreign keys marked `Backstop only` are recognised and deliberately rethrown as a 500 because `assertSeated` passed on the same facts moments earlier, and an unknown name is rethrown unlogged. It also cited `backstopFired` as where the unknown-name rethrow is argued; it is argued on `mentions`. A fourth containment claim — the third copy of the one corrected in ADR-023, this time in changeset `004`'s comment on `winner_play_id` — is corrected at the claim, and the over-strong "the only thing zero can mean" on the winner update is narrowed to the two things it can actually mean

- **The deal-once gate is now a recorded decision rather than a Javadoc comment.** Taking `current_leader_seat IS NULL` as the definition of "not yet dealt" makes the *session row*, not a count of `hand` rows, the point at which dealing serialises. That was decided in code during this slice with its entire rationale in a repository comment and no presence in `docs/`, even though ADR-020 owns session concurrency control and was amended by this same slice for lock ordering. ADR-020 now carries it: the statement, the rejected alternative (counting `hand` rows first is check-then-act across two statements, which only narrows the window and pays a round trip to do it), why the session row rather than the `hand` table (the deal must write the column anyway, and one contended row keeps the lock order a tree), and the cost — `HandAlreadyDealtException` acquires a second origin that the constraint-name translation table in ADR-023 structurally cannot cover, because this one is not a constraint violation. ADR-020's "declares exactly two writes" is also corrected; there are five

- **Liquibase had never run.** `liquibase-core` was on the classpath and `spring.liquibase.*` was configured, but Spring Boot 4 moved `LiquibaseAutoConfiguration` into a separate `spring-boot-liquibase` module that was never added. Every migration property was inert. Nothing failed, because an unread changelog and an empty one are indistinguishable until the first migration exists — which is exactly when this surfaced
- **The master changelog matched no files.** `includeAll` used an absolute `classpath:` path that silently resolved to zero changesets; it now resolves relative to the changelog file and is filtered to `.xml`, so the `.gitkeep` placeholder that previously sat in `changes/` cannot crash startup once the directory is genuinely read
- **Documentation named an uninstallable prerequisite.** `SETUP.md`, `docs/devops/local-development.md` and `.opencode/rules/performance-testing.md` all instructed the reader to install or launch Docker Desktop, which needs administrator rights this project does not have and a paid licence above an organisation-size threshold. All three now describe Colima (ADR-016)
- **The two Compose files shared one project namespace.** Both derived their project name from the directory, so each stack reported the other's containers as orphans and `docker compose down --remove-orphans` on either would have destroyed the other. They are now named `eop-app` and `eop-monitoring` explicitly
- **`.env.example` pointed k6 at a container hostname.** `INFLUXDB_URL` was `http://influxdb:8086`, which cannot resolve from the host where k6 runs, and omitted the `/k6` database path
- **The ADR index claimed four delivered decisions were unimplemented.** The OpenAPI contract, the RFC 9457 handler and the first Liquibase changeset all shipped with the card catalogue, and the index still read "No" for each; the front-end row read "No" since July. `docs/adr/README.md` now matches what is on disk
- **The attribution footer disclaimed the content it now has to attribute.** It read "The cards shown above are placeholders written for this project, not Microsoft's" — true for the six invented prompts, and false the moment the real deck was seeded. An attribution notice that denies authorship of the work it credits is worse than none
- **`docs/performance/TRENDS.md` held placeholder rows.** Every cell had been an em dash since the file was created, because the load test had never run against a container. It now carries measured figures and records the baseline reset
- **Configuration consented to an unauthenticated SQL console.** `application.yml` had said `spring.h2.console.enabled: true` since the Walking Skeleton, on the profile every local run uses.
