The H2 console is arbitrary SQL against the application's own database and accepts a caller-supplied JDBC URL — the CVE-2021-42392 shape — and there is no Spring Security dependency in this project that could have stood in front of it. It was never actually served: exactly as with Liquibase above, Spring Boot 4 moved the autoconfiguration into a separate module, `spring-boot-h2console`, that nothing here depends on, so no class ever read the property. That makes it a latent exposure rather than a live one — one transitive dependency and it arrives with the configuration already saying yes and no review to catch it. The property is now an explicit `false` with the reasoning attached rather than deleted, so whoever adds the module has to change a line that explains why it is off; `application-prod.yml` keeps its own redundant `false` as a second independent guard; and `H2ConsoleAbsentIntegrationTest` carries a tripwire on the autoconfiguration class so the build fails the day the module lands. The documentation that promised a console at `/h2-console` in a `dev` profile — a profile that has never existed either — is withdrawn in `docs/devops/local-development.md` and amended in ADR-008
- **The rule files taught two profiles that have never existed.** `.opencode/rules/configuration.md` stated the convention as `dev` (local), `test` (CI) and `prod` (production); only `prod` is real, alongside the unnamed default. `src/main/resources/` holds `application.yml` and `application-prod.yml` and nothing else, and the test suite activates no profile at all — it overrides the default through `src/test/resources/application.properties`. `.opencode/rules/caching.md` told the reader to disable caching in the `dev` profile and `.opencode/rules/feature-flags.md` to override flags with an `application-test.yml`; neither instruction could be carried out. These files are injected into every agent's context on every session, which makes them the most-read prose in the repository and the likeliest origin of the false consequence withdrawn from ADR-008 above — the fix there corrected the document that repeated the error, this one corrects the documents that generate it. The convention now names the two real profiles and says why it is two and not three, citing ADR-012, so the correction survives being read without the ADR to hand
- **The feature-flag rule prescribed a naming convention no flag has ever used.** It required `ff_{feature}_v{n}` under an `eop.feature-flags.*` root; the one flag that exists is `eop.features.session-lifecycle`, matching ADR-013. No code, configuration, test or ADR ever depended on the fictional form, so the rule has been rewritten to follow the shipped code rather than the code retrofitted to the rule. It had propagated to one other place that still carried it — the story template in `.opencode/agents/product-owner.md`, which named `ff_feature_name_v1` in its flag strategy and in both Gherkin scenarios, so every story written from it would have carried a flag name the rule now bans; that template is corrected here too. It also directed the reader to catalogue flags in `docs/feature-flags.md`, a file that does not exist — flags are recorded in `application.yml` beside their defaults and in ADR-013, and the rule now says so
- **The ADR index disagreed with two of the ADRs it indexes.** ADR-002 had been amended on 2026-07-27 and ADR-012 on 2026-08-10; the index showed the first as plainly `Accepted` and the second as `Accepted (amended)` with no date. An undated amendment reads as current, and a missing one reads as unamended, so the index was quietly overstating how settled two decisions were. Both rows now carry the substance and the date. Because the index has drifted twice for the same reason — keeping it in step is step 5 of a manual checklist — `AdrIndexConsistencyTest` now fails the build when an ADR has no index row, a row links to a missing file, an ADR does not carry exactly one `**Status:**` line to compare against, a status word disagrees, or a dated amendment reaches the index without its date. The invariants are deliberately narrow: a test strict enough to dictate wording would be worked around rather than obeyed
- **`X-Forwarded-For` was believed from any caller, so the join-code throttle could be bypassed by rotating one header.** The application read the last entry of the header and used it as the client address — the key the join-attempt limiter counts failures against. The reasoning was that Caddy is the only published entry point (ADR-017) and Caddy appends to the header, so the last entry is the one the proxy wrote: sound, but valid only if the peer really is the proxy, and nothing checked that. Reached directly — by `./mvnw spring-boot:run` on port 8080, from a sibling container on the Compose network, or through any later change that publishes the application port — a caller supplied its own rate-limiter key, and a fresh value per request meant a fresh empty bucket per request, so the ten-failures-per-address limit never fired. The per-code counter could not compensate, because a code-guessing attacker uses a different code every time. ADR-019 designates that limiter a *primary* security control rather than defence in depth — a six-character join code is thirty bits, unguessable only while guessing is slow — so this was not a thinned-out layer but the removal of the control the entropy budget was calculated against (EOP-26, ADR-021)
- **Trust in a forwarding header is now an explicit, default-denied allow-list.** `eop.web.trusted-proxies` holds literal addresses or canonical CIDR blocks; the header is ignored entirely unless the peer address is on that list, and the default is empty, so an application deployed without a thought about forwarding headers trusts nobody and keys on the peer. A malformed or non-canonical entry fails startup rather than being skipped — silently masking `10.0.0.5/8` to `10.0.0.0/8` would trust sixteen million hosts where the author plainly meant one. Every resolved address is canonicalised before it becomes a map key, because two spellings of one address are two buckets, which is the same defect arriving by another route. `server.forward-headers-strategy` is pinned to `none` in both profiles so neither Tomcat's `RemoteIpValve` nor Spring's `ForwardedHeaderFilter` can launder the header into `getRemoteAddr()` behind the check: their default trust lists cover `172.16.0.0/12`, which contains this project's own Compose subnet. The deployed value sits beside the proxy's pinned container address in `compose.app.yml`, so the two halves of one fact cannot drift apart (EOP-26, ADR-021)
- **A green build had been evidence that the bug was still present.** The integration suite's `freshAddress()` helper gave each test its own throttle bucket by setting `X-Forwarded-For` — a test choosing its own rate-limiter key from outside the process, which is precisely the attack. The suite had been getting its isolation *from the vulnerability*, so closing the hole broke sixteen previously-passing tests. Trusting `127.0.0.1` suite-wide would have let all sixteen pass unchanged and was rejected: it would have re-opened the hole everywhere in the suite except in the tests that assert it is closed. The helper is now `unusedAddressHint()`, documented as having no effect, and the throttling tests get a fresh limiter from `@DirtiesContext` instead of from a forged header (EOP-26, ADR-021)
- **The 503 for an exhausted join-code budget justified logging a full stack trace by arithmetic alone, and nothing defended the arithmetic (EOP-56, [ADR-019](docs/adr/ADR-019-session-lifecycle-and-join-codes.md), [ADR-033](docs/adr/ADR-033-session-creation-rate-limit-and-body-size-cap.md)).** `GlobalExceptionHandler.handleJoinCodeUnavailable` logs at `WARN` *with the throwable*, and both it and ADR-019 rested that choice on the path being unprovokable — a run of independent collisions across the whole attempt budget is vanishingly unlikely. That was true, and it is still true, but it was contingent on three constants nobody had pinned: `MAXIMUM_CODE_ATTEMPTS`, the join-code alphabet and the code length. Shorten the code or narrow the alphabet and a caller-reachable path starts emitting stack traces, with nothing in the build to say the justification had been withdrawn. **The control that closes it already existed and was simply never cited.** EOP-19 added a per-address creation rate limit (ADR-033), and `SessionController` consults it *before* `CreateSessionUseCase` runs, so every request that reaches the join-code retry loop has already passed it; the rate at which this `WARN` can be emitted is therefore bounded by the configured per-address allowance and by the limiter's tracked-key ceiling, which fails closed on an unrecognised address once full. Both the handler Javadoc and ADR-019 now say which half is load-bearing: the arithmetic is why an occurrence is *evidence worth logging*, the limiter is why it cannot become a *flood*, and the limiter is the half that survives a change to any of the three constants. **The scope difference is stated rather than collapsed** — the limiter is keyed per address while the occupied fraction of the code space is global, so it bounds the rate of this log line and not the number of live lobbies. **The second direction is a build gate, and its motivation is a measured failure rather than a hypothetical.** The width has already drifted once: EOP-24 widened join codes from six characters to eight, and keeping prose in step meant a hand-audited sweep amending ADR-019, ADR-021, ADR-023 and ADR-033 wherever each quoted the superseded figure. `JoinCodeSpaceClaimsTest` now derives the width from `JoinCode.ALPHABET` and `JoinCode.LENGTH` and fails the build if the live claims stop quoting the derived figure, so that sweep is announced instead of remembered. It works in two layers: three anchored checks on the sentences whose argument depends on the figure, each anchor asserted to occur exactly once so a rewording fails rather than quietly matching nothing, and a repository-wide sweep of every `.java`, `.md` and `.yml` file under `src/main/java`, `docs/architecture`, `docs/adr` and `docs/api` that requires every English-word bit figure either to equal the derived width or to sit on a seven-entry allowlist of deliberately historical mentions. Proved in both directions rather than only green: five tests pass on the tree as it stands; temporarily setting `LENGTH` to 7 fails all five and enumerates roughly twenty-three stale sites, which is the hand-audit EOP-24 performed, now printed by the build; and inserting a bogus allowlist entry also fails, because an exemption whose passage no longer exists is reported and must be deleted rather than left to widen what the sweep tolerates. **The ticket's own arithmetic is corrected here.** EOP-56 gives the collision probability as `(N / 2^30)^5`; the exponent is right and the base is not, because a thirty-two character alphabet over eight characters is 2^40, so it is `(N / 2^40)^5` — the ticket understates the code space by a factor of 1024. ADR-019's "forty bits wide" was always correct, and the new gate is what stops the ticket's version being copied back in later. **Three of the ticket's four asks were already discharged by EOP-19** — the create path is rate-limited, nineteen unit tests and an integration test assert the limit fires, and ADR-033 already carries the paragraph explaining why successes are counted rather than failures — so what shipped under EOP-56 is the fourth: the justification, plus the gate that keeps it honest. **Four limits stated plainly.** No behaviour changed: the status, the `Retry-After` header and the log level are all as EOP-17 left them. The gate pins two of the three constants, not three, because `MAXIMUM_CODE_ATTEMPTS` is private to `CreateSessionUseCase` and reachable only by reflection, so the attempt budget remains a reviewer's concern. It verifies that prose and constants *agree*, never that forty bits is *adequate* — deciding that is ADR-019's job. And it reads English number words only, so a figure written as `2^40` or `40 bits` passes unexamined — the sweep is a net with a known mesh, not a proof — while the seven allowlist entries remain a human judgement that each named mention really is historical. **The completeness claim in this entry's first draft was false and is worth recording as such.** It said the gate pinned three sites and that those were the complete set of live claims, reasoning that ADR-023 and ADR-033 carry no width figure and that ADR-021's only mention is a quotation of the superseded "thirty bits" wording. The premises are true; the conclusion was not, because roughly a dozen further live claims sit in the domain, the ports, the adapters, the C4 model and the runtime view. The sweep was added to make the claim true rather than to narrow the wording, and it immediately caught two allowlist entries naming a file that does not exist by that name — so the phrase-anchoring the original reasoning defended is still there, but as the first of two layers rather than as the whole gate. **The same thing then happened a second time, one layer up, and is recorded for the same reason.** With the sweep in place and all five review gates passed, `@tester-api` observed that `docs/api/openapi.yml` states the width three times — `:1161`, `:2011`, `:2049` — and sat outside the sweep's roots, so the API contract was the one place a stale figure could still survive the gate built to catch it. It was graded a documented limitation rather than a defect and would have been legitimate to defer; it was closed instead, by adding `.yml` to the swept extensions and `docs/api` to the roots, because a contract is what a client integrates against and is the last place to leave a figure nobody checks. One of those three mentions wraps across lines and so is invisible to a line-based `grep`, which finds two where there are three — the second time in this story that flattening changed the count, the first being ADR-021, where one visible mention proved to be four. **That miscount was itself caught at the gate**, in the first draft of this entry and of the ADR paragraph, both of which said two mentions wrapped rather than one — a story about prose that misstates arithmetic, doing it again in the paragraph announcing the fix

### Security

- **The k6 CI container no longer runs in the runner's network namespace (EOP-160, ADR-055 amended 2026-09-04).** `docker run --network host` gave the canary every host port, every loopback service on the runner and the ability to bind host ports, for a step whose only need is outbound HTTPS to one reverse proxy; @security-auditor raised it as MEDIUM during EOP-155's gate round, acceptable on an ephemeral runner but not minimal. It now joins Caddy's own namespace with `--network container:eop-caddy` and addresses `https://localhost:8080`. This is strictly narrower and confers nothing new — the host could already route to the Compose bridge subnet, so the move *drops* host loopback and port-binding while adding no destination. The address must stay `localhost`: `ui/Caddyfile` serves `tls internal` for only `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` and `[::1]`, and a non-empty SNI outside that set is aborted server-side with `tlsv1 alert internal error`, which `default_sni` does not rescue and `--insecure-skip-tls-verify` cannot either — so addressing Caddy by Compose service name is dead at the TLS layer, and an IP literal handshakes only to have its Host header match no site block and return an empty 200. Verified locally against a minimal Caddy stand-in started with no published ports at all, so the namespace join was the only possible route: 663/663 checks and all three CI thresholds green with the real pinned image. Residual, unchanged rather than introduced: k6 can still reach `app:8080` and `postgres:5432` directly over the Compose network, exactly as it could under `--network host`
- **Embedded Tomcat bumped 11.0.22 → 11.0.25, closing three CRITICAL CVEs that had turned `main` red (EOP-189, ADR-050).** `tools/supply-chain/scan-dependencies.sh` exited 1 on three unallowlisted CRITICAL findings in `org.apache.tomcat.embed:tomcat-embed-core`: CVE-2026-65182 (security constraint bypass via improper access control), CVE-2026-65905 (authentication bypass via limited replay attack in the DIGEST authenticator) and CVE-2026-68525 (unauthorized resource access via FORM authentication bypass). **They were not introduced by any story — they were freshly disclosed against unchanged code**, which is visible in the CI history rather than asserted: `main`'s last two runs failed on `dependency-cve` while the three before them passed, and `git diff --stat main -- pom.xml` was empty for the branch that first tripped over them. This was found by EOP-188's `@dependency-vulnerability` gate rejecting a documentation-only change, which is the gate behaving correctly — it adjudicates the tree as it stands, not the diff. **The fix is one line and it is the fix ADR-050 mandates**, not an allowlist entry: `<tomcat.version>11.0.25</tomcat.version>` beside the four existing CVE-driven overrides of parent-managed versions in `pom.xml`, whose own comment block states "PREFER THE UPGRADE". One property governs the whole `org.apache.tomcat.embed` family, so `dependency:list` was checked rather than assumed — `tomcat-embed-core`, `-el` and `-websocket` all resolve coherently at 11.0.25, reached transitively through `spring-boot-starter-web → -starter-tomcat`. **Two of the three advisories are in fact unreachable here, and that is deliberately recorded as an exploitability note rather than used as an argument**: the application ships no authentication at all (ADR-015), so it has no DIGEST authenticator and no FORM login to bypass. Accepting a CRITICAL on that basis when an in-range patch exists would invert ADR-050's policy, and a `reachability` trace for `accepted-cves.json` would have amounted to arguing that an authenticator which does not exist cannot be bypassed. `tools/supply-chain/accepted-cves.json` is therefore untouched and still ships `"advisories": {}` — the correct steady state. The scan now exits 0 with no HIGH or CRITICAL findings, and none at MEDIUM or LOW either, across both manifests. One stale figure in the surrounding prose was corrected in the same commit: the `postgresql` entry described itself as the only directly-declared dependency "of the six", the count of findings the block closed at EOP-89; three more advisories make nine. Both of that sentence's claims survive unchanged — postgresql is still the only HIGH among them and still the only dependency `pom.xml` declares directly. **The override is a rot liability on the same terms as the other four and nothing automated will catch it**: delete `tomcat.version` once `./mvnw help:effective-pom` shows the Boot parent's own managed version has reached 11.0.25, because a stale override stops being a fix and becomes a ceiling holding the library back.
- **The two RNG-backed authorisation primitives now have tests that fail if their `SecureRandom` seeding is downgraded (EOP-175).** Neither `SecureRandomJoinCodeGenerator` nor `SecureRandomIdentityTokenGenerator` had any dedicated test: `SecureRandomDeckShufflerTest` was the only test in that package, and `grep -rn` found zero references to either generator anywhere under `src/test/`. Replacing `new SecureRandom()` with `new java.util.Random()` in both left the whole suite green at 1371 tests. That matters because these are the application's only two authorisation primitives — the join code is the sole gate on entering a session (40 bits of entropy behind `InMemoryJoinAttemptLimiter`, ADR-019, an argument that assumes the code is unguessable), and the identity token is the sole credential distinguishing one player from another and a facilitator from a player (ADR-015), so a predictable token is impersonation up to and including privilege escalation to facilitator. `java.util.Random` is a 48-bit linear congruential generator whose internal state is recoverable from a handful of outputs, against which a per-code attempt limiter is irrelevant because no guessing is required. **The code was already correct — this closes a regression-protection gap, not a live vulnerability.** Two new test classes add nine tests. Behaviourally they pin the observable contract each generator owes its caller: every join code is exactly `JoinCode.LENGTH` characters and draws from exactly `JoinCode.ALPHABET`, asserted as set equality in both directions so a drifted alphabet *and* an off-by-one `nextInt` bound both fail; every token is 43 characters, matches `[A-Za-z0-9_-]+` with no padding, and base64url-decodes back to exactly 32 bytes, which pins the generator's private `ENTROPY_BYTES` through its output. Each also draws a thousand values and asserts no duplicate, justified arithmetically in the class Javadoc rather than left as a vibe (birthday bound ≈ 4.5e-7 over 32^8; symbol-absence ≈ (31/32)^8000). **None of that can distinguish `SecureRandom` from `Random`, which is the whole difficulty**, and the property is therefore asserted the one way it can be: a source-text assertion on the generator's own `.java` file, mirroring the pattern `IdentityTokenHashTest.shouldCompareInConstantTime` already established for constant-time comparison. Each generator's source must contain both `private final SecureRandom random;` and `this.random = new SecureRandom();` and must not contain `new Random()` or `new java.util.Random(`. Asserting the field *type* as well as the instantiation is deliberate: `SecureRandom extends Random`, so the narrower declared type cannot hold a plain `Random` and a real downgrade takes three coordinated edits — the assertion pair catches any of them. Each carries an `.as(…)` rationale long enough that the surefire failure message explains why the literal matters, since a bare `contains` failure would read as pedantry to whoever next tries to make it pass. **The gate was negative-tested rather than assumed**, per the ticket's own "an assertion that never fails is not coverage": both generators were downgraded to `java.util.Random` and `./mvnw verify` went red with exactly two failures, both of them the new source-text assertions and nothing else, then reverted. Three limits stated plainly. A source-text assertion is a text match, not a semantic one, so it proves the literal is present and not that the field it initialises is the one `nextJoinCode` and `nextToken` actually read — a sufficiently determined edit could satisfy both assertions and still be wrong; and its inverse, a rename of the field or a move to constructor injection, turns the build red on a change that is not a downgrade, which is the intended tradeoff and the reason the rationale is written into the assertion. The probabilistic assertions are non-deterministic by construction: they are safe by many orders of magnitude, but "safe" is not "impossible". And the test files hardcode paths into `src/main/java`, so moving either generator breaks its own test — an acceptable coupling for a gate whose entire purpose is to be coupled to one line of one file.
- **No read route in the API had a rate limit at any layer, so one participant could recompute the most expensive query in the application as fast as their machine allowed (EOP-88, ADR-051).** Two limiters existed and both guarded writes: `InMemorySessionCreationLimiter` (ADR-033) and `InMemoryJoinAttemptLimiter` (ADR-019). Nothing counted reads, and there was no servlet filter, no `HandlerInterceptor` and no `WebMvcConfigurer` anywhere in `src/main/java` to hang one on — `grep -rlE 'OncePerRequestFilter|HandlerInterceptor|addInterceptors|FilterRegistrationBean|WebMvcConfigurer' src/main/java` returned nothing — and no `rate_limit` directive in `ui/Caddyfile`, Caddy's limiter being a third-party plugin absent from the image. The route that made it worth fixing is `GET /api/v1/sessions/{sessionId}/leaderboard`: it resolves the player, loads the session, loads the game result, loads up to sixty-six trick plays and recomputes a whole `ScoreSheet`, because the score is derived from play rather than accumulated (ADR-030). EOP-82 then gave the browser a `Retry loading results` button with a pending guard but no attempt cap, so a human holding it down was a supported way to drive that recomputation in a loop. **The control is a `HandlerInterceptor`, and the layer was the decision rather than an implementation detail.** A servlet filter runs outside the `DispatcherServlet`, so a `RateLimitedException` thrown there never reaches the single `@ControllerAdvice` and the RFC 9457 body would have to be hand-serialised in the filter — forbidden by `.opencode/rules/error-handling.md` and a duplicate of `GlobalExceptionHandler` besides. `preHandle` runs inside it, so refusal reuses the existing `RateLimitedException` mapping and inherits 429, `application/problem+json` and `Retry-After` with **no new exception type and no new handler**; `GlobalExceptionHandlerTest` already covered that mapping, so one half of the ticket's second acceptance criterion was satisfied before the story began and was verified rather than reimplemented. `ReadRateLimitInterceptor` is registered by `WebInterceptorConfiguration` — the application's first `WebMvcConfigurer` — on `GET` and `HEAD` under `/api/v1/**`, a pattern rather than the five routes the ticket enumerated, so a route added later is covered without anyone remembering to add it. `HEAD` counts because it reaches the same handler and costs the same work. **The limiter key is `ClientAddressResolver.of(request)` and nothing else, which is the point of the story rather than a detail of it.** EOP-26/ADR-021 exists because the join throttle once keyed on a caller-supplied `X-Forwarded-For`, so a fresh header value bought a fresh empty bucket and ADR-019's *primary* security control counted nothing; `eop.web.trusted-proxies` now defaults to empty so the header is ignored outright, with `server.forward-headers-strategy: none` as a second independent guard. `ReadRateLimitInterceptorTest` asserts over twelve candidate headers — including `X-Forwarded-For` rotated on every request, `Forwarded`, `CF-Connecting-IP`, `True-Client-IP` and `X-EoP-Player-Token` — that none of them moves an exhausted caller into a new bucket or charges another caller's allowance. **The player token was considered as the key and rejected on a security argument, not a convenience one:** the counter fails closed when its key table saturates (ADR-033), and a caller can mint unlimited distinct bogus tokens, so a token-derived key would let one attacker fill the table and have the limiter refuse everybody — a spoofable identifier must not select the bucket a fail-closed control counts in. The cost of keying on the address is that players behind one NAT share a budget, which the limit absorbs. Counting is done by a new package-private `SlidingWindowCounter`: a sixty-second window per key, prune-check-insert under one lock per deque so two threads at `limit - 1` cannot both pass, a one-second floor on the reported `Retry-After` because `Retry-After: 0` reads as no wait at all, and a refused call that is not itself counted so a throttled caller cannot extend its own penalty. `ReadRateLimitProperties` binds `eop.web.read-rate-limit` with `limit: 300` and `max-tracked-keys: 10000`; 300 is sized off the SSE doorbell (ADR-014), where each of roughly sixty-six trick events wakes about two re-fetches per client and a NAT'd three-player household peaks near a hundred reads a minute. It is a separate `@ConfigurationProperties` record from `TrustedProxyProperties` because a third unrelated limit would make that name actively misleading. **Test isolation deliberately does not forge the key**, which the ticket named as an acceptance criterion for the reason above: the suite raises the limit to `Integer.MAX_VALUE` in `src/test/resources/application.properties` because every integration test reads under `/api/v1/**` from the same `127.0.0.1` through one shared context, and `ReadRateLimitIntegrationTest` lowers it to 3 for its own context with `@SpringBootTest(properties = …)` plus `@DirtiesContext`. That test asserts the problem+json body, and — with the budget spent — that `GET /api/v1/sessions/{random uuid}/leaderboard` answers **429 rather than 403 or 404**, which proves the named route is covered without standing up a completed game. Client side, `GameOverScreen` now caps retries at five with cooldowns of 1, 2, 4, 8 and 16 seconds (31 seconds of enforced waiting) and then replaces the button with guidance to reload; five is ample for the EOP-86 race in which the game-completed event outruns the result row, and it is defence in depth on the one control the client owns — the server limit is the control. Six things are recorded rather than glossed. The counters are in memory and per-instance, so a restart forgets them and a second instance would double the effective limit — acceptable while ADR-012 keeps one instance and while a restart is a deployment rather than something an attacker can trigger. Address rotation still sidesteps a per-address window, so this is a deterrent and not an absolute cap. **The SSE stream is deliberately excluded and its reconnect path is therefore still unlimited**, because `preHandle` runs again on the `ASYNC` dispatch and would double-count every stream, and because a long-lived stream is not a request rate — its cost is bounded by ADR-034's subscriber cap and the ten-minute emitter timeout instead. The public card catalogue shares one budget with the token-bearing reads. The sliding-window algorithm now exists three times, because migrating the creation and join limiters onto the shared counter would have put a refactor of a primary security control and a brand-new limiter in the same review; the migration is filed as a follow-up. And because the suite raises the limit, **no test asserts that production runs with 300** — the shipped default is guarded by review, not by the build.
- **The `havingValue = "true"` mandate is now a build gate rather than reviewer attention (EOP-50, ADR-052).** ADR-013 has required it in prose since the flag mechanism was chosen, and it was violated twice in two independent commits while the ADR already said so — `TrickController` (fixed in `56c31b1`, EOP-14 slice D) and `SessionController` plus four unconditional use-case beans (fixed in `34d30d7`/`50850d1`, EOP-48) — each caught by a reviewer rather than by the build. The defect it prevents is fail-*open*: `OnPropertyCondition$Spec.isMatch` reduces, when `havingValue` is empty, to `!"false".equalsIgnoreCase(value)`, so the loose form enables the feature for every present value but the literal `false` — `off`, `no`, `0`, `disabled`, and the empty string an `EOP_FEATURES_SOMETHING=` export produces. `off` is the worst case, being simultaneously the spelling an operator reaches for as a kill switch and a YAML 1.1 boolean false. `matchIfMissing` already defaults `false`, so an *absent* property was never the hole **in the loose form** — but the attribute is audited too, as a second and independent invariant, and ADR-013 was amended the same day to carry the prohibition alongside the `havingValue` mandate rather than leaving the flag rules split across two ADRs. `@ConditionalOnProperty(name = "…", havingValue = "true", matchIfMissing = true)` satisfies the mandate to the letter while enabling the feature whenever the property is absent, inverting the fail-closed default that an unset flag reads as disabled; it is in one respect worse than a loose `havingValue`, which at least needs an operator to set the property to something, whereas this fires with no operator action at all. That half was added during this story's own review round, on @security-auditor's finding that a gate asserting `havingValue` alone would admit a configuration defeating its own purpose; the ADR-013 amendment followed from @architecture-guardian's gate, which called it split authority for the enforcement ADR to own an invariant the mandate ADR did not state. No site sets `matchIfMissing` today, so it is preventive on the same terms as the rest of the gate, and the two invariants were confirmed genuinely independent rather than assumed so — against a loosened site, exactly one of the four tests failed and the `havingValue` assertion passed untouched. `ConditionalOnPropertyHavingValueTest` now walks the compiled `.class` files under `target/classes` through Spring's own ASM metadata reader — `spring-core` is already on the test classpath transitively, so **no new dependency was added**; ArchUnit was the close call and is recorded in ADR-052 as the natural upgrade should a second architectural invariant ever need enforcing, while a docs-style text test (brittle against annotation formatting) and a Checkstyle `regexp` module (`checkstyle.xml` has no such module today, and text cannot see annotation defaults) were rejected. Reading bytecode rather than source is what makes it robust: multi-line annotations, attribute order and comments cannot defeat it, both attribute spellings in use here resolve to the same effective key, and an *omitted* `havingValue` reads back as `""` so a violation is caught by a positive comparison against `"true"` rather than by proving text absent. Two things to be honest about. The gate is **preventive, not corrective** — all 21 annotation sites in `src/main/` already complied and no live defect was fixed (the ticket's baseline of eleven sites was stale: `trick-play` and `game-over` landed after it was filed). And only the first half of the ADR-013 mandate is mechanised: bean-level repetition — that a flag is repeated on every bean which opens or mutates the flagged state — is **not** mechanically checkable, because "opens or mutates" is a semantic judgement no signature encodes, the carve-outs are justified in javadoc prose, and mechanising it would recreate the drift-prone per-flag bean register that EOP-48's security analysis rejected; ADR-052 states that asymmetry openly rather than implying full automation. Startup validation of `eop.features.**` *values* remains deliberately out of scope on @security-auditor's EOP-48 reasoning: the residual failure modes all fail closed and loudly, a validator would turn a self-announcing operability annoyance into a boot crash, and `@ConditionalOnProperty` is evaluated before any `@ConfigurationProperties` bean is bound so a `@Validated` class could not govern these keys anyway. The check was proven to fail before being trusted, against three deliberately loosened sites — two for the `havingValue` invariant, one per discovery path and one per defect shape, and one more for `matchIfMissing` — with the failure output for each recorded in the pull request. `.opencode/rules/feature-flags.md`, which every agent reads and which contained no occurrence of the string `havingValue`, now carries the rule and the enforcement asymmetry.
- **Which feature flags exist is now derived from the code and the shipped config rather than from a hand-written list, and every flag must declare its intended state, its owner and its expiry (EOP-84, ADR-053).** EOP-82 shipped a finished, fully tested feature dark for two stories: `eop.features.game-over` stayed `false` in `application.yml` while the slice behind it was complete and merged, the routes answered the framework's own 404, and the build stayed green throughout. The reason nothing caught it is structural and has not been removed — `.opencode/rules/feature-flags.md` requires every flag pinned `true` in `src/test/resources/application.properties`, because a suite running with a feature off would be testing its absence, so the Spring `Environment` in every `@SpringBootTest` resolves the override and never the shipped value. EOP-27's fix for the identical `spring.h2.console.enabled` trap was to remove the pin; that remedy is unavailable here by construction. ADR-042 mitigated it with `ShippedFeatureFlagDefaultsTest`, which read `application.yml` off the classpath as a file — the right vantage point — but asserted **three hand-named keys**, and recorded in the same breath that this was "an interim workaround, not the architectural answer": a fourth flag was invisible to it, so the next feature merged dark would have shipped exactly as quietly. It detected the recurrence of one instance, not the class of fault. That test is now deleted. `FeatureFlagRegistryTest` derives the flag set **three independent ways** and requires all three to be equal: the keys under `eop.features` in the shipped `application.yml`, read as a `ClassPathResource` through `YamlPropertiesFactoryBean` with no Spring context; every `@ConditionalOnProperty` in `target/classes` whose resolved key sits in that namespace, read through Spring's `SimpleMetadataReaderFactory` exactly as ADR-052's scanner does; and the entries in a new `src/test/resources/feature-flag-registry.yml`. Each pairing catches a different fault — a YAML key with no registry entry is an undeclared flag whose intended state nobody wrote down, an annotation key in neither the YAML nor the registry is the fail-closed trap itself (no declared default, so the flag ships OFF silently), and an entry with neither is a stale or misspelled declaration describing protection that is not there. **The direction of the assertion is what changed.** The old guard was silent unless someone remembered to add a line; the new one fails *until* someone declares intent, which is what makes it the tripwire EOP-27 built rather than a snapshot of today's positions. The registry declares four fields per flag and all four are mandatory, checked in **both** directions so a surplus field fails as loudly as a missing one — that is what catches a typo, since `shipped-defualt` would otherwise read as an absent `shipped-default` plus a harmless extra key. Field **values** are type-checked too, and this was the one substantive change the gate round produced: checking the field set catches a misspelled field *name* but never a misspelled *value*, and a tolerant `Boolean.TRUE.equals` read answered `false` for anything that was not the boolean `true` — so `shipped-default: flase` arrived as a `String` and read back as a deliberate declaration that the flag ships dark, a sentence nobody wrote. Worse, the same typo in `application.yml` would *agree* with it, since Spring treats every value but the literal `true` as disabled, so the pair would pass the guard while the flag shipped OFF — the EOP-82 fault reintroduced through the mechanism built to catch it. A fail-closed control must not silently reinterpret a mistake as an intention, so `shipped-default` must now be an unquoted YAML boolean and `expiry` a quoted string, each asserted with the fix named in the message. `shipped-default` is *asserted* against the YAML rather than documenting it, so changing a flag's shipped value now means changing the registry in the same commit and the change is a recorded decision instead of an edited literal. `owner-story` must match `EOP-\d+`. `expiry` takes a quoted ISO date or the literal `null` but **may not be omitted**, which is the point: `null` means no expiry was ever authorised and is a legitimate resting state, whereas an absent field would let a new flag inherit no-expiry by silence — the habit ADR-042 condemned. `game-over` carries the 2026-09-18 date ADR-042 wrote in prose, now enforced; `session-lifecycle` and `trick-play` carry `null`, because ADR-042 condemned inventing dates no decision authorised and this story did not invent two. Unquoted dates are rejected rather than guessed at, since YAML resolves `2026-09-18` to a `java.util.Date` and the guard would then compare a date nobody wrote, and expiry is evaluated against `LocalDate.now(ZoneOffset.UTC)` so the verdict does not depend on which side of midnight the build ran. Ten tests, zero new dependencies — SnakeYAML is already on the classpath through `spring-boot-starter`. All five acceptance criteria were demonstrated by **deliberate mutation**, each failing exactly one test and no others: a new YAML key with no entry, a key and entry with no annotation, a shipped value disagreeing with declared intent, an expiry moved one day into the past, and an omitted `expiry` field. Three vacuity guards exist because two empty sets compare equal, and they earned their place within minutes: the first implementation read the YAML through `Properties.stringPropertyNames()`, which contractually admits a key only when *its value is also a String*, so Spring's parsed booleans were silently dropped, the derivation returned empty, and the agreement assertion reported the registry's own three entries as missing while `getProperty` kept working perfectly. Six things are recorded rather than glossed. **`owner-story` and `shipped-default` are checked for shape and for agreement, never for truth** — nothing verifies that EOP-25 is a real issue or that it is the story which last moved that flag, so a plausible wrong value passes, the same bound the documentation gates in `src/test/java/org/maglez/eop/docs/` operate under. The registry is still hand-editable and this does not remove the need for review; what it removes is the *silent* case, and three coordinated edits are visible to a reviewer in a way a missing line in a hand-written list was not. **`game-over`'s expiry will turn `main` red on a calendar boundary in a project that deploys every passing commit**, blocking unrelated work; that is accepted as the intended signal on the `tools/supply-chain/accepted-advisories.json` precedent of failing in both directions deliberately, mitigated only by the date being visible in the registry and extendable under review before it passes. ADR-042's second-order trap on removal is still reviewer-enforced — nothing detects that `GameOverControllerDisabledIntegrationTest` has been left asserting an unreachable condition once the flag and its guards are gone, though the expiry failure guarantees somebody looks by 2026-09-19 and the registry comment repeats the warning. ADR-037's front-end build-time `VITE_*` flags are a different mechanism in a different toolchain and remain entirely uncovered, so a front-end feature can still ship dark exactly as `game-over` did. And **the masking trap itself is untouched**: flags stay pinned ON in test resources and will keep hiding the shipped default from any `Environment` lookup, so ADR-053 routes around the trap rather than removing it and any future guard in this area must read the shipped artefact. `.opencode/rules/feature-flags.md`, whose closing bullet flatly stated "there is no separate flag catalogue document", now excepts a machine-read registry that gates the build — the prohibition targets prose that drifts silently, and drift is precisely what this file cannot do.
- **Springdoc disabled by default — fail-closed configuration (EOP-38, ADR-049).** `springdoc.api-docs.enabled: false` and `springdoc.swagger-ui.enabled: false` moved from `application-prod.yml` into `application.yml`, so the default profile is now secure by default. Local development opts in via `SPRINGDOC_APIDOCS_ENABLED=true` and `SPRINGDOC_SWAGGERUI_ENABLED=true` in `.env.example`. `application-prod.yml` keeps a redundant second guard. This satisfies `.opencode/rules/security.md`'s "Fail securely — default-denied access, explicit allow-lists" and adds a second, independent mechanism to ADR-047's artifact-layer exclusion. The ticket's claim about `server.error.*` was disproven — Spring Boot has defaulted those to false since 2.3, and the project bypasses `BasicErrorController` via its RFC 9457 `@ControllerAdvice`.
- **CVE scanning for both application dependency trees, and six real vulnerabilities fixed (EOP-89, ADR-050).** There was previously no dependency-vulnerability scanning anywhere in the build or CI — no OWASP dependency-check, no Trivy, no Snyk, no `npm audit` step, no CodeQL, no dependency-review action, no SBOM. `maven-enforcer-plugin` was the nearest thing and is not close: it enforces dependency *convergence* and a banned-coordinates list, which stops two versions of one library reaching the classpath and says nothing about whether the single converged version has a published CVE. The `bannedDependencies` entry for `log4j:log4j` illustrates the gap exactly — those are the 1.x coordinates, so it never saw `org.apache.logging.log4j:log4j-api` 2.25.4 at depth 3 of the compile tree. `tools/supply-chain/audit-plugins.sh` did gate on high and critical advisories, but its subject is the seven OpenCode developer plugins, not the deployed application. A new `dependency-cve` job in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` now runs `tools/supply-chain/scan-dependencies.sh`, scanning `pom.xml` and `ui/package-lock.json` with Trivy pinned by version *and* by the SHA-256 of the release asset rather than installed through a third-party action — which detects a tampered mirror and does not prove the release is benign. Gating is HIGH and CRITICAL only, matching `audit-plugins.sh` and deliberately diverging from the SpotBugs high-and-medium posture in `build-quality.md`: a medium SpotBugs finding is in our own code and always actionable, a medium CVE in a third-party transitive is frequently unreachable, often has no in-range fix, and arrives at a volume set by other people's release cadence. The first run was not a formality — it found six findings, all compile or runtime scope, including a HIGH: CVE-2026-54291 in `org.postgresql:postgresql` 42.7.11, a man-in-the-middle protection bypass via SCRAM-SHA-256-PLUS downgrade, in the one dependency of the six that `pom.xml` declares directly. All four vulnerable versions proved to be Spring Boot parent-managed properties, so all six were closed by patch-level overrides (`postgresql.version` 42.7.12, `jackson-2-bom.version` 2.21.5, `jackson-bom.version` 3.1.5, `log4j2.version` 2.25.5) rather than suppressed, and `tools/supply-chain/accepted-cves.json` therefore ships empty — a gate that is red the day it lands gets switched off rather than fixed. That allowlist follows the house model rather than `.trivyignore.yaml` because it fails in both directions: an unlisted HIGH fails, a listed entry no longer reported fails with an instruction to delete it, and a listed entry whose module or severity drifted fails. Entries are keyed `ID@module`, a divergence from `accepted-advisories.json`'s bare-GHSA keying forced by a measured collision — CVE-2026-59889 appeared twice in one scan, against Jackson 2 and Jackson 3 under different groupIds, so a bare-ID key would have suppressed both when only one had been traced. Every entry needs a reachability trace, a reviewer, a date and an `expires`, the last being the one field `accepted-advisories.json` lacks. The failure policy was demonstrated end to end in six steps by reverting the real HIGH: clean, then exit 1 unlisted, then exit 0 suppressed, then exit 1 on an expired suppression, then exit 1 on a suppression outliving its finding, then clean again. Five costs recorded rather than glossed: the four version overrides are rot liabilities with no automated check, to be deleted once `./mvnw help:effective-pom` shows the parent has caught up; the scan reads the resolved tree rather than the shipped jar, so ADR-047's H2 exclusion is not honoured and an H2 CVE would gate falsely, which is the safe direction of that error; scanning the fat jar was measured and does not work (`num=0` language-specific files, unchanged when `--java-db-repository` was set explicitly), which is why CycloneDX is deferred with its design recorded rather than rejected; the gating tier is empty today, so the policy rests on that demonstration rather than on a standing log; and the job is deliberately not a required status check on landing, for a narrower reason than the `supply-chain` job's, since that job's "a developer-tool advisory is a thing to know about, not a reason to block a merge" does not transfer to dependencies that execute in production. Runtime 4.75s warm, about 6s more cold for a 108.82 MiB database pull, cached at `~/.cache/trivy` keyed by UTC date — never by `hashFiles('**/pom.xml')`, because the database ages on a clock independent of our dependencies. One correction was needed after the first CI run and is recorded in ADR-050's Consequences: the job originally carried no `setup-java` on the reasoning that Trivy resolves the Maven tree with its own parser rather than invoking Maven, which is true of the parser and false of the job — that parser keeps no cache, re-fetches every parent POM and BOM on every run, and from a shared GitHub Actions IP that earned a `429 Too Many Requests` from Maven Central with a 1800-second `Retry-After` on `spring-data-bom`. The gate behaved exactly as designed, exiting 2 rather than reporting a clean tree from a scan that could not resolve the tree, so the exit contract earned its keep on day one; the job now runs `setup-java` and `./mvnw -B dependency:resolve` behind an `~/.m2/repository` cache that shares the `build` job's restore prefix, adding about 3s warm. That resolve step omits `-q` on purpose: quiet mode would suppress the per-artifact lines that named `spring-data-bom` as the artefact Central refused, which is the log the incident was diagnosed from. It also deliberately does **not** use `--offline-scan`, which would let an unresolvable parent POM shrink the tree Trivy believes exists and present an under-report as a clean scan.

- **Liquibase migrations now run against real PostgreSQL 17, not only H2 (EOP-164, ADR-056).** Three failsafe integration tests under `src/test/java/org/maglez/eop/migration/` drive the full 26-changeset changelog against a `postgres:17-alpine` Testcontainers container. `PostgresChangelogIT` asserts every changeset applies and that the `<dbms>`-gated PostgreSQL branch of `006-session-expiry.xml` is `EXECUTED` while the H2 branch is `MARK_RAN` — the mirror image of what the fast suite proves — and that `expires_at` really is rendered with a `now()` interval default. `PostgresSchemaValidationIT` is a `@SpringBootTest` wired to the same container by `@ServiceConnection`, so Hibernate's `ddl-auto: validate` at last validates the mappings against the PostgreSQL-rendered schema, and every mapped entity (read from the JPA metamodel rather than a hand-kept list) is selectable. `PostgresRollbackRoundTripIT` runs apply-all → rollback-all → apply-all over seeded sessions, players and game results. Before this, `006-session-expiry.xml` carried two mutually exclusive `<dbms>`-gated changesets of which only the H2 one was ever executed by a test, the PostgreSQL one running for the first time in production, and no rollback had ever been exercised on the engine production runs.
- **The integration suite is separated from the fast suite by class name alone (EOP-164, ADR-056).** `*IT` classes are invisible to surefire's default includes, so `./mvnw test` is unchanged at 1360 tests and still needs no Docker daemon, while `./mvnw verify` runs the twelve PostgreSQL tests through a bare `maven-failsafe-plugin` declaration — the seventh declared plugin, a cost ADR-047 weighed and this story accepts. Do not delete that declaration as redundant: `spring-boot-starter-parent` supplies its `integration-test` and `verify` bindings through `pluginManagement`, which materialise only when the plugin is also declared, so an effective-POM reading taken with the declaration in place is circular. It was deleted once on exactly that reasoning, and `./mvnw clean verify` then ran zero integration tests while still reporting BUILD SUCCESS with every coverage check met. Local `clean verify` went from 56 s to 1 m 12 s. Testcontainers 2.0.5 renamed every module, so the coordinates are `org.testcontainers:testcontainers-postgresql` and `:testcontainers-junit-jupiter` rather than the 1.x names, and `.envrc` now exports `DOCKER_HOST` and `TESTCONTAINERS_DOCKER_SOCKET_OVERRIDE` when a Colima socket is present, because Testcontainers does not read the docker CLI context and bind-mounts the resolved socket path into Ryuk.
- **The PostgreSQL rollback tests contradict EOP-163's premise and pin the real behaviour (EOP-164).** Rolling back `2026-08-22--widen-join-code-to-8-characters.xml` was described as lossy *and hard-failing*. On PostgreSQL 17 it does not fail: Liquibase narrows the column with an explicit cast, and an explicit cast to `varchar(6)` truncates rather than raising `value too long`, so the rollback reports success while silently discarding two characters of **every** eight-character join code — not merely the small fraction ending `00` that changeset 002's own rollback catches. A genuine hard failure does exist but is data-dependent: two live sessions whose codes share their first six characters collide on `uq_game_session_join_code`, so a rollback that succeeds against a lightly-seeded rehearsal database aborts against production. Both behaviours are now pinned by tests whose display names begin `DEFECT (EOP-163)`, which EOP-163 should invert rather than delete.

### Removed

- **Ace cards removed from the deck — deck size is now 68 (EOP-75, ADR-041).** The six Ace cards (one per STRIDE suit, rank 14) do not appear in the physical printed Elevation of Privilege card game. The committed card artwork (`ui/src/assets/cards/`) contains exactly 68 PNG images with no Ace in any suit, confirming the physical deck. `Rank.ACE` (value 14) has been removed from the `Rank` enum; `Card.isOpenThreat()` has been removed — the Open-Threat concept does not exist in the physical game. The Liquibase migration `2026-08-18--remove-ace-cards.xml` deletes the six Ace rows. King (rank 13) is now the highest rank. ADR-041 records the decision and resolves the conflict between the author's "74 cards" quote in `LICENCE-eop-deck.md` and the committed card images. ADR-023 deal arithmetic is re-derived for D = 68.

- **`VITE_LOBBY_UI_ENABLED` feature flag removed — lobby UI now always on (EOP-77).** The `VITE_LOBBY_UI_ENABLED` build-time flag introduced in EOP-11 has been removed per `.opencode/rules/feature-flags.md` (flags are removed once the feature is stable). The Create and Join session buttons in `HomeView` are now always enabled. The `isLobbyUiEnabled` constant and its guards have been removed from `App.tsx` (both the `useState` initializer and `HomeView`). `VITE_LOBBY_UI_ENABLED` has been removed from `ui/src/vite-env.d.ts` and `ui/.env.example`. The `lobby-ui: false` documentary entry has been removed from `application.yml`. Flag-specific tests in `App.test.tsx` have been removed; the remaining session-reconnect tests no longer stub the flag. ADR-037 Consequences has been amended to record the retirement and to name `VITE_GAME_SCREEN_ENABLED` as the current live example of the pattern.

- **`Rank` enum reduced from 13 to 12 values (EOP-75).** `TWO(2)` through `KING(13)`. `Rank.ofValue(14)` now throws `IllegalArgumentException`. The OpenAPI `Rank` enum is updated to remove `ACE`; `rankValue.maximum` is updated from 14 to 13.

- **Ungated `response time < 100ms` check removed from the k6 health canary (EOP-157, ADR-055).** `test/k6/health-check.js` carried a `check()` that read as a latency gate but gated nothing: a k6 check never affects the process exit code, and neither `test/k6/config/options.js` nor `options-ci.js` declares a threshold over `checks` or `checks_failed`, so any response between 100 ms and the real ceiling failed the check silently while passing every threshold. Latency is owned by the thresholds — `p(95) < 500` in CI, `p(95) < 200` locally. Relabelling the check "advisory" was considered and rejected: this scenario's summary export is the one the `perf-trend` job reads, and it records `checks` as a `passes/(passes + fails)` count into `ci-history.jsonl` on the `perf-history` branch, which `tools/perf/trend-page.html` then renders verbatim — both consumers see the count and neither sees the name, so an advisory label would have left a permanent trend column oscillating with shared-runner noise. `summaryTrendStats` already publishes avg, min, med, max and p(50)/p(95)/p(99), a richer latency picture than a binary counter. The reasoning is recorded in the script itself. A local smoke run now reports 442 of 442 checks (221 iterations × 2) rather than the 663 of 663 quoted in `docs/performance/TRENDS.md` and ADR-055 for runs predating this change; those figures stand as accurate history and the drop is the deletion, not a regression.

### Fixed

- **A player whose deal notification arrives while the SSE stream is being established waits forever with no error and no way to recover (EOP-224, [ADR-069](docs/adr/ADR-069-sse-doorbell-catch-up-read.md)).** The SSE doorbell (`GET /api/v1/sessions/{id}/events`) carries no payload — it means "state changed, re-read it" — and the client had a race window between its initial read and the moment the stream opened. Any event published in that window was delivered to nobody and lost forever. The EOP-217 Playwright suite observed this: a participant received a 409 on `/hand` while the deal was committing, the `cards-dealt` doorbell fired while the stream was still being established, and the participant sat on "Waiting for cards to be dealt…" indefinitely. The fix adds an optional `onOpen` callback to `subscribeToSession` in `ui/src/api.ts`, invoked exactly once after the response headers arrive and before the body is read; both call sites (`LobbyScreen.tsx` and `GameScreen.tsx`) pass a catch-up read, so a read issued after the headers observes everything published before the subscriber was registered. The fix is deterministic — no polling, no timers, no server-side replay — and closes the window for every doorbell, not just the deal. A separate, unfixed defect was discovered in the same investigation: requests for a single game session block together for up to a full 15-second SSE heartbeat period and are released together on the tick; that is now resolved by the fix below.

- **Every SSE stream leaks one JDBC connection for its lifetime, exhausting the pool and stalling all database requests on a 15-second heartbeat grid (EOP-227, [ADR-070](docs/adr/ADR-070-jdbc-connection-leak-via-open-in-view.md)).** `spring.jpa.open-in-view` defaults to `true`, which binds an `EntityManager` to the request thread for its entire duration. An SSE request does not complete until the stream ends — bounded only by the ten-minute `spring.mvc.async.request-timeout` — so every open stream holds one JDBC connection for up to ten minutes. With a three-player game opening six streams (three browser contexts × two screens) plus stale streams from torn-down contexts, the Hikari pool (default `maximum-pool-size: 10`) is exhausted. Every subsequent DB-touching request then parks in `HikariPool.getConnection` until a heartbeat write discovers a departed peer, closes its stream and returns its connection — producing the observed 15-second stall clusters. The fix sets `spring.jpa.open-in-view: false` in `application.yml` (safe here because the codebase uses DTOs, never entities, across boundaries) and adds `spring.datasource.hikari.leak-detection-threshold: 10000` for observability. Three consecutive clean E2E runs (15/15, 59.7s / 59.0s / 58.7s) against 3 failed / 12 passed before the fix. Across ~16,200 non-`/events` requests measured at the proxy, zero exceeded one second and the slowest was 0.243 s.

- **A single HTTP request could hang for tens of seconds, logging nothing, whenever one SSE subscriber stopped draining its socket (EOP-223).** `SseSessionEventPublisher.publish` fanned every notification out serially, on the request thread of whatever call triggered it, with no timeout and no isolation. A *departed* peer was always handled — the write throws `IOException` or `IllegalStateException` and the emitter is evicted — but a peer that is still connected and has simply stopped reading makes the write **block** instead, and nothing handled that and nothing logged it. The EOP-217 Playwright suite caught it as a join `POST` that had not returned after ten seconds with the join form still mounted and `isSubmitting` true, three of fifteen results failing, and `docker logs` carrying only two Spring startup lines for the entire run. Per-subscriber writes are now handed to `sendPool` and not waited on, exactly as `beat()` has done for the heartbeat sweep since EOP-20 and ADR-034 and for the same stated reason, so this closes an omission rather than adding a mechanism; the class javadoc promise is now that publishing never fails **or delays** the request that triggered it. That pool was also made **elastic**, because a fixed four threads merely moved the queue: four non-draining peers occupied every thread and healthy subscribers waited behind them, which the suite showed as a hand that never arrived. It is now `ThreadPoolExecutor(4, max(4, MAX_TOTAL_SUBSCRIBERS), 60s, SynchronousQueue)` — a `SynchronousQueue` never accepts a task no thread is ready to take, so the pool creates a thread rather than making a healthy subscriber wait behind a sick one, and growth is bounded by the same number that already bounds subscribers and therefore file descriptors, with threads above the core four retiring when play goes quiet. Rejection is now `LoggingDiscardPolicy`, which WARNs and drops: a drop matters here because no event history is kept and `Last-Event-ID` is not honoured, and it must be a plain discard rather than Spring's `DiscardOldestPolicy` because that policy polls the queue and re-enters `execute`, which on a `SynchronousQueue` has nothing to poll and would recurse. Notifications are deliberately **not** coalesced: a per-emitter in-flight guard was written, measured against the whole suite, moved nothing, and was removed as unsound, because only a *named* frame makes a client re-read `GET /api/v1/sessions/{sessionId}` and skipping an event behind an in-flight unnamed heartbeat comment would lose it outright. Two consequences for readers of the class: `subscriberCount` is now eventually rather than immediately consistent after a publish, since the write that discovers a departure happens on a pool thread; and a write failing for a reason other than a departed peer can no longer reach the caller, so it is logged at `WARN` inside the task rather than escaping a pool thread to `System.err`. The ticket's leading hypothesis — JDBC pool exhaustion via `spring.jpa.open-in-view` pinning a connection for the lifetime of a ten-minute SSE request — was **refuted by reading**: `@Transactional` sits on adapter methods only (`SessionRepositoryAdapter`, `TrickPlayRepositoryAdapter`, `GameResultRepositoryAdapter`), so every call is its own short transaction, `publish` runs outside any transaction, a long-lived `GET /api/v1/sessions/{id}/events` pins no pooled connection, and the coincidence between the observed thirty seconds and Hikari's default `connectionTimeout` is just that. Neither `spring.datasource.hikari.maximum-pool-size` nor `spring.jpa.open-in-view` was therefore changed, deliberately: both were suggested on the strength of that hypothesis, and a `LazyInitializationException` on a path the suite does not cover would be a worse regression than a startup warning. Evidence: `SsePublishDoesNotBlockTheCallerTest` pins the defect red at **30.06 s** against the pre-fix synchronous loop and green in under two seconds after, with a second case proving a healthy subscriber is still served while another is stalled; `./mvnw -o clean verify` exits 0 with 1468 unit tests, 13 integration tests, SpotBugs at zero and every JaCoCo limit met. **Known limitation, tracked separately:** EOP-217's fifteen-result suite is still red on four scenarios from a *different* cause — each failing scenario passes in isolation against a fresh stack in about two seconds, and two radically different pool shapes (fixed four threads behind a thousand-deep queue, versus an elastic pool of up to five hundred threads) produced byte-identical suite outcomes, so the residual is cross-test interference within the single Playwright process rather than anything in the SSE fan-out.
- **`CHANGELOG.md`'s `Unreleased` block no longer carries duplicate change-type headings (EOP-142).** Keep a Changelog expects one heading per change type per release, and this file had three pairs: `### Added` appeared twice, `### Changed` twice and `### Fixed` twice, all inside the single `## [1.0.0-SNAPSHOT] - Unreleased` section - there is no second release section in the file, so every `###` heading belonged to the same release. The ticket noticed only the `### Fixed` pair; its own Definition of Done forbids leaving *any* duplicate change-type heading in the release section, which put the other two in scope rather than making them scope creep. Each pair is merged at the position of its first occurrence and every bullet from both blocks is preserved: 176 bullets before, 176 after, and a sorted line-by-line comparison shows the only difference is the three surplus heading lines, with nothing added and nothing lost. Section order is deliberately left as it was (Added, Changed, Security, Removed, Fixed) rather than reshuffled into canonical Keep a Changelog sequence, because the Definition of Done does not ask for reordering and a gratuitous reshuffle of a 280-line file is how entries get lost; `### Decided, not yet implemented` is likewise preserved, because `.opencode/rules/resilience.md` references that section by name and requires the two to move together. The defect was harmless in itself - what it cost was a rule: a future author had two plausible places to add a fix and nothing to choose between them, and while editing this file five times earlier the same ambiguity had to be worked around each time, on the mistaken assumption that the second heading belonged to an older release.

- **Three hand-counted plugin figures that had gone stale are corrected, and two of them are corrected by deletion rather than by a new number (EOP-151, EOP-126).** The `supply-chain` job comment in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` said it guards "the six plugins pinned in the `plugin` array" when seven are pinned. The word is simply gone: the sentence already named the array it guards, `tools/supply-chain/expected-plugins.json` already carries the roster, and `audit-plugins.sh` already fails on drift between that baseline and `.opencode/opencode.json` — so the number was redundant *and* unguarded, and replacing six with seven would reinstate exactly the same rot the moment an eighth plugin is pinned. Following that ticket's own instruction to look for other figures of the same shape turned up a second, larger one in `pom.xml`, whose `maven-javadoc-plugin` comment block claimed **six** declared Maven plugins and enumerated them without `maven-failsafe-plugin`, derived "only five of the six carry a version" from that wrong base, and put the effective `build/plugins` at **fourteen** — against `.opencode/rules/build-quality.md`, which says seven, five of seven, and fifteen. Measurement settled it in `build-quality.md`'s favour: `pom.xml` declares seven, and counting direct top-level `<plugin>` children of `./mvnw help:effective-pom` gives `build/plugins` **15** and `pluginManagement` **31**. **That is a correction of a falsified figure, not the "do not correct 31/14" mistake EOP-126 warns against**, and the evidence is a single commit: `94155f7`, 2026-08-23, declared `maven-failsafe-plugin` for the Testcontainers integration tests, adding one to the declared roster and one to the effective bindings in the same change — which is why the comment's enumeration omits failsafe, and why `pluginManagement` re-measures at exactly the 31 that ticket recorded, confirming the counting method is the direct-children one it endorses rather than the `.//artifactId` one it rejects. The comment now carries the re-measurement recipe, the warning that a regex over `<artifactId>` over-counts because `h2` sits inside spring-boot's `<dependencies>` and `maven-project-info-reports-plugin` recurs across report sets, and a sentence recording that an earlier revision said six and fourteen — so the next reader re-measures instead of trusting the prose. **Three parent-managed version literals were deleted outright** in the same block — `liquibase-maven-plugin` "at 5.0.3", `maven-compiler-plugin` "3.15.0" and `maven-surefire-plugin` "3.5.6". None is ours to pin: each comes from the Boot parent and moves with a parent bump, with no signal to this file and no test to catch the drift, which is the same reasoning that removed the liquibase literal from `build-quality.md` under EOP-122 and left this one behind. The surrounding propositions are kept as they were, because they are true and load-bearing: liquibase is not one of the declared plugins, the parent manages one anyway as a bare entry with no `<executions>`, nothing binds it to a phase so it never runs, and `liquibase-core` executing during tests as an application dependency is a different thing again. The two remaining `liquibase-maven-plugin` `5.0.3` mentions in this file are dated records of what was measured on a date and are deliberately untouched — a dated measurement cannot go stale the way a standing present-tense claim can

- **Three `typescript:S6772` findings in the front end are resolved, and the ticket that asked for them was wrong about the rule, the fix, the count and the severity (EOP-191, [ADR-063](docs/adr/ADR-063-sonarqube-frontend-project.md)).** EOP-191 described S6772 as "async arrow functions should not be used directly as JSX props", explained it in terms of a new function reference per render causing re-renders and unmount races, and prescribed extracting each handler into a `useCallback` for a stable reference. **Every part of that is a different rule.** `rules.sonarsource.com` was unreachable, so identity was established from SonarSource's own sources instead: `packages/analysis/src/jsts/rules/README.md` in `SonarSource/SonarJS` maps `S6772` to the ESLint rule `react/jsx-child-element-spacing`, and the rule's shipped metadata (`S6772.json`) titles it *"Spacing between inline elements should be explicit"*, with `attribute: FORMATTED` and impacts `MAINTAINABILITY: MEDIUM, RELIABILITY: LOW`. So the rule is about ambiguous whitespace between JSX text and an adjacent inline element, the prescribed `useCallback` refactor would have resolved **zero** of the three findings, and the defect the ticket described **did not exist in the code** — `LobbyScreen.tsx` already wrapped both of its async handlers correctly as `onClick={() => { void copyJoinCode(); }}`. Two smaller corrections travel with that: the ticket called these "high-severity" when the metadata rates them MEDIUM maintainability and LOW reliability, and its title said two findings while its own body table listed three. **Three is right, and the ratchet is what proves it** — `RELIABILITY 9 → 6` and `MAINTAINABILITY 26 → 23`, three fewer on each, tightened by `scan-ui.sh`'s own `--tighten` tail step rather than by hand. **Locating the findings was the bulk of the work, because the baseline key deliberately carries no line number.** A key is `QUALITIES|rule|path|hash` where the hash is SonarQube's digest of the offending *line's content*, which is what lets a finding survive an unrelated edit above it — and equally what stops a reader going straight to it. That digest turns out to be the MD5 of the line with all whitespace removed, so brute-forcing every line of both files against the three hashes yielded a unique match for each: `ui/src/App.tsx:236`, `ui/src/components/LobbyScreen.tsx:227` and `:283`. That also settled where the rule reports, which the hashes disagreed with the obvious guess about: `App.tsx:236` is the `</a>` **closing** line of an `<a>` spanning 231–236, not its opening line, so *ambiguous spacing after the previous element* is reported at that element's closing boundary and *before the next element* at the next element's opening boundary — in both cases the boundary nearest the offending text, not the text itself. **Each of the three fixes makes the flagged pattern unmatchable rather than merely moving it**, which is checkable against the rule's implementation: it only ever flags a `JSXText` child, via `/^\s*\n\s*\S/` for the trailing case and `/\S\s*\n\s*$/` for the leading one, and both patterns require a non-space character. In `App.tsx` the sentence-final period text was pulled up onto the `</a>` line and the paragraph rewrapped, so the following text node no longer begins with newline-whitespace. In `LobbyScreen.tsx` the join-code label was wrapped in its own `<span>`, leaving the `<p>`'s two children separated by *whitespace-only* text, which matches neither pattern; that also deleted a dead trailing space JSX was silently stripping. The third fix is the one that changes behaviour rather than only formatting, so it is recorded as its own entry immediately below; in outline it inserts an explicit `{' '}`, which clears the rule because `isInlineElement` returns false for a `JSXExpressionContainer` — the reason `{' '}` is the rule's own documented remedy. **Those first two fixes are byte-identical in output**, and in particular neither introduces a rendered space where the author's dead trailing space hinted at one — the gap beside the join code comes from `govuk-!-margin-left-2`, so adding a text space would have changed the appearance. **The cost to state plainly is that nothing local will stop this class of finding coming back.** `ui/eslint.config.js` enables `@eslint/js`, `typescript-eslint` and `eslint-plugin-react-hooks` — **not** `eslint-plugin-react` — so `react/jsx-child-element-spacing` is invisible to `npm run verify`, and the front-end SonarQube scan that does see it is not part of `./mvnw verify` either. The `sonar-ratchet-ui` CI job is therefore the only guard, and it catches a reintroduction after the fact rather than at the keyboard; installing the plugin to close that gap was left out of scope rather than smuggled into a ratchet story. Two further measurements, recorded so the report diff reads as expected: `ncloc` fell 2814 → 2813 from the one-line rewrap, and coverage is unchanged at 90.8%
- **The GOV.UK warning-text notice in the lobby was announcing itself as one run-together word to screen readers, and now reads as two (EOP-191, [ADR-009](docs/adr/ADR-009-frontend-stack.md)).** The Design System's warning-text pattern puts a visually-hidden `<span class="govuk-warning-text__assistive">Warning</span>` on its own line above the message, and in **HTML** the newline between them collapses to a space, so assistive technology reads *"Warning, The game has started"*. **JSX does not collapse that newline, it deletes it** — whitespace containing a newline between an element and adjacent text is stripped entirely — so `ui/src/components/LobbyScreen.tsx` was rendering an accessible string of `WarningThe game has started`. Copying the Design System's markup verbatim into JSX is precisely what introduces the defect, which is worth knowing before the next component reaches for that pattern. The fix is an explicit `{' '}` after the closing `</span>`, restoring the rendering the Design System intends. **This is a genuine behaviour change and is deliberately carried as its own entry**, separate from the `typescript:S6772` formatting remediation above, even though the same edit satisfies both — the rule flagged the ambiguity, but a reader looking for behaviour changes should not have to find this buried in a lint-fix entry. **It is also now pinned by a test, which matters more than usual here.** `ui/src/components/LobbyScreen.test.tsx` asserts `textContent` on the `<strong>` equals `Warning The game has started`; the two pre-existing assertions in that file were blind to the defect, because `getByText` resolves through testing-library's `getNodeText`, which concatenates only *direct* text-node children and so never saw the nested assistive span at all. Reading `textContent` instead includes it, so the assertion genuinely fails without the `{' '}` and passes with it. That test is the only thing standing between this fix and a silent regression: `eslint-plugin-react` is absent from `ui/eslint.config.js`, so nothing in `npm run verify` would object to the `{' '}` being deleted again, and the Sonar job that would notice runs in CI rather than at the keyboard
- **k6 measurements now reach InfluxDB and the Grafana dashboard renders them; one of the three causes previously recorded was misdiagnosed, and two further faults had never been recorded at all (EOP-154, [ADR-016](docs/adr/ADR-016-local-container-runtime.md)).** The pipeline was broken from 2026-07-27, when HTTP authentication was enabled on the monitoring stack, until 2026-08-23 — four weeks in which every load test exited `0`, passed its thresholds, and wrote nothing. **The write path had no mystery in it.** ADR-016 recorded that "a direct credentialed write still returns 401, so why the configured admin credentials are refused is unresolved"; that does not reproduce. Probed against the running container, a write with the container's own `INFLUXDB_ADMIN_USER`/`INFLUXDB_ADMIN_PASSWORD` returns `204`, a write with deliberately wrong credentials returns `401`, and a write with none returns `401` — which is a functioning auth setup, not a broken one. The admin user existed all along, and the ordering in the old diagnosis was backwards: auth was enabled on 2026-07-27 and the volume actually in use, `eop-monitoring_influxdb_data`, was created on 2026-08-05, so the seed variables *were* honoured on its first init. (The volume that does predate auth is `eop-threat-medeling_influxdb_data`, the orphan left under the pre-`name:` Compose namespace, and nothing mounts it.) What was actually wrong is that `INFLUXDB_URL=http://localhost:8086/k6` carries no userinfo, so k6's writes were unauthenticated and were correctly refused. **Fixing that alone would have left the dashboard empty**, because two independent read-path faults sat behind it, neither in any ticket. The provisioned Grafana datasource took `url: ${INFLUXDB_URL}` — the same variable k6 uses — but `access: proxy` means Grafana makes the call from inside its own container, where `localhost:8086` is Grafana: `curl http://localhost:8086/ping` there fails to connect while `curl http://influxdb:8086/ping` returns `204`. One variable cannot serve a host consumer and a container consumer, so the datasource now pins `url: http://influxdb:8086` literally and `docker-compose.yml` no longer passes `INFLUXDB_URL` to Grafana at all. And the datasource declared no `uid`, so provisioning generated one — observed as `P951FEA4DE68E13C5` — while all ten panels in `tools/monitoring/grafana/dashboards/k6-load-testing.json` reference `"uid": "InfluxDB"`, so no panel could resolve its datasource even with a reachable URL; the `uid` is now pinned. **Authentication is disabled rather than credentialed, and that is the decision rather than a shortcut.** The port binds to `127.0.0.1`, the database holds load-test timing samples with no application or personal data, and k6's only way to authenticate is to embed the password in the `--out influxdb=$INFLUXDB_URL` URL that `test/k6/run.sh` echoes to the terminal — so authentication bought no confidentiality against the threat this stack faces while guaranteeing a secret in scrollback, shell history and any CI log.
