Recreating the volume to re-seed credentials was rejected too: it destroys the metric history the dashboard exists to show and fixes neither read-path fault. `INFLUXDB_USER` and `INFLUXDB_PASSWORD` are therefore deleted from both Compose services, `.env.example` and `SETUP.md`, on the same principle [ADR-043](docs/adr/ADR-043-liquibase-contexts-are-not-used.md) applied to inert Liquibase context tags — a setting nothing reads is worse than absent, because it reads as a control while enforcing nothing; Grafana's own admin credentials are untouched. One incidental finding is recorded in ADR-016 because it cost a broken stack: **pinning a `uid` on a datasource name Grafana's volume already knows under a generated uid aborts startup** — `Datasource provisioning error: data source not found`, cascading through `*appregistry.Service`, `*ngalert.AlertNG` and `*live.GrafanaLive`, ending in exit `1` — so the file declares a `deleteDatasources` entry for the same name, which runs first and always and converges from any prior volume state without `docker compose down -v`. **Verified by query, never by exit code**, which is the trap that hid this for four weeks and has not gone away: a `SMOKE_STAGES` run logged zero `Couldn't write stats` lines against 41 in a 40-second run before, left 16 measurements in the `k6` database, returned `count(value)` of 221 for both `http_req_duration` and `http_reqs` against the 221 requests in k6's own summary, and answered a `percentile(value, 95)` query through Grafana's own datasource proxy at `uid/InfluxDB` with a non-empty series — which proves the pinned uid and the container URL together. The four "known broken" disclosures in ADR-016, `.opencode/rules/performance-testing.md`, `docs/performance/TRENDS.md` and this file are retired in the same change, each replaced by the standing warning that k6 exits `0` regardless and by the `SHOW MEASUREMENTS` query that checks properly
- **`GlobalExceptionHandler` mapped the JDK's own `IllegalArgumentException` to a 400 and returned its message, which both echoed library-authored text to callers and denied a whole class of internal fault the only error-level log in the application (EOP-28).** The handler carried `@ExceptionHandler(IllegalArgumentException.class)` and set `problem.setDetail(exception.getMessage())`, justified in its Javadoc on the grounds that such exceptions "are raised by our own guard clauses with text written for a caller, not by a library with text written for a maintainer". That was an assumption about the codebase, not an invariant anything enforced — and `IllegalArgumentException` is thrown pervasively by the JDK, Hibernate, Jackson and Spring, with `NumberFormatException` extending it. **The second consequence is the more interesting one and was the reason to treat this as a defect rather than a tidy-up.** Because that mapping was *more specific* than `handleUnexpected(Exception)`, a fault of this kind never reached the single `LOG.error` in the class: it was reported to the caller as a client-side 400 and left no record whatsoever. That is the exact inverse of EOP-17, where two unmapped exceptions fell through to a 500 *with* a stack trace; here a mapped exception produced no trace at all. It was latent rather than live — an audit of all sixty `throw new IllegalArgumentException` sites found that only `PageQuery`, `DisplayName` and `TrickPlay` have a caller-reachable guard, and Jackson and path-variable failures surface as different types already handled by `ResponseEntityExceptionHandler` — so **what was actually wrong was the absence of the invariant, not any observed response.** The fix mints `org.maglez.eop.entity.InvalidInputException`, narrows the handler to `@ExceptionHandler(InvalidInputException.class)`, and adds a `LOG.warn` on that branch so that a legitimate rejection still leaves a trace. Status `400` and title `"Invalid request"` are unchanged, which is why the endpoint tests that pin them (`CardControllerIntegrationTest`'s `size=500` case, and the rate-limit slot-refund case in `SessionControllerIntegrationTest` that provokes a failure with a control character in a display name) pass untouched. **The one design point worth recording, because it deliberately inverts the house pattern:** `InvalidInputException extends IllegalArgumentException`, where all thirty-five other domain exceptions extend `RuntimeException` directly. It has to, and the reason is Spring's persistence exception translation. The persistence adapters are `@Repository` beans, so an `IllegalArgumentException` raised while reconstituting an entity from a database row is rewritten to `InvalidDataAccessApiUsageException` on the way out and reaches the catch-all as the logged 500 that a corrupt row deserves — behaviour three integration tests already pin, one of them asserting `.isNotInstanceOf(IllegalArgumentException.class)` in as many words. A value object cannot tell whether it is being constructed from a request or from a row, so had the new type extended `RuntimeException` it would have escaped translation untouched and a corrupt row would have been echoed to the caller as a 400 carrying a database-derived message — the very defect this entry closes, merely relocated. Extending the JDK type is also what keeps the sixteen value-object unit test files, and `PlayCardUseCaseTest`'s over-long-notes case, asserting `IllegalArgumentException` with no churn. Converted: all three `PageQuery` guards, all three `DisplayName` guards, and six of `TrickPlay`'s seven. **Deliberately not converted, so that they now become logged 500s instead of silent 400s:** `TrickPlay`'s seat-range guard, which bounds a credential-resolved value rather than caller input, and every other site — `Hands`, `Trick`, `GameSession`, `Player`, `Card`, `Rank`, `Hand`, `Standing`, `ScoredPlay`, `GameResult`, `IdentityTokenHash`, `JoinCode`, `PageResult`, `RealtimeProperties` — none of which is reachable with a value a caller chose. `TrickPlayRepositoryAdapter`'s `seatToWrite` was left on the JDK type for a second, unrelated reason: converting it to a type imported from `org.maglez.eop.entity` would add an eleventh origin to the set `TrickPlayExceptionOriginTest` derives from that adapter's source text, failing a gate whose `numberWord` helper stops at ten. **Two limits of the fix, stated plainly.** The missing invariant is now asserted directly rather than left as prose: a reflection test walks the handler's `@ExceptionHandler` annotations and fails if any maps a `java.*` type other than `Exception` itself, which is what a future contributor re-mapping `IllegalArgumentException` would trip while every other test in the file kept passing. But nothing detects the opposite mistake — a *new* caller-input guard written with a bare `IllegalArgumentException`, which would silently become a 500 — and that remains a matter for review.
- **`docs/api/openapi.yml` contradicted itself about when the leaderboard is available: the operation said `COMPLETED` only, the `Leaderboard` schema said any session state (EOP-83, ADR-004).** The contract-first rule in `.opencode/rules/api-design.md` makes the spec authoritative, so a spec that disagrees with itself cannot be satisfied by any implementation — and the half that was wrong was the schema. `components.schemas.Leaderboard` closed its description with "It is available in any session state, not only `COMPLETED`", while the `GET /api/v1/sessions/{sessionId}/leaderboard` operation two hundred lines above it correctly said "The leaderboard is only available once the session is in `COMPLETED` state. Requesting it while the session is still in progress returns `409`", which is exactly what `GetLeaderboardUseCase` does. The schema description now states the `COMPLETED`-only rule, records that an earlier revision claimed the opposite and that no implementation ever matched it, and explains that `sessionStatus` is read off the resolved session rather than written as a constant by the adapter (EOP-87) — so `COMPLETED` is the only value a client can observe today, because the operation refuses every other state one step earlier. It also states plainly that **widening that guard is a two-sided change**: the browser checks `sessionStatus` against the `SessionStatus` members it mirrors and rejects an out-of-contract value outright (ADR-045), so admitting `ABANDONED` server-side without the client change would turn a `200` into a `ContractViolationError`. **Both of the divergences the ticket reported turned out to be stale, in different ways, and saying so is the substance of this entry rather than an aside.** The ticket's first divergence claimed the spec documents `POST /sessions/{sessionId}/deal` as dealing all 68 cards with the surplus falling to the lowest seats (23/23/22 at three players) while three players were observed receiving 22/22/22, and inferred that "the dealing logic drops two cards … a domain bug affecting scoring completeness". It does not. `Hands.deal` runs `for (index = 0; index < orderedDeck.size(); index++)` and files card `i` to seat `i % seats.size()`, so the loop reaches the end of the deck and nothing is truncated or discarded — its own javadoc is titled "Deals every card in the deck, so that nothing is discarded" and already asserts the spec's figures. The deck really does hold 68 rows: `002-real-deck.xml` seeds 78, `2026-08-17--trim-deck-to-74-printed-cards.xml` deletes four and `2026-08-18--remove-ace-cards.xml` deletes six, and that arithmetic is pinned twice against the real migration chain — `DeckTrimMigrationRoundTripTest` counts the rows after applying, after rolling back each step and after re-applying, and `CardRepositoryAdapterIntegrationTest` reads them back through the real repository and checks the **per-suit** distribution as well as the total, precisely so a wrong mix cannot hide behind a right count. The distribution is pinned twice more: `HandsTest` asserts 23/23/22 at three players, seventeen each at four, 14/14/14/13/13 at five and 12/12/11/11/11/11 at six, that every card is dealt exactly once, that no card is lost or duplicated at any supported table size, and that the final card of the deck is dealt rather than dropped; `DealHandsUseCaseTest` asserts a total of 68 and twenty-three cards at the first seat through the use case. So four independent test classes and the migration arithmetic all agree with the spec, and **the acceptance criterion asking for "a test asserts the agreed deal distribution so the count cannot drift again" was already satisfied before the ticket was filed** — no test was added, because adding a fifth pin would be duplication rather than coverage. The observed 22/22/22 is `68 − (68 mod 3)`, which is not a rounding artefact but the exact signature of the equal-hands truncation EOP-72 briefly introduced and EOP-92 reverted; `Hands.deal`'s javadoc names both tickets and explains that the truncation also had to swap the lowest Tampering card into the kept range, a swap which always landed it on the last seat and so handed the opening lead to the highest-seated player. The observation therefore predates EOP-92 and describes behaviour that no longer exists. The second half of the ticket's second divergence — that the 409 "is absent from that operation's documented responses" — is simply false, and was false when the ticket was written: `git show` of `openapi.yml` at the merge base shows the operation already carrying all five of `200`, `400`, `403`, `404` and a full inline `409` block with an `application/problem+json` body. That stale claim had propagated into the codebase, so it is corrected in the one place it was repeated: the `Rfc9457ContractGuards` javadoc in `GameOverControllerIntegrationTest` said the 409 was "currently undocumented … that reconciliation is tracked as EOP-83", and now records what was actually checked and found. **Three limits stated plainly.** This is a documentation change and alters no behaviour — no production Java was touched, and the only source edit is a test-class comment. It does **not** decide whether the leaderboard *should* be available before completion; it aligns the spec with the code as it stands, and if that rule is ever revisited the two-sided client constraint above is the thing to plan around. And nothing in the build gates the deck-arithmetic figures quoted *in this entry*: `DeckArithmeticClaimsTest` reads `docs/**/*.md` and `src/**/*.java`, so a claim in `CHANGELOG.md` at the repository root is outside its reach and rests on review alone.
- **`GameOverController` wrote the string `"COMPLETED"` into every leaderboard response itself, and `LeaderboardResult` required a `GameResult` that no consumer read (EOP-87, ADR-030).** Two defects on one line. The literal was **correct only by accident**: it is right today solely because `GetLeaderboardUseCase` throws `GameNotCompletedException` for any other status one step earlier, so the adapter never sees a session that would contradict it. That is temporal coupling to another class's guard clause — the kind that does not fail when it breaks, it simply starts reporting `COMPLETED` for a session that is not, the moment anybody relaxes that guard. And relaxing it is not hypothetical: `docs/api/openapi.yml` already claims the leaderboard "is available in any session state, not only `COMPLETED`", a divergence tracked as EOP-83, so the guard is under active question. **The status is now read off the resolved session** — `resolved.session().status()`, already in hand at that point in `execute` for the guard itself, so no extra lookup — carried out through `LeaderboardResult` as a `SessionStatus`, and converted to its wire form with `.name()` in the adapter, which is the layer whose job that is. `LeaderboardDto.from(ScoreSheet, String)` needed no signature change. The second defect was the mirror image of the first: `LeaderboardResult` demanded a non-null `GameResult` whose javadoc said it was there "for the standings and metadata … so the controller can build the full leaderboard DTO", and **a `grep` for `gameResult()` across `src/` returned exactly one call site, in a test asserting the getter returned what was put in it.** Nothing in `adapter/` ever read it. Standings are recomputed from the trick history on every read (ADR-030) and the response contract carries no session metadata, so there was nothing for it to supply. **It was dropped from the record rather than made used**, because the alternative — inventing a response field to justify a field — is a contract change in service of a mistake; the row is still read inside the use case, as the existence gate that raises `GameResultNotRecordedException`, and a gate belongs at the gate. Reviving it needs a reader and a response field first, in that order. The read is now an `Optional.isEmpty()` check rather than an `orElseThrow` whose return value was discarded into an unused local. **The acceptance criterion asked for a test proving `$.sessionStatus` reflects the real status rather than passing because the literal happened to match, and no HTTP-level test can prove that** — the use case refuses every non-`COMPLETED` status with a 409, so `MockMvc` cannot reach a response carrying anything else. A new plain-JUnit `GameOverControllerTest` in the controller's own package (the constructor is package-private, so no Spring context is needed) stubs the use case with Mockito to return `IN_PROGRESS` and asserts the DTO carries `"IN_PROGRESS"`; a companion test pins `COMPLETED` so the only production-reachable value is proven unchanged. Reinstating a literal fails those two tests and nothing else. On the use-case side, `shouldCarryTheResolvedSessionStatus` asserts the status equals the fixture session's *own* status rather than the constant `COMPLETED`, which is what makes it a derivation test, and `shouldNotReturnTheGameResult` asserts the returned record equals one built with only a status and a score sheet while the gate still fired exactly once. `shouldReturnLeaderboardForCompletedSession` passes **unmodified**, as the ticket required. `docs/architecture/runtime-view.md` Sequence 11 named the wrong record components and now names the right ones, with the temporal coupling and the dropped field both written down. **Two limits stated plainly.** `SessionStatus` has four members and only `COMPLETED` can reach a client through this route today, so this change makes the value *derived*, not *variable* — anyone citing it as evidence that the endpoint serves other states is citing it wrongly, and EOP-83 is where that question actually gets settled. And `LeaderboardDto.sessionStatus` is one of the four fields EOP-105 retyped client-side into a union checked by `isSessionStatus`, so if the guard is ever relaxed to admit `ABANDONED` the browser will reject the payload with a `ContractViolationError` rather than render it — correct behaviour, but it means relaxing the guard is a two-sided change, not a one-line edit in a use case.
- **A seated player whose game had no recorded result was told their own session did not exist, because `GetLeaderboardUseCase` threw `SessionNotFoundException` for two unrelated conditions (EOP-86, ADR-005, ADR-042).** The leaderboard read resolved membership first and only then looked for the `game_result` row, and when that row was missing it reused the same exception the absent-session path uses — so the browser rendered "No session found with identifier …" next to a `Retry loading results` button, to a player demonstrably sitting at that table. The `@throws` tag on `execute` admitted the overload in as many words, documenting one type as meaning *either* "no session has that identifier" *or* "the session is completed but no result has been persisted yet", which is the shape of defect that survives review precisely because it is disclosed. **The fix is a new `GameResultNotRecordedException` in `org.maglez.eop.usecase`, mapped to 404 — the same status as before.** Keeping the status is the whole point: EOP-82 stopped the front end ejecting the player on a leaderboard 404 and left it ejecting only on 403 (ADR-042), so moving this condition to a status of its own would have invalidated working client logic to express a distinction that the problem `title` and `detail` express perfectly well. Only the body changed, from title `Session not found` to `Game result not recorded`. **Investigating it changed the ticket's own diagnosis, and the name with it.** The ticket described the condition as a timing window and proposed `GameResultNotYetPersistedException`; three paths actually reach it and only one is transient. `EndSessionUseCase` records a completion and deliberately writes no result at all, because a facilitator ending play early stops it before every card is played and there is no final standing to record — **every early-ended session's leaderboard therefore 404s permanently**, and that is the dominant cause, not a race. On the automatic path the write is best-effort by design: `ResolveTrickUseCase` and `PlayCardUseCase` both catch and log any exception out of `PersistGameResultUseCase` rather than fail a trick that is already durably committed, and nothing retries, so a failed write is permanent too. Only a read landing between `recordCompleted` and the persister sees the state and recovers. A type named "not *yet* persisted" would have promised a retry that will never succeed in two cases out of three, so it is `GameResultNotRecordedException`, and its javadoc enumerates all three causes rather than leaving the next reader to rediscover them. **The strongest evidence was already in the test suite:** `GameOverControllerIntegrationTest.completedTable()` could not reach the leaderboard happy path without calling `persistGameResultUseCase.execute(...)` by hand after `POST /end`, with a comment explaining that `EndSessionUseCase` does not go through trick resolution. That hand-written call was a standing demonstration that a facilitator-ended session is permanently resultless, and it had been sitting in the repository unread. It is now factored into an `endedTableWithoutResult()` helper that the new test uses directly, so the state under test is the one the application genuinely produces rather than a contrivance, and `completedTable()` builds on it — **both `HappyPath` tests are unmodified**. The exception is placed in `org.maglez.eop.usecase` rather than beside `SessionNotFoundException` and `GameNotCompletedException` in `org.maglez.eop.entity`, deliberately and against the pull of local consistency: those two state facts about identity and lifecycle that an entity can assert without knowing anything is stored anywhere, whereas "no row has been recorded" is a statement about a repository port, `GameResultRepository` lives in the use-case package, and the entity layer must not learn that persistence exists. That package already holds `RateLimitedException` and two siblings and `GlobalExceptionHandler` already imports from it, so nothing new crosses a boundary. Coverage is a unit test asserting the status mapping (mandatory under `error-handling.md`) plus a companion asserting the status is *equal* to `handleSessionNotFound`'s while the title and detail are not — the EOP-82 contract and the new distinction pinned by the same pair — and an integration test asserting the full RFC 9457 body on the completed-but-unrecorded path, a gap `@tester-api` had flagged as uncovered. Both regression guards are negative: the detail must not contain `No session found`, and must not contain `No static resource` either, the EOP-82 framework-fallthrough signature. `docs/api/openapi.yml` now documents both meanings of the leaderboard 404 inline instead of `$ref`-ing `SessionNotFound` (which remains in use elsewhere and was not removed), and `docs/architecture/runtime-view.md` Sequence 11 no longer carries a single `GameResult (else 404)` edge — each 404 is annotated where it actually arises, the absent-session one on the `SessionRepository` reply inside `ResolvePlayerUseCase` and the unrecorded-result one on the `GameResultRepository` reply, with labels quoting the two problem titles so the diagram is traceable to the handler. **Three limits stated plainly.** This is **not** a security change and must not be cited as one: membership is resolved before either exception can be raised, a stranger is stopped with `PlayerNotRecognisedException` → 403 and never reaches the repository read, so splitting the two meanings discloses nothing that was previously withheld — the enumeration-defence argument that justifies `PlayerNotInSessionException` sharing a body with `SessionNotFoundException` simply does not apply at this point in the flow. Nothing here makes a missing result *appear*; the two permanent causes remain permanent, and a facilitator who ends play early still gets no leaderboard, now with an accurate explanation instead of a false one. And the front end still offers a retry on this 404, which is correct for the transient cause and merely useless for the other two — a better client affordance is a separate change, not something a domain exception can fix. Noted in passing and left alone: `GetLeaderboardUseCase`'s class javadoc cites ADR-024 for the membership-candour point, but ADR-024 is about one adapter implementing both trick-play ports and authorises nobody, so that citation is wrong; the new class does not repeat it.
- **The seven accepted non-blocking findings from the EOP-10 session-lifecycle review are disposed of: three fixed, four closed with a recorded reason, none silently dropped (EOP-31).** Three needed code or prose changes. **`.env.example` shipped two plausible-looking passwords** — `GF_SECURITY_ADMIN_PASSWORD` and `INFLUXDB_PASSWORD` held values with mixed case and special characters that read as deliberately chosen credentials, and `SETUP.md` instructs `cp .env.example .env` (or `grep -v '^#' .env.example >> .env`) without ever telling the developer to replace them, so a copied template boots Grafana and InfluxDB with a git-published secret that looks intentional. All four non-empty password keys are now the single idiom `CHANGE_ME`; the two that already read as placeholders (`POSTGRES_PASSWORD`, `DATASOURCE_PASSWORD`, which shared the value `choose-a-strong-value`) were normalised too, deliberately widening the change beyond the two offenders so the must-supply set is enumerable by one `grep -n CHANGE_ME .env` rather than by recognising two idioms. `DB_PASSWORD=` is left empty on purpose — it pairs with `DB_USERNAME=sa` as the H2 development default and is not a secret — and `SETUP.md` now says so alongside a new instruction to replace every `CHANGE_ME` before starting anything. **The ticket said three sample passwords; measurement says two**, and that correction is recorded rather than quietly absorbed, because a claim stated more precisely than its evidence supports is the defect class EOP-120 existed to remove. Two mechanical notes for whoever touches this file next: VibeGuard redacts every `*PASSWORD*` value in an agent's view as `__VG_ENV_SECRET_<hash>__`, so the `edit` tool cannot be used on those lines at all — its `oldString` would be a redaction placeholder rather than file content — and the fix was applied with a key-anchored `perl -pi -e` that never reads a value, a justified exception to the prefer-`edit`-over-`sed` guidance; and the shared redaction hash is itself a measurement, since two keys hashing identically is how the duplicate `choose-a-strong-value` was found without seeing it. Nothing consumes `.env.example` as working config — no script, compose file or `.envrc` parses it — so no build could break. **`GameSession.join()`'s capacity re-check is now documented as deliberate rather than removed, and `nextSeatOrder()`'s Javadoc no longer claims the opposite.** `join()` throws `SessionFullException` on `players.size() >= MAXIMUM_PLAYERS`, and `nextSeatOrder()` — which the use case calls immediately beforehand — throws the same exception on the same condition, so the branch in `join()` is unreachable by every current caller. The ticket offered removal or documentation; documentation is chosen, on the ground that `security.md` mandates defence in depth and that reachability is a property of today's callers, not of the method: a future use case that seats a player without asking for a seat number would reach it. What the check contributes is narrower than the first draft of this entry claimed, and the correction is worth recording because it is this story's own defect class: it is the private constructor, not this check, that makes `MAXIMUM_PLAYERS` true of every instance, and deleting the check still refuses a seventh player — as an `IllegalArgumentException` the API reports as 400 quoting an internal invariant. What the check preserves is the 409 the caller is owed, held at that seam rather than delegated to the constructor's coarser refusal. @security-auditor caught the over-attribution in review, in both the Javadoc and this entry. The half that was a genuine falsehood is `nextSeatOrder()`'s closing sentence, "Keeping the capacity rule here means one place states it." — falsified by the duplicate thirty lines below it since both were written. It is replaced by a paragraph stating that the rule is held in two places on purpose, that neither check makes the other redundant, and that the tidier sentence must not be restored. No behaviour changed; both edits are Javadoc, which since EOP-120 means they can turn `./mvnw verify` red on their own. **ADR-014's "verified on a real socket through Caddy" is now an explicitly dated manual observation, and the sweep found the claim had silently widened.** `docs/adr/README.md` asserted the frame-level behaviour of the proxy as settled fact with no date and no reproducing check. `git log -S` dates the phrase to commit `87d8c49`, 2026-08-05, under EOP-10 — when the row listed exactly two events, `player-joined` and `game-started`. Commit `43f0cbc` (2026-08-14, EOP-14 Slice E) folded `hand-dealt`, `card-played` and `trick-resolved` into the **same sentence**, so a one-off observation of two events became a standing claim about five without anyone extending the observation. That is a finding beyond what item 6 alleged and is recorded as such. The row now names the date, the commit, the ticket and the two events it covered, states that the three later events were never part of it, and points at what does and does not reproduce it: `SessionStreamIntegrationTest` exercises the stream against the application directly, ADR-035 records that no test in the suite goes through Caddy, and ADR-017's k6 smoke test does go through the proxy but not on the SSE path. Four findings are closed without a change. **Item 1** (the `IdentityTokenHash` false safety certificate) was fixed by EOP-120 and is recorded there. **Item 4** — `SessionRepositoryAdapter.mentions()` deciding which uniqueness constraint was violated by lower-cased substring match on an exception message, while integration tests run on H2 and production runs PostgreSQL — is **closed with the residual risk recorded and a follow-up filed as EOP-136**, because the honest fix is a real engine in the test path and there is no Testcontainers dependency anywhere in `pom.xml` or `src/test/java/`; adopting one is an infrastructure decision needing its own ADR, not a line in a hygiene sweep. Two things make the deferral defensible and both are stated rather than assumed: the prose around `mentions()` is already honest about the gap rather than over-claiming, and the test suite openly marks PostgreSQL behaviour as *unmeasured* at eight separate sites (`TrickPlayUniqueConstraintTest`, `SessionRepositoryAdapterIntegrationTest`, `HandAndTrickUniqueConstraintTest`, `SeatAndSequenceBoundsTest` and others each say which SQL state or constraint name PostgreSQL 17 *would* report). Worth knowing for whoever picks the follow-up up: the suite does not even run H2 in PostgreSQL compatibility mode — `src/test/resources/application.properties` sets a plain `jdbc:h2:mem:eop-test` with no `MODE=`, and `mentions()` has since been duplicated into `TrickPlayRepositoryAdapter` with seven more constraint names behind it, so the surface is larger than item 4 described. **Item 5** (a stale Graphify index) is closed as already resolved: `.graphify/GRAPH_REPORT.md` now reports 3531 nodes and 10874 edges across 247 communities against the finding's 163/559, the top hub is `GlobalExceptionHandler` rather than `StrideCategoryTest`, session-lifecycle classes are present (`InMemorySessionRepository` is a god node), and the limitation *and* its re-index trigger are recorded at `ADR-011:45` — which also explains why the report's own freshness section showing a commit behind `HEAD` is the documented steady state rather than the defect. **Item 7** (root `AGENTS.md` claiming `ui/` is not scaffolded) is closed as fixed by events: no "not scaffolded" string survives anywhere in `AGENTS.md`, `.opencode/rules/` or `docs/`, and the file now carries the cited "Front-end: shipped, not pending" section that EOP-40 wrote.

- **Six more stale claims survived the second sweep, two of them in places neither review agent had looked — so the census was finally run as an enumeration of all 145 `git grep -n 'trick-play' -- docs/` hits read in full, because the filtered version of that same census is what let them through (EOP-41/EOP-49 gate-round remediation, round three, on findings from @architecture-guardian and @security-auditor).** The round-two entry below claimed a census over every mention of the flag. It was a census over a *filter* of them, and the filter was the defect: a line-oriented `grep` over `docs/adr/README.md` cannot see a clause in the middle of a 1,271-word table row, so it matched something harmless elsewhere in the row and reported the row clean. That failure is not hypothetical or mine alone — @architecture-guardian ran its own filtered census on round two and it silently missed the same two sites, and its report names the mechanism: "line-oriented grep over a 333-word table row matches on some unrelated clause elsewhere in the row … That is a real, structural reason the residue survived." So the shape of the index conceals defects in the index, which is now filed as EOP-132, and it would blind EOP-130's proposed line-anchor gate the same way. The method that worked was to split every one of the 145 hits into clauses, match each clause, and read every candidate in full. **Two of the six were found by neither gate**, which is the strongest available argument that a reviewer's list is a starting point and never a specification: `docs/adr/ADR-027-singleton-subresource-naming.md:110-111` asserted in the `## Relations` section, outside any dated block, that "the route is withheld by `eop.features.trick-play` and answers 404 while it is `false`" — three lines below a bullet whose own stale clause is correctly frozen and superseded from below, so the file simultaneously demonstrated the right pattern and violated it; and the tail of `docs/adr/ADR-023-deal-remainder-and-turn-order.md:1415` ended a dated Slice D correction with "the whole containment rests on the flag", which stopped being true when EOP-70 flipped it. The other four were the gates' own: `docs/adr/README.md:35` (the ADR-025 row) said the flag is one "which `application.yml` defaults to false" and that its test "asserts all three beans are absent" — @security-auditor graded it MEDIUM and @architecture-guardian MAJOR, and @security-auditor's reasoning is worth recording because it is the sharpest statement of why this class of defect matters: it asserts containment of a live, state-mutating write surface, which is the fail-dangerous direction, and an operator consulting the canonical index to establish what is exposed would draw the wrong conclusion; `docs/adr/ADR-025-dealing-is-its-own-use-case.md:397-398` was that row's counterpart in the ADR body, wrong on both the value and the arity, three lines above a bullet demonstrating the correct dated-note pattern; `docs/adr/ADR-031-the-score-is-read-through-its-own-route.md:220-221` still called slice C's gap "the one thing left before the flag can be flipped" six lines above a dated note that quotes that exact phrase — the note had scoped itself to the numbered conditions list and did not reach a bullet outside it; and that note itself was widened from "conditions 1 and 2" to "conditions 1, 2 and 5", because condition 5 forbade the flag going `true` "while thirteen use cases log nothing" and the flag went `true` with them still logging nothing — that condition was discharged by relocation rather than satisfaction, since ADR-026 put audit logging at the HTTP boundary instead of in the use cases, so it records a bar that was moved rather than one that was cleared. All six were superseded in place on the `ADR-027:81-82` pattern: nothing deleted, nothing renumbered, no **Status** line touched, and where a stale clause sat inside another story's dated correction it was left intact with a further dated note appended rather than edited. Three rounds of this suggest the standing lesson is narrower than "run a census": it is that a fact restated in more than a handful of places has no owner, and the durable fix is the delegation these amendments now perform — every corrected site points at ADR-013 for the flag's live value and arity instead of restating either, so the next flip has one place to change rather than eleven.

- **Five more stale containment claims survived the first sweep, including one three rows below a row that sweep had just corrected — so the search was redone as a census over every mention of the flag rather than over the sites a reviewer had named (EOP-41/EOP-49 gate-round remediation, round two, on findings from @architecture-guardian and @security-auditor).** The first remediation fixed the four sites the gate round reported and then asserted the set was empty. It was not, and the way it failed is the lesson: `docs/adr/README.md:41` said "The flag stays `false`" while the same commit was tense-pinning the body of the very ADR that row indexes (`ADR-031:191`, "remains `false`" → "was `false` when this slice shipped"). A fix applied site-by-site from a reviewer's list will always stop at the edge of that list, and the arity and the flag value between them were restated in at least eleven places, so the only sound method is to enumerate every occurrence and classify each as live, correctly historical, or stale. That is the method this round adopted, and both agents' survivor lists were swept in full rather than triaged into a third round — but it was applied with a filter rather than to completion, and six further sites survived it; see the entry above, which records what the filter concealed and why — @architecture-guardian graded `README.md:41` MAJOR and @security-auditor graded the other four LOW/INFO with a recommendation to defer, and deferring them would have left the index asserting `true` at rows 23, 33, 34 and 50 and `false` at rows 39 and 41. The five: `docs/adr/README.md:39` (ADR-029 row) claimed the flag "stays `false` on merge, so all five slices ship unreleased" and named ADR-026, EOP-48 and EOP-15 as undischarged predecessors — all three are discharged, the slices are released, and `ADR-027:81-82` had already recorded exactly that; `docs/adr/README.md:41` (ADR-031 row) as above, with "slice C owns the `COMPLETED` transition" also spent; `docs/adr/ADR-030-scoring-is-derived-not-accumulated.md:190-191` read "safe to merge with `eop.features.trick-play` still `false` — which it stays, per ADR-028", where the merge clause is sound history and only the forward claim "which it stays" had rotted; `ADR-030:268` said ADR-028 "keeps" the flag `false`; and `ADR-031:222-232`'s carried-conditions list still read as live instructions, the first of which was "Keep `eop.features.trick-play: false` on merge". Every one is superseded in place on the `ADR-027:81-82` pattern — tense pinned, original proposition and figures left standing, a dated note appended stating the current position — because each is a true record of what its own story decided and only the containment in front of it has gone. `ADR-031`'s list took a dated note after the heading rather than an in-place edit, since editing the conditions themselves would have rewritten what slice C was asked to carry; it closes "Do not read condition 1 as a standing instruction to merge with the flag off." Two of the round-one findings were also revised on re-review and are recorded here because the revisions went against this PR's own first reading: @security-auditor downgraded `ADR-027:77-78` after establishing that its stale `application.yml:112` anchor sits *inside* a dated 2026-08-14 block which `:81-82` already supersedes, so correcting that anchor would violate the pattern the rest of this work copies — it is correctly frozen, and EOP-131 is filed with "do not edit `:77-78` in place" as an acceptance criterion and won't-do as its likely outcome. Four follow-ups are filed for what remains out of scope: EOP-128 (`docs/architecture/C4-Diagrams.md` contradicting itself, `:81-84` against `:1081-1089`), EOP-129 (`application.yml:114-120`'s comment understating the gated surface, which is the other half of the two-artefact register), EOP-130 (a prose gate for cited line numbers, per @tester-unit-and-quality, with the cheaper embedded-`grep` alternative to be weighed first), and EOP-131. `EOP-47`, `EOP-97` and `EOP-116` were also found to be three open tickets for the single missing `docs/architecture/building-blocks.md`, none cross-referencing another; EOP-97 is now canonical with `Duplicate` links from the other two, which is the same defect this story is about — a fact restated in three places drifts in three directions.

- **Four documents still asserted that `eop.features.trick-play` contains the trick-play surface, long after EOP-70 opened it — including the flag register contradicting itself and the ADR index contradicting itself across three adjacent rows (EOP-41/EOP-49 gate-round remediation, on findings from @security-auditor and @architecture-guardian).** Both agents rejected the first cut of this PR on the same defect seen from two sides, and both were right: correcting ADR-013's arity while leaving other live claims saying the flag withholds that arity produces documentation that is *inconsistently* wrong rather than consistently wrong, which is harder to audit, not easier. `docs/adr/ADR-013-feature-flags.md` was the worst of the four and the reason this was a rejection rather than a nit — the paragraph this PR rewrote to state the flag is `true` sat roughly 150 lines above an undated present-tense sentence claiming it "remains `false`, so deal, play and resolve-trick routes are still absent", in the one document every other ADR now delegates to for the flag's value. A register whose two halves corroborate opposite answers is worse than one that is merely out of date. Tense pinned, with a dated note recording which paragraph is the live one and how to check. `docs/adr/README.md:23`, the index row for **that same ADR**, carried the superseded six-beans/two-controllers/eight-beans/six-routes figures undated, and this PR had edited the file while an earlier draft of the EOP-41 entry asserted the index "needs no edit" — true of the status column, wrong about the row, and now withdrawn below. `docs/adr/README.md:33`, the ADR-023 row, asserted in the present tense that "what contains the deferrals **today**" is the flag with `matchIfMissing` left at false over six beans at `application.yml:112` — false on the value, the line (that line is now `session-lifecycle`, so the cite reads as corroboration rather than as a dangling pointer), the bean count, the controller count and the route count, and sitting two rows from the row this PR had just corrected to say the gate is open. Both rows are now reframed as dated history with the current exposure stated and the arity delegated to ADR-013 rather than restated a fifth time. `docs/adr/ADR-031-the-score-is-read-through-its-own-route.md:194` claimed "Nothing shipped here is reachable in a deployed container"; the score route is reachable, so the bullet is tense-pinned and left standing as the slice-outcome record it is, since what shipped did not change — only the containment in front of it. The pattern followed throughout is `docs/adr/ADR-027-singleton-subresource-naming.md:81-82`, the one place in the repository that had already superseded this exact sentence in place. Three further sites are **not** fixed here and are filed instead, because this PR did not open those files and the C4 model is not what either ticket was about: `docs/architecture/C4-Diagrams.md:81-84` (the same eight-beans/flag-`false`/"stays `false` on merge" claim, contradicted by the same file at `:1081-1089`), `:501` ("six trick-play use cases") and `:1086` (a stale annotation range), plus `docs/adr/ADR-027:77-78`. The lesson worth keeping: the arity was restated in six places, so correcting it needed a census rather than a file. Deriving one is the acceptance criterion for the follow-up.

- **Thirty-odd line citations across ADR-023, ADR-024, ADR-025 and the ADR index had drifted onto the wrong lines — most of them onto javadoc `@throws` tags roughly forty lines above the code they name — and the "first statement" ordinal was wrong for the one use case it was most often quoted about (EOP-49, closing @architecture-guardian's gate-5 findings from the EOP-14 Slice E review, deliberately deferred rather than added to a remediation pass already carrying 1564 changed lines).** *Part 1 — anchors that had come to point at documentation.* ADR-024's Slice C2 amendment cited `DealHandsUseCase.java:120`, `PlayCardUseCase.java:139` and `ResolveTrickUseCase.java:111` as the sites where authorisation happens; re-derived, those are `:133`, `:189` and `:166`, and the stale three had come to land on two `@throws` javadoc tags and a `private final Clock clock;` field. That matters more than a broken pointer usually does, because the paragraph they sit in is the one that *discharges ADR-024's security obligation* — authorisation precedes any hand, trick or card read — and @security-auditor's Slice D condition is still open, so an auditor following a pointer to confirm it arrived at a comment and could confirm nothing. The same failure ran through ADR-025 decision 9, whose whole value is line-by-line checkability: all five of its `PlayCardUseCase` pre-flight cites (`:140`, `:147-149`, `:156`, `:168-170`, `:181-190`) had drifted inside that method's own `@throws` block. Every anchor in both documents was re-derived from source with `awk`/`grep` before being written — not adjusted by an offset — which is also how the ticket's own "should be" column was found to be stale (it assumed `PlayCardUseCase.execute` opens at `:161`; it opens at `:185`), so the numbers here are derived from the tree rather than from the ticket. Four cites in ADR-025's eight-row table were **already correct and are left untouched** (`Trick.java:372`, `Trick.java:378`, `Hand.java:115`, `DealHandsUseCase.java:162`), because a documentation-accuracy pass that rewrites correct lines destroys the reader's ability to tell what was checked. *Part 2 — a claim that had become false, reframed rather than renumbered.* The ticket asked for two ADR-023 anchors to be renumbered on the stated premise that "the substantive claim, that the flag is `false`, remains true". It is not true: EOP-70 flipped `eop.features.trick-play` to `true` on 2026-08-17, so renumbering would have left a false statement standing behind fresh line numbers, which is worse than leaving it visibly stale. Both sites are instead reframed as dated history — the tense pinned to Slice D, a `2026-08-21, EOP-49` note recording the flip, and the consequence spelled out rather than implied: every storage gap ADR-023 enumerates is no longer one property change away from reachable but *is* reachable through a live route, so the reader's obligation moves from watching a property to auditing a reachable surface. The four `UseCaseConfiguration` anchors and the `TrickController.java:69` cite in the second site were **dropped rather than corrected**, because the current arity belongs in exactly one place — ADR-013, the flag register, which carries it with a date and a regeneration command as of the EOP-41 commit — and restating it in ADR-023 would only give it a second place to drift. *Part 3 — the ordinal.* "Authorisation is the first statement of all three use cases" is true of `DealHandsUseCase` and `ResolveTrickUseCase` and false of `PlayCardUseCase`, where a null check (`:186`) and a session-id read (`:187`) precede the port call (`:189`). Corrected to "first port call" at ADR-025's decision-4 heading and sentence, in ADR-024's Slice C2 amendment, and on the ADR-024 row of `docs/adr/README.md`. **Only the ordinal was ever wrong — the security property was not.** Every one of those sites paired the phrase with the qualifier "before any hand, trick or card is read", which was true throughout, and Slice E had already corrected the three sites it authored (`PlayCardUseCase.java`'s javadoc, `CHANGELOG.md`, the ADR-025 index row) on the principle that a line you rewrite is a line you own; those three are confirmed still correct and untouched here. The replacement wording deliberately carries no cardinal: `grep -n 'resolvePlayerUseCase\.' src/main/java/org/maglez/eop/usecase/*.java` shows **eleven** such call sites, not the five the ticket assumed, so "all three" is retained only where it means the three use cases the ADR is about. *Three defects found while re-deriving and fixed in place, none of them ticketed.* ADR-024's `:120` and `:111` cites (the ticket flagged only the PlayCard one); ADR-025 decision 9's remaining anchors — sixteen in the pre-flight table alone, plus the constructor paragraph and the two-refusals paragraph — and the `Trick.java:360-384` extent and two event cites in decision 8 (`PlayCardUseCase.java:229`→`:253`, `ResolveTrickUseCase.java:162`→`:195`); and a **live** claim on the ADR-024 index row asserting the trick-play oracle is reachable "only through beans that do not exist while `eop.features.trick-play` is false", which EOP-70 falsified — now stating that the gate is open and only authorisation stands between a requester and the oracle. Each sat on a line already being rewritten, and leaving a known-wrong citation on such a line is indefensible; unrelated files stayed out of scope. **No ADR was renumbered, no decision was rewritten, and no dated block had its meaning altered** — every correction is either in place with the tense pinned, or carried in a new dated note appended to the block it concerns, per the amendment convention. No **Status** line changed anywhere, so `docs/adr/README.md` needed no status or date update and `AdrIndexConsistencyTest` is unaffected.

- **The `@ConditionalOnProperty` exception in `.opencode/rules/configuration.md` was filed under the one rule it does not qualify, and ADR-013's self-described "current" bean counts were wrong on every particular (EOP-41, closing two LOW findings from the EOP-32 gate round).** *Item B — the misfiled exception.* The rule file read "`@ConfigurationProperties` with `@Validated` … never raw `@Value`" with a nested sub-bullet beginning "One exception:" that described `@ConditionalOnProperty` being evaluated before any `@ConfigurationProperties` bean is bound. The statement is true, but `@ConditionalOnProperty` is not `@Value`, so as written it advertised an exception to a prohibition it has nothing to do with — and that file is injected into every agent's context on every turn, so the misreading was available on every turn. The clause it actually qualifies was never stated: that every property is read through a typed properties class. Both bullets are now top-level, the prohibition is hardened to "**Raw `@Value` is banned outright and has no exceptions**", the corollary is stated explicitly and the binding-order exception is attached to *that*, closing with the discrimination in terms — "`@ConditionalOnProperty` is not `@Value`, so this exception never licenses reaching for `@Value`". The prohibition also now carries the command rather than a count (`grep -rnE '@Value\s*\(' src/` returns nothing today and must keep returning nothing, with a warning that the loose `@Value` matches under `src/test` are all JUnit `@ValueSource`), because a citation that teaches re-verification cannot rot into a lie the way a bare number can. Nothing in `src/` changed and nothing was at risk: the annotation has zero uses in `src/main` and `src/test`, which is why the guardian graded this a wording risk rather than a live one. *Item A — verified already satisfied, no edit.* The ticket asked that ADR-013 be told it is the register of active flags and that there is deliberately no separate catalogue document. It already says so twice, in notes predating the follow-up: `ADR-013:114-116` ("makes this ADR and `src/main/resources/application.yml` the flag register and says there is no separate catalogue") and `:165-167` ("Because this ADR and `src/main/resources/application.yml` *are* the flag register … an operator asking \"is flag-on reachable?\" reads this paragraph and no separate catalogue"). Adding the sentence would have duplicated existing prose — the exact failure the ticket itself warns against for the naming convention, which it correctly declined to have restated. Worth recording because the ticket's framing is looser than the ADR's: the register is **two** artefacts, the ADR *and* `application.yml`, not the ADR alone. The acceptance criterion that no document instructs anyone to create a catalogue holds independently — searching for `flag catalogue`, `flags catalogue` and `docs/feature-flags` returns three hits (`CHANGELOG.md`, `.opencode/rules/feature-flags.md`, `docs/adr/ADR-037-frontend-build-time-feature-flags.md`) and all three assert the opposite. Stated as a search rather than as "the surviving mentions of the phrase", because the bare word *catalogue* also appears at `docs/adr/ADR-042-game-over-flag-on.md:113` and `docs/adr/ADR-013-feature-flags.md:379`, `:394` and `:396` in the unrelated sense of *catalogued inside ADR-013* — which is the register working, not a second document being proposed. *An unticketed defect found in EOP-41's own file, and repaired rather than deferred.* A paragraph in ADR-013 opened "The counts also moved, and this is the register's current statement of them" — a live claim, not one of the document's dated blockquotes, so it could not be reframed as history — and every particular of it was false. It claimed the `trick-play` flag withholds "six use-case beans and two controllers — eight beans in all"; it withholds **seven use-case beans and three controllers, ten beans in all**, `EndSessionUseCase`, `GetScoreUseCase` and `EndSessionController` having joined since. All eight cited anchors had drifted off the annotations they named, including `TrickController.java:69`, which is the `@RequestMapping` and not the flag. It claimed six routes where there are seven, and quoted `TrickPlayDisabledIntegrationTest` at eight bean assertions and six route assertions where the test makes ten and seven. It closed "It stays `false` on merge — `application.yml:112`", false in both the value and the line: EOP-70 flipped the flag to `true` and it now sits at `:129`. Ten beans, seven routes and every anchor were re-derived from source before being written, the paragraph is dated 2026-08-21 instead of calling itself "current", and it carries the two commands that regenerate the figures — the distinction the superseded wording lost. ADR-013's **status line** is unchanged, so `AdrIndexConsistencyTest` had nothing to react to; an earlier draft of this entry inferred from that "so `docs/adr/README.md` needs no edit", which was wrong and is withdrawn — the index's ADR-013 row carries the same arity in its Implemented column, was equally stale, and @architecture-guardian caught it in the gate round. It is corrected in this PR, and the correction is recorded with the rest of that round below.

- **`.opencode/rules/build-quality.md` quoted a `liquibase-maven-plugin` version and cited ADR-006 as its authority, but ADR-006 contains no such claim (EOP-122, closing @architecture-guardian's F-1 from the EOP-35/EOP-40/EOP-108 gate round).** The rule file asserted that liquibase "*is* parent-managed at 5.0.3 in the effective `pluginManagement`, with no lifecycle binding, so it never runs", under a sentence citing "ADR-006 as amended 2026-08-20 (EOP-120)". ADR-006 mentions liquibase on exactly three lines, and the only one carrying `5.0.3` says something else entirely: that `liquibase-core` 5.0.3 reads `contextFilter` before `context` (`ChangeSet.java:432-434`) — an attribute-precedence fact about the **library**, not a statement about the Maven `pluginManagement` layer or about any plugin. So a version literal sat in a file injected into every agent's context every session with no dated, supersedable home, which is the exact defect class EOP-35 was raised to eliminate. The four other literals in the same sentence (`maven-javadoc-plugin` 3.11.2, the Boot-owned 3.12.0, `maven-enforcer-plugin` 3.6.3, `spring-boot-maven-plugin` 4.1.0) are all homed at `ADR-006:100`; only liquibase was orphaned. **The claim itself is true, and was verified rather than assumed:** `./mvnw help:effective-pom` shows 31 entries in the effective `pluginManagement` including `org.liquibase:liquibase-maven-plugin`, that entry carrying coordinates and a version but no `<executions>` and no `<configuration>`, and 14 entries in the effective `build/plugins` with no liquibase among them — so nothing binds it to a phase and it never runs. **Of the ticket's two permitted dispositions the literal is dropped rather than homed in an ADR (option b), on three grounds of unequal weight.** The decisive one is that the literal carries no guidance weight: every operative proposition — the parent manages one, nothing binds it, it never runs, `liquibase-core` is a different artifact — is true at *any* version, so a reader who knows `5.0.3` can do nothing a reader who does not cannot. The second supports that without being able to carry it. The number is not ours to pin: it comes from the Boot parent's own `liquibase.version` property — the same property that governs `liquibase-core`, which is why the two `5.0.3` values coincide despite being distinct artifacts — so a Boot bump moves it with no signal to any file quoting it and no test to catch the drift. **That second ground must not be cited alone, because it proves too much:** the Boot-owned `3.12.0` on the same line is equally not ours to pin, yet is correctly *kept* at `ADR-006:100`, where it is quoted contrastively to state that this repository deliberately sits behind the parent at 3.11.2 — strip the number and that proposition becomes unstatable. Guidance weight is the discriminator, not ownership. Homing the liquibase literal would therefore have added a second copy of a value nothing keeps true, rather than closing anything. **One such copy does survive, so the sweep is narrower than it may read:** `pom.xml:164` still says `liquibase-maven-plugin` "sits in the effective pluginManagement at 5.0.3, parent-managed with no lifecycle binding" — same value, same parent property, same absence of any test, and the drift argument above applies to it verbatim. It is left deliberately: it falls outside this ticket's `.opencode/rules/` scope, it sits beside the parent declaration that actually determines the value rather than in a file injected into every agent's context every session, and it does not contradict the new text. Counting standing present-tense claims — the dated records in this file aside, which state what was measured on a date and so cannot go stale the way a standing claim can — the surface for this coordinate therefore went from two sites to one, not from zero to two averted, and the remaining site is recorded here rather than left for the next auditor to rediscover. The third ground is that writing a `liquibase-maven-plugin` version into ADR-006 would stand it beside the same-numbered `liquibase-core` version already there, inviting precisely the conflation the ticket flagged — and a repo-wide census puts that beyond doubt: of fifteen tracked `5.0.3` sites, twelve concern `liquibase-core` and only three the Maven plugin — `pom.xml:164`, the EOP-120 entry at `CHANGELOG.md:59`, and this one — so option (a) would have dropped a plugin assertion into the middle of that `liquibase-core` cluster, forty lines from `ADR-006:59`. An earlier draft of this entry said thirteen sites and claimed *every one* was `liquibase-core`; both figures were wrong and are corrected here, because a census stated in a paragraph about not overstating claims had better be countable. The trap is worth recording: `git grep` reports `Binary file … matches` notices as lines, which is how three tracked PDFs under `docs/EoP_Microsoft_Docs/` inflated the denominator while two `CHANGELOG.md` lines fell out of it. And the fact loses no verifiability, because `help:effective-pom` is how it was checked here in the first place. **No proposition was weakened to achieve this**, which the ticket explicitly forbade: all four operative claims survive verbatim — liquibase runs as an application dependency during tests, we declare no `liquibase-maven-plugin`, the parent nonetheless manages one, and nothing binds it — and the passage gains two precisions it lacked (the entry is bare of both `<executions>` and `<configuration>`), the reason no literal is quoted, and a standing instruction not to helpfully restore one. Because option (b) needs no amendment, ADR-006's status line and `docs/adr/README.md` are untouched and `AdrIndexConsistencyTest` is unaffected. **The rest of `.opencode/rules/` was swept for the same defect and one site was tightened.** `performance-testing.md` stated "InfluxDB 1.8" twice; the version is real but was attributed to nothing, so the surviving mention now names its pin inline (`image: influxdb:1.8` in `docker-compose.yml`, which the same sentence already cited for the loopback binding) and the duplicate under **InfluxDB Schema** is dropped, leaving the version stated once, at its citation. The two literals in `versioning.md` are correct as they stand and were deliberately left: "Semantic Versioning 2.0.0" is a specification version homed at `ADR-007:13` and `:37`, not a dependency pin, and `1.2.0-SNAPSHOT` → `v1.2.0` is an illustrative example that asserts nothing about the repository's current version. Nothing in the build could have caught any of this — `grep -rn '\.opencode' src/test/java/` returns a single hit, a Javadoc *mention* of `security.md` in `SessionLifecycleOffValueIntegrationTest`, and the five documentation-integrity gates scope to `docs/`, `src/`, `docs/api/openapi.yml` and `ui/src/api.ts` — so rule-file literals remain enforced by review, and that gap is now stated where the next auditor will look.

- **`IdentityTokenHash` carried a false safety certificate: it claimed digests were compared by an indexed database lookup when they are compared in Java by `String.equals` (EOP-120, closing item 1 of EOP-31).** The type's Javadoc asserted "comparison happens as an indexed database lookup rather than in Java, so there is no meaningful timing side channel in this type". The real path is `ResolvePlayerUseCase` → `GameSession.playerByTokenHash` → `players.stream().filter(p -> p.isIdentifiedBy(hash))` → `Player.isIdentifiedBy` → the record's generated `equals` → `String.equals` on hex text: a short-circuiting byte-wise comparison, in memory, over at most six players. `PlayerJpaRepository`'s own Javadoc said the opposite all along ("there is no method that finds a player by credential digest"), and `uq_player_identity_token_hash` exists to enforce uniqueness, never as a lookup path — so the claim was not merely imprecise, it named a mechanism the code does not contain. **The comment was made true rather than retracted:** `IdentityTokenHash` now overrides `equals` to compare through `MessageDigest.isEqual` over the US-ASCII bytes of the hex value, with a matching `hashCode` (Checkstyle `EqualsHashCode` requires the pair), and `Player.isIdentifiedBy` carries a warning not to "simplify" it back to a comparison on `value()`. **Nothing exploitable was closed and the entry does not claim otherwise:** an attacker supplies a token, not a digest, so steering the compared bytes needs SHA-256 preimage work. The risk was documentary — a future lower-entropy credential (a rejoin PIN, a short reconnect code) added to this seam would have inherited a written guarantee that was never true. Timing is not directly assertable in a unit test, so `IdentityTokenHashTest` gains six cases including one that reads the source file as text and fails if the `MessageDigest.isEqual` call disappears, guarding against a silent revert to the generated `equals`.

- **The join sequence diagram claimed a 201 the endpoint has never sent, and the two Javadocs describing a contested seat contradicted each other ~700 lines apart (EOP-58).** `docs/architecture/runtime-view.md` ended the join sequence `SC-->>P2: 201 sessionId, identityToken, seatOrder`, but `SessionController.joinSession` returns a bare `SessionAdmissionDto` with no `@ResponseStatus`, so Spring answers **200** — which `docs/api/openapi.yml` (`'200'` on `POST /api/v1/sessions/{joinCode}/players`), `SessionContentionIntegrationTest` (`status().isOk()`) and the controller method's own `@return` Javadoc all already said, leaving the diagram as the single dissenting source. One digit changed. The neighbouring `SC-->>F: 201 sessionId, joinCode, identityToken` is **correct and deliberately untouched**, because `createSession` genuinely returns `ResponseEntity.created(location)`; `C4-Diagrams.md`'s 201 for `POST /plays` was checked for the same defect and is correct too (`@ResponseStatus(HttpStatus.CREATED)` on `TrickController.playCard`), so the sweep found no third site. **The contradiction was that both accounts were true and neither said why.** `JoinSessionUseCase` explained its attempt budget by arguing that a joiner "can lose the race for a seat at most five times before the table is full", implying `SeatAlreadyTakenException` is unreachable, while `GlobalExceptionHandler.handleSeatAlreadyTaken` justified logging at debug level on the grounds that a caller "can provoke this at will by firing concurrent joins at one lobby". Both hold, because the domain fullness check in `GameSession.join` runs against the snapshot fetched at the top of each attempt: simultaneous joins can each pass a check that was already stale and lose on `uq_player_session_seat` instead. Each Javadoc now carries that clause, so the reader who finds one is not misled by the other — unreachable by arithmetic at a quiet lobby, provokable on purpose under load. The handler's "at will" wording is kept rather than softened, because `handleJoinCodeUnavailable` twenty lines below draws an explicit contrast against it ("unlike a contested seat this is not something a caller can provoke"). **The `attempts > capacity` relationship was an unwritten invariant and is now enforced by construction rather than by comment**: `MAXIMUM_SEAT_ATTEMPTS` is `GameSession.MAXIMUM_PLAYERS + 2` instead of the literal `8` — same value, following the precedent `SseSessionEventPublisher` set with `2 * GameSession.MAXIMUM_PLAYERS` — and `JoinSessionUseCaseTest` asserts `SEAT_ATTEMPTS > GameSession.MAXIMUM_PLAYERS` alongside the existing mirror of the budget, so lowering the budget below the table size fails a test rather than quietly making a 409 routine. An assertion rather than a new branch, per the branch-coverage lesson in ADR-006: a runtime guard on two compile-time constants would be a branch no input can exercise. The other thing keeping the path off the quiet route is negative — `SessionRepository` has no seat-vacating method — and that is now stated in the Javadoc, since adding a leave-lobby feature would make the refusal live and should not be discovered as a surprise.

- **Four of the twelve Mermaid sequence diagrams in `docs/architecture/runtime-view.md` had never rendered, and no build could tell (EOP-000, ADR-006 amended a fourth time).** Sections 2 (the SSE stream), 6 (resolving a trick), 7 (reading the score) and 9 (the expired-session sweep) showed a parse error instead of a diagram. The cause is one lexer rule: `packages/mermaid/src/diagrams/sequence/parser/sequenceDiagram.jison` tokenises label text as `[^#\n;]*`, so a literal `;` ends the token and the rest of the line is parsed as a fresh statement. Measured against mermaid 11.17.0, that breaks **thirteen** text-carrying constructs — message text, note text, `alt`, `else`, `opt`, `loop`, `par`, `and`, `critical`, `break`, `title`, `participant … as …` and `box` — not merely the two this document happened to trip over. The same rule is why the three `&lt;`/`&gt;` sites failed: an HTML entity ends in `;`. It is also why mermaid's own documented `#59;` escape is unusable here, because the token excludes `#` as well, so there is no escape sequence and the characters have to go. Eight lines changed: five delete a redundant `;` before a `<br/>` or replace a mid-sentence one with an em dash (333, 852, 938, 939, 1094), and three replace the entity form with the raw text it stood in for — `List<Trick>` (930), `List<UUID>` (1090) and the verbatim SQL `expires_at < :before` / `status <> 'ABANDONED'` (1088). No prose was reworded and no line reflowed; line 929 is byte-identical, because `DeckArithmeticClaimsTest` pins it as one unbroken line. Two things are worth recording because they cut against the obvious reading. First, a hypothesis that raw `<` followed by a letter would be swallowed as an HTML tag was **disproven** by rendering diagrams 7, 9 and 11 at `securityLevel` `strict` and `loose`: mermaid's sequence renderer escapes angle brackets itself, `List<Trick>` displays intact, and an edit made to line 1234 on the strength of that hypothesis was reverted — it had never been a defect. Second, `#` is the more dangerous of the two characters and was nearly missed: it *parses*, then silently truncates the label at render time, so `issue #42` displays as `issue` with nothing logged. `;` fails loudly; `#` deletes your text and stays green. Evidence is a before/after parse of every fence (8 of 12 → 12 of 12) plus rendered SVGs in which the SQL operators and both generics are visibly present. The residual risk is that GitHub pins its own mermaid version, so a local parse is a strong proxy rather than proof; the token has been stable for years.
- **Nothing in the repository or CI had ever parsed a Mermaid diagram, so the above was undetectable except by a human opening the page on GitHub (EOP-000).** `MermaidSequenceTextTest` (`org.maglez.eop.docs`) is the fifth documentation-integrity gate and the first that guards a diagram rather than prose. It walks `docs/**/*.md`, keeps only ```mermaid fences whose first directive is `sequenceDiagram` — the scoping is load-bearing, because `docs/architecture/C4-Diagrams.md:228` is a flowchart node label that legitimately contains `&lt;` inside a quoted string and renders correctly — and forbids `;` and `#` in any label, reporting `file:line` and the offending text. It is structural rather than a phrase list, so unlike the other four gates it holds for diagrams nobody has written yet. Its limits are stated in ADR-006 rather than left to be discovered: it proves the absence of two characters and nothing more, so an unbalanced `end`, a malformed arrow or a participant that no longer exists all still pass; its coverage floor is the hand-written `MINIMUM_SEQUENCE_DIAGRAMS` constant, which exists only so the guard cannot silently stop matching and pass forever, per the branch-coverage lesson in ADR-006; and the withdrawn angle-bracket invariant is recorded there with an instruction not to re-add it. It was verified by mutation, not by assertion that it works: reinstating the `;` on line 333 failed `shouldNotCarryASemicolon` alone, appending `#22` to line 1090 failed `shouldNotCarryAHash` alone, and both named the exact line.
- **The join-code widening rollback silently truncated live data on PostgreSQL and was data-dependent on both engines — a guard now refuses the rollback when any eight-character code exists (EOP-163, ADR-057).** The widening in `2026-08-22--widen-join-code-to-8-characters.xml` padded six-character codes with `00`, destroying the distinction between a padded code and a genuinely generated one ending `00`. On PostgreSQL 17, Liquibase's `modifyDataType` generator narrows with an explicit cast that **truncates** rather than raising `value too long`, so every eight-character code loses its last two characters and the rollback reports success. On H2, the padding-removal changeset runs first and its `LIKE '%00'` predicate silently destroys a genuinely generated code ending `00`. The fix adds a guard changeset dated `2026-08-23` that runs **first** on rollback (alphabetically last on forward), adding a CHECK constraint `LENGTH(join_code) <= 6` that aborts the rollback if any violating row exists — nothing modified, no data loss. The guard is deliberately conservative: it refuses even the reversible case where every wide code is a padded one, because that case is not identifiable. The blast radius is bounded by the 24-hour `expires_at` TTL (ADR-036), so padded sessions expire within a day. A latent gap: a future changelog dated before `2026-08-23` would sort between the widening and the guard and silently defeat it — no build gate detects this. Tests in `PostgresRollbackRoundTripIT` that pinned the defect are now inverted, and `JoinCodeRollbackGuardTest` covers the guard against H2.

- **Front-end security hardening: cross-origin isolation headers, the IP-literal header bypass, and production source maps.** `ui/Caddyfile` now sets `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy: same-origin`, `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy: same-origin` and `Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy: require-corp`, on the `handle_errors` path as well as the success path, so an error response cannot carry a weaker policy than a `200`. None of the three fixes a live defect — the bundle loads no cross-origin subresource, which is also why `require-corp` cannot break a load — and Trusted Types and a nonce-based CSP were each declined with the reason recorded in ADR-035 rather than left to be rediscovered. The site address gained the loopback IP literals (`localhost:8080, 127.0.0.1:8080, [::1]:8080`), because a request whose `Host` matched no site address previously got an empty `200` with `Server: Caddy` and none of the security headers; `https://127.0.0.1/` now returns the full set, verified with curl against the running stack rather than by reading the config. An arbitrary unmatched `Host` is still not covered, and that residual is recorded rather than hidden. `ui/vite.config.ts` sets `build.sourcemap: false`, so the production bundle no longer copies the original TypeScript into Caddy's web root (`find /srv -name '*.map'` in the built image now finds nothing), and `ui/Caddyfile` refuses `*.map` with a `404` at the edge as a second layer — without it the single-page-application fallback would answer a source-map request with `index.html` and a `200`, hiding the regression instead of reporting it. The `sessionStorage` player token was re-examined and upheld, with the cross-site-scripting exposure it accepts now stated in those terms in ADR-015. `.opencode/agents/ui-builder.md` gained the security section it never had: token handling, never `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`, what the Content-Security-Policy forbids, why a form must use `onSubmit` with `preventDefault()` and never `action=`, and that the answer to a CSP refusal is never to relax the directive. ADR-017 and ADR-035 were amended to match. (EOP-107)
- **The Vite dev server port reverts from `:5371` to Vite's default `:5173`, and ADR-009's explanation of why it was `:5371` was wrong on both halves (EOP-106, ADR-009 amended a sixth time).** EOP-40 found the dev server listening on `:5371`, corrected four documentation sites to say so, and added a `### Why the front-end port is :5371` section guessing that the value was "very likely a typo in the original commit". `git log -- ui/vite.config.ts` disproves both halves of that guess: `7efd900` (`[EOP-9]`) created the file with `port: 5173`, Vite's default, and `:5371` arrived four weeks later in `496d7b5`, a dedicated `+1/-1` commit whose subject line names the new value — a deliberate edit, not a mistyped original. What it was not is a *reasoned* edit. The one question the code could not answer was put to the commit's author, who said he got the number wrong and had no reason for the change, which is why the disposition is a revert rather than a rationale: a non-default port nobody chose on purpose is pure carrying cost, with four documents each explaining a deviation whose only explanation is a mistake, and every reader told that the port is not the one their tooling defaults to. Reverting deletes the exception instead of documenting it. This also satisfies the objection @architecture-guardian raised while reviewing EOP-40, though the choice between reverting and recording was not one it expressed a preference on — its objection was to recording `:5371` as a *deliberate decision*, because an ADR asserting intent behind a probable typo manufactures false-but-authoritative prose that later becomes load-bearing, which is exactly what the superseded section did; recording an author-confirmed mistake is the honest alternative, and it leaves ADR-009 with no "why the port is unusual" section to maintain. One commit changes `ui/vite.config.ts` (`port: 5173`, with a comment naming that line as the port's single source of truth and warning that the documentation cites it), the `AGENTS.md` Front-end bullet's Build clause, three live sites in `docs/devops/local-development.md`, and four sites in ADR-009 — the EOP-40 Development-workflow amendment, the amendment-table port row, the shipped-layout tree comment and the `### Why the front-end port is :5371` section — each **superseded in place by a dated note rather than removed**, per the house convention. Four things deliberately did not change: ADR-009's original Development-workflow line, preserved historical text that happens to be accurate again on the port; `ui/Caddyfile`, `ui/Dockerfile` and `compose.app.yml`, none of which ever referenced the dev port, because Caddy serves the *built* bundle and the dev server never runs in a container (ADR-017), which is why a dev-port change cannot touch the deployed topology; this file's existing `:5371` mentions in the EOP-110 and EOP-40 entries, which are records of what those changes did and would be falsified by a rewrite; and `docs/adr/README.md`, since `AdrIndexConsistencyTest` needs every ISO date in an ADR's status line to appear in its index row and this amendment's 2026-08-20 is already there from EOP-110. `npm run dev` in `ui/` was actually run rather than reasoned about — the server bound `:5173` and `/health` and `/api/v1/cards` through it returned the Spring Boot responses from `:8080`, so the proxy still works on the reverted port. A loose end is filed as EOP-111: `496d7b5` is prefixed `[EOP-63]`, a ticket about adding card images, which violates `.opencode/rules/git-commits.md` and is the mechanical reason no rationale was ever captured — with no port ticket to record it against, the change's only trace was a value nobody could account for three days later.

- **Six advisories in the `ui/` dev toolchain — one critical in `vitest`, two high in `vite` and `nanoid` — are cleared by moving to vite 7.3.6 + vitest 3.2.7, and npm's own suggested majors were declined for scope (EOP-110, ADR-009 amended a fifth time).** The findings were raised by @security-auditor during EOP-105's gate round and deliberately not charged to that story, whose diff touched no dependency file. `npm audit` in `ui/` reported 6 vulnerabilities (1 critical `vitest <=3.2.5` UI-server arbitrary file read, high `vite <=6.4.2` path traversal plus `server.fs.deny` bypass, high `nanoid <3.3.18`, moderate `esbuild <=0.24.2`, `@vitest/mocker` and `vite-node`); `npm audit --omit=dev` reported **0 both before and after**, so none of it ever reached the shipped bundle. That is why this was scheduled rather than escalated — but not why it could be ignored: `.github/workflows/ci.yml` runs `npm run verify` on every push, so the vulnerable dev-server code executed in CI against branch content, and the real exposure was developer machines and the runner, not a deployed container. The change is four edits to `ui/package.json` (`vite ^5.4.9 → ^7.3.6`, `vitest ^2.1.3 → ^3.2.7`, `@vitejs/plugin-react ^4.3.2 → ^4.7.0`, `engines.node >=22 → >=22.12`), one import in `ui/vite.config.ts`, and a regenerated `ui/package-lock.json`; **no file under `ui/src/` was touched**, which is what makes the test-count and parity evidence below meaningful. Three of the six advisories are cleared transitively rather than by name: `nanoid` is reached only as `vite → postcss → nanoid`, and vite 7's `postcss ^8.5.6` resolves `nanoid@3.3.18` on a fresh install, so adding a direct `nanoid` dependency to pin it would have been a fiction — the fix is the lockfile resolution, not a version string. `esbuild` likewise moves 0.21.5 → 0.28.2 as vite 7's own dependency.
